#236. Waymo suspends freeway rides nationwide after California DMV order

Waymo robotaxis are no longer allowed to operate on California freeways after the state’s Department of Motor Vehicles restricted the Alphabet-owned company’s operations following several incidents that raised safety concerns.

California’s DMV, which regulates the testing and deployment of autonomous vehicles in the state, issued the operational restrictions on Waymo on November 11. However, the restrictions weren’t made public until Tuesday. San Francisco Business Times was the first to report the order.

The DMV confirmed the new restrictions to TechCrunch, stating that the department took action after three Waymo autonomous vehicles entered an active construction closure on U.S. Route 101 near Burlingame, and another separate incident involving California Highway Patrol vehicles on Highway 280 in San Francisco.

Waymo has complied and is preventing its robotaxis from accessing freeways in any of the markets it operates in, including those outside of California. Waymo currently has commercial operations in 15 U.S. cities.

The DMV said Waymo’s freeway operations pose an imminent hazard to public safety.

“The Department determined that Waymo’s driverless freeway operations pose an imminent hazard to public safety due to recurring incursions by its vehicles into active construction zones where construction workers, vehicles and equipment are present, despite a prior restriction and corrective actions implemented following similar incidents that occurred on May 18, 2026,” according to an emailed statement sent to TechCrunch.

The DMV said the restrictions will remain in place until Waymo can demonstrate that it has taken “effective corrective actions” to address the deficiencies.

Waymo spokesperson Dana Gallagher told TechCrunch the company has temporarily restricted freeway operations across its other operating markets to evaluate its performance.

“Safety is at the foundation of everything we do,” Gallagher said in a statement. “Following recent freeway construction zone encounters on U.S. 101 in California that did not involve any injuries or collisions, the California DMV has implemented an operating restriction on freeway operations in the state. In alignment with our safety-first principles, we have also temporarily restricted freeway operations across our other operating markets to evaluate our performance. Our ride-hailing service continues to operate safely on surface streets, serving over half a million trips each week. We are working with the California DMV and other state officials diligently and responsibly to resume freeway operations.”

Waymo began offering freeway rides in late 2025, a commercial milestone that has been central to its expansion in major metro areas. Freeway access allows Waymo to connect riders to airports and other destinations more efficiently, slashing travel times by avoiding slower surface streets.

That has been particularly important in the San Francisco Bay Area because Waymo’s service area stretches from the city south to Silicon Valley. Freeway access has dramatically shortened trips across the Peninsula that previously took 45 minutes or more than an hour.

In May, Waymo abruptly stopped operating on highways. The company didn’t cite a specific reason at the time, but several social media posts showed Waymo robotaxis driving through traffic cones and struggling to navigate construction zones.

Waymo robotaxis returned to freeways in July after the company said it updated the self-driving software system to improve performance around freeway construction zones.

#234. Google joins the tech outage trend by announcing it’s not having one

Was Google feeling a little left out of the recent wave of tech outages? The company took to X on Tuesday to announce that its Gmail service is actually running just fine. In a post, the company said it was not currently seeing evidence of a broad Gmail service disruption and reminded users they could always check Gmail’s real-time status on its online dashboard.

As it turns out, the company was trying to head off reports of a Gmail outage that began circulating on X on Tuesday around the same time that Apple was seeing a number of verified outages of its own. While some posts on the social network suggested Gmail was experiencing a disruption, others said they were not having any problems.

The concerns seemed to stem from an X post by Downdetector, a crowdsourced website where users can report service disruptions themselves and see if others are having problems. Downdetector is not an official source for verifying or understanding the true scope of internet outages, and as people become more concerned about possible cyberattacks, the site’s data may become less valuable over time if every minor glitch and hiccup is reported as an “outage” by non-technical users.

The fact that Google had to announce it was not having an outage speaks to the growing anxiety around the reliability of major tech services. Over the past week, Apple and Microsoft experienced outages, an FAA network failure led to a nationwide ground stop, and some banks and airlines also reported disruptions.

However, Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton said on Fox Business Network’s “The Claman Countdown” on Tuesday that the U.S. is not currently facing coordinated cyberattacks. He told the outlet, “We do not see the indicia of a coordinated cyberattack — let me just be clear about that — and we do not see the indicia of state-sponsored activity here.”

Instead, Clayton attributed the rise in issues to businesses patching their systems. The patching may be a downstream effect of AI, as newer models have become increasingly adept at uncovering previously unknown vulnerabilities. “They’re upgrading them, they’re putting patches in place,” he said, adding that this is happening at “a much larger scale” than before.

As a result, some of the systems are “not coming back as expected in all cases,” he added.

#232. HSBC's top execs face tense shareholders calling for a breakup

HSBC’s top brass defended their strategy Monday to frustrated shareholders in the lender’s largest market, as Europe’s biggest bank continued to face calls to be split up.

At an informal shareholder meeting in Hong Kong, Chairman Mark Tucker and CEO Noel Quinn took questions from investors on issues ranging from how the bank was approaching demands for an overhaul of its business to its purchase of Silicon Valley Bank’s UK arm.

In prepared remarks, Tucker and Quinn each reiterated the board’s recommendation that shareholders vote against a resolution on the docket for its annual general meeting in May that would force the bank to come up with a plan to spin off or reorganize its Asian business — the lender’s main source of profits.

HSBC's biggest shareholder reignites debate about breaking up the bank

Tucker said the board was unanimous in its opposition to the resolution, stating plainly: “It would not be in your interest to split the bank.”

He said the board had previously reviewed a range of options for restructuring the bank, and concluded that such alternatives would “materially destroy value for shareholders,” including dividends.

“Our strategy is working,” Tucker told the room of more than 1,000 shareholders. “Our current strategy is moving dividends up.”

HSBC has been facing calls to separate its Asian business from the rest of the bank over the past year.

Shareholders in Hong Kong — where HSBC is a mainstay of many retail investors’ portfolios — contend that the London-based lender’s performance has been dragged down by its businesses in other regions.

Quinn addressed those complaints head-on Monday, saying “our profits in Hong Kong and the UK are no longer being dragged down by underperformance elsewhere. The group is performing well as a whole.”

Pressed later by a shareholder on the issue, Quinn said a breakup of the bank would result in “significant revenue loss” because much of its business relied on cross-border transactions.

Investors have also been unhappy with HSBC scrapping its dividend in 2020, at the request of British regulators. They argue that if the lender cordoned off its activities in Asia, it would no longer have to expose Hong Kong shareholders to requests in other jurisdictions.

Christine Fong, a district council member in Hong Kong, said she represented about 500 small shareholders who had been affected by the dividend cancellation.

“Street hawkers, taxi drivers or teachers — they all relied on the dividend to pay for their regular expenses, like mortgage, insurance payments, school fees,” Fong told CNN.

“That’s why, three years ago, what HSBC did upset those small minority shareholders.”

Fong has now joined calls for shareholders to vote in favor of the proposal for the bank to spin off its Asian business, despite the lender bringing back its dividend in 2021, albeit at a lower level.

Ken Lui, an activist shareholder in Hong Kong who put the resolution together, doubled down on his call for support ahead of the meeting Monday.

The resolution will require 75% of votes to be passed in May, but “nothing is impossible,” he told reporters outside the meeting venue.

Lui, who said he personally held a stake worth 100 million Hong Kong dollars ($12.7 million), laid out plans for his team to focus on “targeted outreach to institutional shareholders to present our case and gain their support.”

His group will also canvass 18 districts of Hong Kong “to tell HSBC shareholders that they finally have a chance to speak for themselves and protect their rights through voting,” he added.

HSBC is also facing pressure from its largest shareholder.

Ping An (PNGAY), China’s biggest insurer, holds an 8% stake in HSBC and has backed calls for the bank to rethink its structure.

In a series of remarks made public by the Chinese firm last November, Huang Yong, chairman of Ping An’s asset management arm, said “we will support any initiatives including a spinoff that are conducive to improve HSBC’s performance and value.”

Since then, the insurance giant’s views haven’t changed, according to a person familiar with the matter.

The source told CNN that Ping An has been calling for HSBC to explore a reorganization, with an eye on boosting its valuation and simplifying its regulatory obligations around the globe.

The insurer has not recommended a specific path forward but will support any initiatives, including a spinoff of its Asian business, that could boost its stock performance or value, the person added. Ping An did not immediately respond to a request for comment on how it planned to vote at the upcoming general meeting.

HSBC’s leaders were also asked Monfday why the bank had scooped up the British unit of SVB following the stunning collapse of its parent in the United States. The purchase was made for £1 ($1.20) last month, just days after SVB had folded.

Critics have questioned HSBC’s ability to perform adequate due diligence on SVB UK’s customers because of how quickly the deal came together.

“Did HSBC look into the clients of SVB in detail? Say, the financial statement — whether they can pay back the loan?” said Fong.

HSBC buys SVB's UK business, ending 'nightmare' for British tech

Quinn and Tucker defended the acquisition, calling it a good business opportunity that allowed the bank to gain hundreds of innovative startups as customers. They pushed back on the notion that management hadn’t had time to carry out proper due diligence.

Tucker also weighed in on recent tumult in the banking industry, saying he did not expect an “immediate impact” on HSBC.

“After the collapse of a number of smaller regional banks and the takeover of Credit Suisse, the share prices of all banks have been suppressed,” he noted.

But he said he did not believe such developments represented “a systemic risk” to the sector. “I do expect a period of uncertainty” before nerves settle, he added.

#228. SEC reportedly investigating Nvidia amid Huang exit

The Securities and Exchange Commission is opening a civil probe into Nvidia over whether the company’s disclosures misled investors, according to The Wall Street Journal.

The investigation adds to Nvidia’s stack of drama after CEO Jensen Huang resigned following news of his extramarital affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy, Alice Huang, an executive at Singapore-based data company Megaspeed.

The Commerce Department has been investigating Megaspeed since late 2024 under suspicion that the company diverted some of its Nvidia chips to China, which would violate U.S. export laws.

Congress has also demanded that Huang answer questions about his affair and how it might have affected his stance that export restrictions should be loosened. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent put it more bluntly over the weekend, accusing Nvidia of knowingly selling powerful chips to China to “goose their revenues and their earnings.”

The news of the SEC probe also comes as Nvidia reports earnings to a market that has seen its stock fall more than 10% since Huang departed. The chipmaker reported revenue of $115.8 billion, up 103% from a year ago.

#225. Amazon and Google will together own nearly 30% of Anthropic after its IPO

Anthropic on Tuesday disclosed more details about the biggest investment agreements with major cloud providers Amazon and Google.

While we already knew that Amazon stood to make a killing on its Anthropic investments from the IPO, Anthropic revealed that Amazon’s total stake will come in at just shy of 15% (14.7% to be exact).

Even more interesting is that Amazon could have, if it wished, owned an even bigger share of the company. When Amazon invested billions in Anthropic in 2023 and 2024 (long before Claude Code took the enterprise world by storm, back in the days when Anthropic looked like it would forever be a speck in OpenAI’s shadow), the cloud giant obtained promissory notes that could convert into equity.

These notes carry conversion prices of about $2.09 or $5.05 per share, depending on the year of investment. Amazon has opted to convert a chunk of them as part of the IPO into a special class of non-voting shares that will give it about 15% ownership. (The shares can receive dividends and convert to standard voting Class A shares when sold, the filing says).

But it has also opted to hold on to billions of dollars’ worth of the notes for now. If it converted them all right now, it would own just under 24% of Anthropic (23.8% to be precise).

Google, meanwhile, will own just under 14% (13.7%), also in non-voting shares, which come from the conversion of notes, as well as the “private placement” sale of shares as part of this IPO. A group of major tech companies (AMD, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia, and TSMC Partners) have agreed to buy $26 billion worth of this non-voting stock. Google and Nvidia each agreed to buy $10 billion worth.

So while a lot of investors stand to make billions of dollars in Anthropic’s IPO, Amazon is one of the biggest winners, and that doesn’t even count that AWS stands to earn from Anthropic’s commitment to spend more than $100 billion on its cloud services over the next decade.

#224. China Renaissance suspends trading, delays results after founder's disappearance

China Renaissance, a top dealmaker in the country’s tech industry, said it would suspend trading of its shares and delay the release of its annual results because it still can’t get in touch with its founder.

Bao Fan, 52, started the boutique investment bank in 2005 and has been unreachable since the middle of February, according to the company. Shares in China Renaissance have plunged since Bao went missing, at one point dropping as much as 50%.

China Renaissance said in late February that it had learned Bao was “cooperating in an investigation” being carried out by certain authorities in the country. It gave no other details.

Chinese media have reported Bao might be assisting in an investigation related to a former executive at China Renaissance.

In a filing on Sunday, China Renaissance said auditors couldn’t complete their work or sign off on their report because of Bao’s absence. The board was also unable to give an estimate about when it would be able to approve its audited results for 2022 or dispatch its annual report by an April 30 deadline as required by Hong Kong’s listing rules.

Missing Chinese billionaire CEO assisting authorities in investigation, company says

Trading in the company’s shares was suspended from Monday as a result.

Bao is known as a veteran dealmaker who works closely with top technology companies in China. He helped broker the 2015 merger between two of the country’s leading food delivery services, Meituan and Dianping. Today, the combined company’s “super app” platform is ubiquitous in China.

His team has also invested in US-listed Chinese electric vehicle makers Nio (NIO) and Li Auto and helped Chinese internet giants Baidu (BIDU) and JD.com (JD) complete their secondary listings in Hong Kong.

Over the weekend, China’s top anti-graft watchdog launched an investigation into Liu Liange, former party secretary and chairman of Bank of China, according to a statement by the Central Commission for Discipline Inspection and the State Supervision Commission. The bank is state-owned and one of the country’s four biggest lenders.

Liu is suspected of “serious violations of discipline and law,” the statement said. He is among the most senior financial executives targeted in a broader financial crackdown by President Xi Jinping.

In January, Wang Bin, former party chief and chairman of China Life Insurance, was charged by national-level prosecutors with taking bribes and hiding overseas savings.

#221. Nvidia says it doesn’t plan to reappoint Jensen Huang as CEO

Despite Nvidia founder and former CEO Jensen Huang’s recent offer to return to the chip company he helped build from the ground up, the company is not looking to take him back.

During the company’s earnings call on Tuesday, Nvidia chairman Harvey C. Jones told listeners that Huang’s return was not something that company leadership was considering.

“On November 12, our board of directors announced that after disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company’s code of conduct, standards, and cultural norms,” Jones said, “Jensen Huang voluntarily resigned his positions as president, CEO, and board member. The Nvidia board unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO.”

On Tuesday, Huang had posted on X that since he left the company, he has “seen an outpouring of calls from Nvidia’s employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO,” and added that “if the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.”

Huang resigned last week in dramatic fashion following revelations of a number of extramarital affairs, including one with a woman who has been accused of being a Chinese intelligence agent. That woman, former Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, has maintained close ties to the former Nvidia CEO for years. Critics worry that the relationship may have influenced Nvidia’s dealings with China, and U.S. intelligence officials have expressed suspicions that she works for China’s Ministry of State Security.

The development has sent shockwaves throughout the tech industry at a moment when the future of the AI business, in which Nvidia plays a pivotal role, hangs in the balance. It has also spurred investigations, including an independent investigation into Huang’s activities by Nvidia’s board and an SEC probe into Nvidia. Huang has been replaced by interim CEO Colette Kress, who is also the chipmaker’s chief financial officer and executive vice president, while the board searches for a permanent replacement. Jones added Tuesday that the company was looking at both internal and external candidates.

#217. Nvidia says its open source AI strategy remains intact after Jensen Huang’s exit

Nvidia interim CEO Colette Kress reaffirmed the company’s commitment to open source AI during Tuesday’s earnings call with analysts — the first since CEO Jensen Huang’s resignation.

“At Nvidia, we are committed to fostering a vibrant open model ecosystem, so every country, company, and industry can build its own intelligence,” Kress said in her opening remarks. She also noted that open models were accelerating AI adoption, citing a 35-fold year-over-year surge in weekly token volume on OpenRouter, including a 3.5-fold increase over the past three months.

Kress also spoke about Nvidia’s plans for Hugging Face, a platform for open source AI models that the company agreed to acquire for $12.9 billion in September, in what would be the second-largest acquisition in Nvidia’s history.

The platform, which serves more than 118 million developers and 200,000 companies, is still on track to join Nvidia, despite Huang’s ouster. Huang had heavily advocated for open source AI technology. His departure had raised questions about the company’s future approach to open source AI models, with Hugging Face’s CEO calling Huang’s departure “a sad day,” and promising to “keep fighting for open-source AI, harder than ever.”

So far, Nvidia’s plans for Hugging Face appear unchanged. The acquisition was not directly addressed during the Q&A portion of the earnings call.

“Together, we will scale its platform and make intelligence more open, more capable, and more accessible,” said Kress of Nvidia’s plans for Hugging Face.

Last week, Nvidia founder Huang resigned from his position as CEO following news about his extramarital affairs, including one with Alice Huang, an executive at the Singapore-based company Megaspeed who is suspected of being a Chinese spy. He’s also accused of having extramarital affairs with two direct reports and is now set to testify before Congress by Thanksgiving. Meanwhile, the SEC is investigating Nvidia over whether the company’s disclosures misled investors, The WSJ reported.

Within Nvidia, Huang pushed for the development of the Nemotron family of open-weight AI models built for long-running agentic AI systems and reasoning workloads. On today’s call, Kress noted that Nemotron is now the “most widely used U.S. open model on OpenRouter,” and it’s “where developers start to build their own intelligence.”

“That foundation is empowering the ecosystem,” she said.

#214. Nvidia is abandoning China after Jensen Huang’s ouster

Nvidia will no longer sell its H200 chips in China, acting CEO Colette Kress said Tuesday, ending the company’s participation in a controversial loophole in the U.S. ban on the sale of advanced chips to the country.

China’s access to leading AI accelerators was an important topic during the company’s first earnings call since CEO and co-founder Jensen Huang stepped down last week. The disclosure comes the same day Nvidia announced it was creating a board-level committee to oversee national security policies, export control compliance, and global trade risks.

Nvidia’s deal to sell H200 chips in China was brokered by Huang and President Donald Trump. The arrangement required the chips to be shipped through the U.S., allowing the government to collect a 25% tariff.

The pathway angered China hawks who wanted to deny Chinese labs computing power and maintain the United States’ lead in AI, but Huang long argued that it was more important for the U.S. to compete in China. In April, he told podcaster Dwarkesh Patel that “to concede that market for the United States technology industry is a disservice to our country.”

Huang’s ouster — in part over an affair with the CEO of a company under investigation for smuggling chips into China — has coincided with new rules, issued Monday, barring advanced U.S. chips from foreign data centers that serve Chinese customers remotely.

Investment bank Jefferies forecast that those rules might trim between $44 billion and $62 billion from Nvidia’s data center revenue next year. Referencing the new export controls, Kress said that the backlog of demand for advanced chips like Vera Rubin means the rules would have no impact on the company’s top-line revenue.

In the long term, the company may face the loss of potential customers in Singapore, Malaysia, Thailand, and Indonesia. Critics of Nvidia’s dependence on China claim that as much as 20% of its revenue comes from demand linked to the country — annual revenue at its Singapore subsidiary hit $104 billion in 2025 before Nvidia stopped disclosing those figures — but that’s clearly going to change.

Even before Nvidia decided to stop shipping H200s, China had largely refused to accept their delivery. The government would prefer to generate demand for the country’s domestic chip industry, even if major AI compute users want the still-superior Nvidia equipment, according to Jacob Feldgoise, a researcher at Georgetown’s Center for Security and Emerging Technology.

Meanwhile, Chinese frontier labs are finding ways to access Nvidia chips elsewhere. All of China’s leading AI models show evidence of having been trained on Nvidia Grace Blackwell chips that are prohibited from being exported to China.

That training may have taken place at data centers outside of China, but sales to those facilities will be barred under the new rules. If large data centers suited for training AI models want to buy Nvidia chips, they will have to certify they aren’t serving any Chinese customers, export control attorney Dan Fisher-Owens told TechCrunch.

If they can’t provide that certification, the new rules may force them toward other chip options and further eat into Nvidia’s market share.

#211. Stripe backer Atomico calls for Europe to own its success stories

Europe is home to many of the world’s fastest growing companies of all time, veteran VC firm Atomico highlighted in its latest annual State of European Tech report.

The report, for instance, notes that the likes of Black Forest Labs and Microagi have reached $100 million in revenue in months, not years. “I think we can put to bed the idea that there’s a fundamental reason or constraint on the ability to scale a breakout company from here in Europe,” Atomico partner and head of intelligence Tom Wehmeier (pictured) told TechCrunch.

Yet, there’s still more that the VC firm founded by Skype’s Niklas Zennström would like to see — and its call this year is for Europe to “build it” and “scale it,” but also “own it.” That’s a way of wishing these companies will remain European longer. But there’s also a sense that this success hasn’t been acknowledged enough, perhaps obscured by louder calls for improvements.

Wehmeier, the report’s lead author, was quick to opine that “policy, procurement and capital are not moving at the same speed as our founders.” But he said the tech sector should advocate for itself in an inspiring way.

“When it comes to policy, I think the language needs to be, ‘Hey, this is not us complaining. This is us saying here is an opportunity to make the magnitude of impact even greater,’” Wehmeier said. “When we frame it as problems, that becomes harder.”

This reframing may also help European tech improve its image abroad, where complaints around overregulation may have overshadowed Europe’s potential to leverage its deep tech talent and growing pool of repeat entrepreneurs to have even more of success stories.

Plenty could be still done to fully unlock this potential, and Wehmeier said that “the next few weeks are going to be a really important test, with EU Inc.” Also known as the 28th regime, this initiative would create a unified European corporate regime akin to the Delaware C Corp.

If the last stretch of EU Inc’s grassroots campaign achieves it goal, it will become much easier to create and operate businesses across the EU. It would also show that the tech system got itself heard, which could open the door to the kind of endorsement Atomico is hoping to see more of.

Politicians have strong incentives to give the tech sector what it wants. If it is Europe’s second largest sector, biggest contributor to growth, and biggest job creator, “Do we not want more of that?” Wehmeier asked rhetorically. But it is unclear whether it will win them any votes.

To make the answer more straightforward, the tech sector will have to work on having public opinion on its side. For Wehmeier, one key is to “demonstrate that this is translating into value for citizens.”

The report is supporting this endeavor with a mix of high-level data and examples from its portfolio; for instance, it notes that ICEYE’s satellites monitor severe weather, flooding, and other hazards; and Daniel Ek’s Neko Health has now done more than 100,000 body scans, some of which helped identify severe or life-threatening health conditions.

One limit to this argument is that Neko Health’s scans will seem pricey to most Europeans, who largely rely on collective, public health insurance. But Atomico seems to hope that its report will give readers FOMO not just as potential customers, but also as potential shareholders.

There’s more to be done for this to happen. Beyond joining the sector’s push for an EU capital markets union, he hopes more of Europe’s institutional investors will invest in the region’s own tech companies. “We must get to the place where Europe and European beneficiaries are the biggest shareholders in the success that we create,” Wehmeier said.

Before that can happen, the industry must overcome anti-industry beliefs like fears of AI-induced job destruction. But Wehmeier is a believer in the power of proof. “I think that trust is going to be earned through showing results to the public — be that lower energy bills, better healthcare, more homes built for cheaper and faster, and having infrastructure that we can rely on.”

#209. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#208. Why did some Apollo missions carry a grenade launcher?

Some Apollo missions carried a grenade launcher and grenades as part of an active seismic experiment. The idea was to create controlled surface explosions near deployed seismometers so scientists could study how shockwaves traveled through the lunar subsurface and infer its structure.

#205. Nvidia promises that revenue will keep soaring — for now

Despite the turmoil at Nvidia following the departure of its disgraced founder and CEO Jensen Huang, the company — as expected — delivered a solid quarter on Tuesday. More importantly, reassured investors that its streak of record-breaking revenue won’t stop.

On Tuesday, CFO-turned-interim CEO Colette Kress, reported revenue of $115.8 billion, up 103% from the year-ago quarter and a beat above the company’s guidance. Nvidia had told Wall Street to expect $108 billion in revenue for its third quarter of fiscal 2027. Kress also projected an even bigger fourth quarter, with revenue expected to reach $130 billion, plus or minus 3%.

She also said full-year revenue is on track to exceed expectations.

“We are raising our fiscal year 2028 outlook. We expect revenue to grow approximately 75% year over year, up from 70% and off what it now a higher fiscal 2027 base,” Kress said on the quarterly conference call on Tuesday. “Our outlook considers data center readiness, the strength and durability of end customer demand, and our customers’ access to capital.”

The company also reminded investors that it was using some of its billions in cash to buy back shares. In September 2026, Nvidia increased its share buyback program by $150 billion. It spent $28.1 billion on its own shares during the quarter and still had another $227.3 billion remaining in the kitty for additional repurchases.

Ironically, Nvidia could also benefit from the plunge in its share price following Huang’s resignation last week, should shares remain depressed over the longer term. The stock is down about 10% since his departure. Investors, however, celebrated Tuesday’s quarterly results, sending shares up about 4% in after-hours trading.

Kress stressed that the company was in good shape now and for the foreseeable future, even without Huang at the helm.

“We have the best technologists on the planet with a world-class multi-year roadmap,” Kress stressed. “From business perspective, it’s business as usual. We’ve engaged with our customers, we’ve engaged with our suppliers, our partners. They’re all 100% up behind us.”

She added that the company has secured memory and advanced packaging capacity and is working with its chip manufacturer, TSMC, to “narrow the supply and demand gaps.”

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

Dogecoin was created December 6, 2013, by a pair of software engineers — as a joke. The name is a nod to the “doge” meme that became popular a decade ago. Its Shiba Inu mascot mimicks that meme: a dog surrounded by a bunch of Comic Sans text in broken English.

#199. California bans Waymo self-driving cars from freeways

State DMV says vehicles pose ‘imminent hazard’ after cars entered closed zone and tried to bypass highway patrol

California has banned Waymo’s autonomous vehicles from state freeways, saying they pose an “imminent hazard” after a spate of recent incidents.

The restrictions, issued last Wednesday, bar Waymo from “operating or testing its  driverless vehicles on all California freeways,” Jonathan Groveman, a spokesperson for the state’s department of motor vehicles, said in a statement.

The measure comes after three of Waymo’s autonomous vehicles “entered an active construction closure” south of San Francisco on 5 November and another incident the next day involving highway patrol vehicles on a highway in San Francisco, according to the department.

Video of the 6 November incident circulated widely on social media last week. In the footage, a Waymo vehicle can be seen trying to bypass California highway patrol vehicles conducting a traffic break.

“The department determined that Waymo’s driverless freeway operations pose an imminent hazard to public safety due to recurring incursions by its vehicles into active construction zones where construction workers, vehicles and equipment are present,” Groveman said, adding that “similar incidents” previously occurred on 18 May.

Waymo’s autonomous vehicles expanded on to freeways and interstates around San Francisco, Los Angeles and Phoenix last year. The Alphabet subsidiary operates the largest fleet of self-driving cars in the US.

A Waymo spokesperson said in a statement that the “recent freeway construction zone encounters on US 101 in California” did not “involve any injuries or collisions” and that the company prioritized safety.

“In alignment with our safety-first principles, we have also temporarily restricted freeway operations across our other operating markets to evaluate our performance,” the spokesperson said.

“We are working with the California DMV and other state officials diligently and responsibly to resume freeway operations.”

A weekly dive in to how technology is shaping our lives

Waymo continues to operate the vehicles as normal on California city streets, according to the company.

California’s restrictions will remain in effect until “Waymo demonstrates to the department that it has taken effective corrective actions to address the deficiencies that precipitated the restrictions”, Groveman said.

#196. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

#195. First responders describe rescue efforts after New Orleans boat crash: ‘We have to save each other’

More than 100 people were pulled from the Mississippi River in mission being hailed as ‘Mississippi miracle’

Mariners and first responders who took part in the so-called “Mississippi miracle” rescue mission, which saw about 100 people pulled from the water after a tanker collided with a cruise ship, have said they are thankful “everything came out all right”.

The collision on Friday between the Atlantic Asphalt tanker and the American Heritage riverboat saw more than 30 people injured.

Speaking days after the incident, responders told how they heard screams coming from inside the rooms of the sinking vessel, before they were able to free those onboard and bring them to safety.

William Brunner, the captain-on-watch of the nearby Madison Stone tow boat across the cruise ship, told Fox8 he initially heard the tanker’s distress call on his radio.

“The only one that could get there fast enough was a tow boat,” Brunner said. When the ship began to capsize, Brunner said the Madison Stone pushed against it to keep it from moving while his five-person crew started to rescue the passengers.

“We had the only spot that [could] get close enough to get people off,” Brunner said. “We live out here on the water and we know that we have to save each other,” he added.

Captain Ryan Williams, who helped develop a rescue plan in the heat of the moment, told the outlet: “No 1 goal was to get everybody out.”

Williams told of how he and several other crew members began to hoist passengers off the cruise ship and on to the tow boat once they were within reach. “We heard screaming coming down from … a hole out of one of the rooms and the glass was closed. It was four individuals in there and we [were] able to get them out,” Williams said.

In total, the tug boat crew managed to rescue 27 people off the cruise ship.

Among the first responders who were also quick to the scene were more than 60 New Orleans firefighters. Varrick Dyer, captain of the New Orleans fire department’s hazardous materials unit, told WWLTV: “We train constantly. As a matter of fact, before we went on that water rescue, we had guys on the roof training doing rope rescue at that, like an hour before that incident happened.”

Dyer, whose crew is trained in rope skills, as well as rescues in water, elevators, high-rises and automobiles, said: “We [were] looking for the people who were injured the most, who couldn’t move, couldn’t swim or couldn’t help themselves.”

Many of the passengers were seniors using wheelchairs or crutches, who were unable to use the firefighters’ rescue ladders. Fortunately, they managed to disembark via a gangway connecting the cruise ship to the dock before the ship then capsized and began to sink.

The fire department’s captain, Grant Rodriguez, told the outlet: “We did not have a lot of time. It went quick. The gangway went. The boat then retracted very hard to the rear. It then pushed away from the wharf all at the same moment … As it rolled, there was a lot of panic. It was cold, people were shivering. The river was extremely cold.”

Melanie Montroll, chief of the the New Orleans Harbor police, who were also on the scene, detailed the moment she ordered two officers on to the vessel as it began to capsize.

“Looking two officers in the face and sending them into a no-win situation, knowing what the potential outcome would be, it’s tough,” Montroll told Nola.com, adding: “I know my officers. I know their families. I know their kids.”

After the rescues, Montroll waded into the river to pull out the two injured officers, one of whom could not stand. “My goal was, one, to make sure that we got as many people, but two that I didn’t leave my officers in the water. That was a big concern of mine. I made that decision to put them on there, but we don’t leave our own,” Montroll told Nola.com.

Amid the rescue operations, the captain of the cruise ship, Leonard Stewart, remained on the bridge as the vessel capsized, repeatedly calling for help and keeping the boat by the dock so everyone else could disembark safely.

Leonard has since received widespread praise from the local community, with Helena Moreno, the New Orleans mayor, saying last week: “We really also appreciate the captain of the American Heritage who was on site with us. Every moment of the critical time yesterday he did not want to leave his ship.

“In fact, he was the last to get off of the ship yesterday, and so we just wanted to express our gratitude to him for his bravery.”

#190. Nvidia doubles revenue as board says it does not intend to reinstate Jensen Huang

Company issues first earnings report after ouster of CEO over allegations of affair with suspected Chinese spy

Nvidia “does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO”, the chairman of the company’s board said Tuesday. Harvey Jones made the remark during Nvidia’s first quarterly earnings report since Jensen Huang’s ouster.

Nvidia announced the same day that its quarterly revenue beat expectations, coming in at $115.84bn, doubling from the same time period a year ago. The third-quarter earnings report comes just days after the world’s most valuable chip company saw the ouster of Huang over allegations of several extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected Chinese spy and two with his own employees.

Following the tumult, Nvidia’s stock tumbled more than 10%, shedding hundreds of billions in value and dropping its valuation to $3.7tn. In October, the company had hit an all-time-high valuation of nearly $6tn, but its share price has steadily fallen since then amid a broader AI slowdown.

In after-hours trading on Tuesday, Nvidia’s stock ticked up 5% from earlier in the day.

Colette Kress, Nvidia’s chief financial officer, was named interim CEO last week and delivered the financial results on Tuesday.

Nvidia reported a revenue of $115.84bn and earnings of $2.68 per share, beating Wall Street’s expectations of $109.15bn in revenue and $2.47 earnings per share, according to Bloomberg analyst consensus. Nvidia’s revenue is now up 103% from the same time last year and up 20% over last quarter.

“We delivered another outstanding quarter,” Kress said during an earnings call with investors. “Our ambition will not change.”

When investors asked about negative effects on Nvidia’s talent retention in response to Huang’s ouster, Kress said she believed the company’s staff was “committed” and that “we will take care of our employees during this transition”.

When it comes to running the business, she said “it’s business as usual”.

Nvidia designs powerful computing chips used for artificial intelligence. The company controls about 90% of the chips market and the world’s top AI companies, including OpenAI, Anthropic and Google, rely on Nvidia for their technology. With much of Wall Street’s billions invested in Nvidia, the company’s twists and turns can have an outsized effect on the US economy.

Huang co-founded Nvidia in 1993 and grew it from a niche firm geared toward video game chips to a trillion-dollar juggernaut at the center of the AI boom. As the CEO, he had about 60 direct reports, giving him control over most of the company’s daily functions.

Last week, following reports that Huang had a romantic relationship with an alleged Chinese asset, the CEO confessed to an extramarital affair. Days later, reports uncovered two more alleged affairs with company employees. On Thursday, Nvidia’s board of directors announced Huang’s resignation saying it was “effective immediately”.

Donald Trump and Silicon Valley insiders, including Elon Musk, the disgraced Uber co-founder Travis Kalanick and prominent venture capitalists, have called for Huang’s reinstatement as the CEO of Nvidia. On Monday, before departing on a trip to Asia, Trump told reporters he would not mind Huang’s return: “I would not mind that … I think he learned a big lesson.”

A weekly dive in to how technology is shaping our lives

Huang issued his first public statement on Tuesday morning apologizing for his mistakes and thanking his friends and colleagues for their support. He also said he would be willing to return as CEO.

“If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO,” he wrote.

Elaborating on the position that Huang would not return, Jones said that the former CEO had voluntarily stepped down and the board accepted his resignation. Jones nodded to Kress’s 13-year tenure at Nvidia, saying she has been “at the center of this company’s extraordinary growth”.

“While this was an unexpected transition,” Jones said. “The board is proactively dealing with the consequences.”

Huang agreed on Tuesday to appear before the House foreign affairs committee, the select committee on the Chinese Communist party and the House intelligence committee for transcribed interviews in early December. The company is also facing an investigation by the US Securities and Exchange Commission over its business in China in the wake of Huang’s resignation, the Wall Street Journal reported on Tuesday.

#188. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.

#186. Man accused of destroying US military plane at Shannon airport in Ireland

Daithi O’Corrain, 48, is charged with destruction of US government property, after allegedly cutting hydraulic lines

An axe-wielding man breached a security perimeter and destroyed an American military plane while it was parked at an Irish airport, US prosecutors said.

Daithi O’Corrain, 48, is accused of cutting hydraulic lines, smashing cockpit gauges and pouring ball bearings into fuel tanks.

O’Corrain is charged with destruction of US government property in connection with the incident in April at Shannon airport in the west of Ireland, according to an indictment unsealed on Tuesday in Manhattan.

He is not in US custody and his arraignment on the charge has not been scheduled, the AP news agency reported. If convicted, he faces up to 10 years in prison.

The $60m (£45m) US Air National Guard C-130 Hercules, which had been en route to a military assignment in another country, was rendered inoperable and will not fly again, US prosecutors said.

O’Corrain, described by supporters as an independent peace activist, was arrested immediately after the attack by Ireland’s police force, An Garda Síochána. He was charged with causing criminal damage and trespassing.

Manhattan US attorney Jamie McDonald said the indictment sends “a clear and powerful message that attacks on US military assets – and thus our national security – will not be tolerated.”

An online fundraiser organised by people describing themselves as O’Corrain’s friends said he disarmed the C-130 in protest against the US’s war with Iran and support for Israel’s war in Gaza.

O’Corrain plotted the attack on 11 April for months, US prosecutors said.

He searched online for information about the construction of the C-130, a quad-engine turboprop mainly used for transporting equipment and personnel, and looked up coordinates for sneaking on to the airport’s grounds, prosecutors allege.

He is also accused of trying to contact someone who had been involved in a prior breach at Shannon airport, prosecutors said.

O’Corrain breached the airport’s perimeter at about 4.40 am and broke into the C-130 as it was parked on a taxiway by destroying a locking mechanism on the rear crew door, his indictment said.

The document added he used the crew’s hammocks to cover the plane’s windows but left the cockpit windshield exposed.

He then barricaded himself inside the aircraft, securing the doors with straps, brackets and screws, and put ball bearings mixed with epoxy in door tracks to prevent doors from being opened or closed, the indictment said.

James Barnacle, the head of the FBI’s New York office, said: “This was not an act of spontaneous vandalism.”

#179. Trump brushes off NSA internal network outage: ‘it’s like that when you’re big and powerful’ – live

President’s words could be seen to run counter to NSA’s claim of ‘scheduled routine maintenance’ causing temporary issue

Donald Trump brushed off reports that the National Security Agency’s internal network is down in remarks to reporters in Manila today, saying “it’s fine.”

“It’s like when you’re big and powerful, and you have all the money. Things like that happen. It’s fine,” Trump said.

As we reported earlier, an NSA spokesperson told the Guardian the outage was the result of “scheduled routine maintenance.”

But after a series of high profile outages over the past week – affecting banks, air traffic control, United Airlines and Microsoft – some have begun to raise concerns.

“If this is all a coincidence, it’s like the greatest coincidence in the history of the world,” Republican senator Josh Hawley told reporters today.

A number of lawmakers have raised concerns about a spat of outages over the past week, offered their perspective on what may be happening and called for increased regulations on artificial intelligence and tech companies.

“Over the last week, we’ve heard A LOT about ‘system outages’ from government agencies such as the FAA and NSA, as well as major companies like Microsoft and JPMorgan Chase. We don’t know the full story yet, but the chaos makes one thing obvious: Letting a handful of tech giants control this industry is just too dangerous,” said congresswoman Pramila Jayapal, a Democrat from Washington state.

“At this point, there is no indication that these incidents are the result of a cyberattack, foreign interference, or some coordinated effort. What appears to be happening instead is a consequence of how quickly artificial intelligence is changing the cybersecurity landscape,” Mark Warner, a Democratic senator from Virginia, said in a social media post. “While we still need to get to the bottom of these incidents, the evidence so far points toward legitimate efforts to secure our systems, not malicious activity.”

“The American people can see everything happening with these service outages, and they feel like they’re not getting the whole story. You can’t blame anybody for that. I’m a member of Congress, and I feel that way!” said senator Rick Scott, a Republican from Florida. “We need congressional briefings. These companies and federal agencies need to be fully transparent about what’s going on.”

“We don’t yet know what has caused recent outages at major companies and government agencies. We do know that advanced AI threatens to undermine cybersecurity for systems that make our hospitals, banks, and government run. And that could make these sort of outages much more common and damaging,” said congressman Greg Casar of Texas, who called for increased regulations on artificial intelligence.

#175. Hundreds protest against Nathan Cofnas talk on DEI at Oriel College in Oxford

Students oppose appearance of self-described ‘race realist’ who made plagiarism claims against Jason Arday

Hundreds of protesters filled the cobbled streets around Oriel College, Oxford, to voice their opposition to an appearance by Nathan Cofnas, the self-described “race realist”.

The event organised by the university’s Reform UK student society – billed as “An evening with Nathan Cofnas: is DEI undermining higher education?” – went ahead on Tuesday evening despite opposition from students, college staff and local politicians concerned that his appearance would create a hostile environment.

Oriel, which allowed the Reform society to use a lecture theatre, declined to cancel the booking, saying it had a “statutory duty” to allow the blogger and academic to appear.

Earlier this year, Cofnas was the first to raise accusations of plagiarism against Prof Jason Arday.

Arday died weeks later, having resigned from his post as professor at the University of Cambridge, amid sustained media scrutiny which began with Cofnas’s claims.

The crowds outside sought to block the college’s main entrances, with hundreds chanting “Say his name – professor Jason Arday”, while several protesters held signs reading “Remember professor Jason Arday”.

One of the protesters, a first-year student studying English and German, who declined to give her name, said: “I think, given the situation at Cambridge with Jason Arday and given what happened, that we as an institution would be looking to not replicate that in any way or hinting that we could support that notion, [but] acting as an example in opposition.

“I think [Oxford and Cambridge], as two academic institutions, should be working together to banish this from ever happening again. And the fact that we can’t even act as the example, I think, makes me almost ashamed to be a part of this.”

Tommy, a second-year maths student at Oxford, said: “This is not the first of this kind of event. There was Tommy Robinson and the Oxford Union, and obviously there were protests for that as well. So I think it’s quite a nasty pattern that we’re seeing with these Oxford societies pandering to the far right, which is just really dangerous.”

Inside the lecture theatre, Cofnas was asked if he wanted to apologise to Arday’s family, but he declined. He said the implication behind the question was that “we shouldn’t criticise powerful figures”.

Cofnas added: “I would ask the people at Cambridge University who protected him for three years, who suppressed all criticism … I would ask them these questions,” earning a brief round of applause.

About 40 people made it into the venue, with less than a third of the space filled. Protests outside could be heard throughout Cofnas’s talk. At times some members of the audience appeared disengaged, looking at their phones.

Cofnas spent the majority of an hour talking about the history of what he called the “race equality thesis”, and his reasons for questioning it.

He has previously written that black people would “disappear from almost all high-profile positions outside of sports and entertainment” under a meritocratic system.

Anneliese Dodds, the MP for Oxford East, said she had contacted Oriel College after receiving complaints from constituents.

Dodds said: “Cofnas’ views are both repellent and contrary to the facts. Many of my student constituents have therefore contacted me to express their concerns about the student reform society choosing to provide him with publicity.

“I have written to Oriel College to ask what they are doing to act on students’ concerns about this meeting, including on safety grounds.”

While the protests were largely peaceful, a group of police appeared towards the end of the event to clear protesters from the entrances.

Hannah, a first-year law student, said that the size of the protest was proof that “even if Oxford won’t listen to us and won’t cancel the event, we’re still going to show up and we’re going to show that we won’t let that decision silence us. And we will still show that we don’t approve of this and we don’t want this type of behaviour here.”

Misan Harriman, the photographer and former chair of trustees of the Southbank Centre, said he attended the protest because he was concerned by the “damaging” rhetoric that Cofnas espoused.

“The university has failed all the students, not just the black students, by having them see this as something that could be remotely seen as part of the course of free speech,” Harriman said.

#172. A new approach to a Covid-19 nasal vaccine shows early promise

Scientists in Germany say they’ve been able to make a nasal vaccine that can shut down a Covid-19 infection in the nose and throat, where the virus gets its first foothold in the body.

In experiments in hamsters, two doses of the vaccine – which is made with a live but weakened form of the coronavirus that causes Covid-19 – blocked the virus from copying itself in the animals’ upper airways, achieving “sterilizing immunity” and preventing illness, a long-sought goal of the pandemic.

WHO experts revise Covid-19 vaccine advice, say healthy kids and teens low risk

Although this vaccine has several more hurdles to clear before it gets to a doctor’s office or drug store, other nasal vaccines are in use or are nearing the finish line in clinical trials.

China and India both rolled out vaccines given through the nasal tissues last fall, though it’s not clear how well they may be working. Studies on the effectiveness of these vaccines have yet to be published, leaving much of the world to wonder whether this approach to protection really works in people.

The US has reached something of a stalemate with Covid-19. Even with the darkest days of the pandemic behind us, hundreds of Americans are still dying daily as the infection continues to simmer in the background of our return to normal life.

As long as the virus continues to spread among people and animals, there’s always the potential for it mutate into a more contagious or more damaging version of itself. And while Covid infections have become manageable for most healthy people, they may still pose a danger to vulnerable groups such as the elderly and immunocompromised.

Researchers hope next-generation Covid-19 vaccines, which aim to shut down the virus before it ever gets a chance to make us sick and ultimately prevent the spread of infection, could make our newest resident respiratory infection less of a threat.

China approves its first mRNA Covid-19 vaccine

One way scientists are trying to do that is by boosting mucosal immunity, beefing up immune defenses in the tissues that line the upper airways, right where the virus would land and begin to infect our cells.

It’s a bit like stationing firefighters underneath the smoke alarm in your house, says study author Emanuel Wyler, a scientist at the Max Delbruck Center for Molecular Medicine in the Helmholtz Association in Berlin.

The immunity that’s created by shots works throughout the body, but it resides primarily in the blood. That means it may take longer to mount a response.

“If they are already on site, they can immediately eliminate the fire, but if they’re like 2 miles away, they first need to drive there, and by that time, one-third of the house is already in full flames,” Wyler said.

Mucosal vaccines are also better at priming a different kind of first responder than injections do. They do a better job of summoning IgA antibodies, which have four arms to grab onto invaders instead of the two arms that the y-shaped IgG antibodies have. Some scientists think IgA antibodies may be less picky about their targets than IgG antibodies, which makes them better equipped to deal with new variants.

The new nasal vaccine takes a new approach to a very old idea: weakening a virus so it’s no longer a threat and then giving it to people so their immune systems can learn to recognize and fight it off. The first vaccines using this approach date to the 1870s, against anthrax and rabies. Back then, scientists weakened the agents they were using with heat and chemicals.

The researchers manipulated the genetic material in the virus to make it harder for cells to translate. This technique, called codon pair deoptimization, hobbles the virus so it can be shown to the immune system without making the body sick.

“You could imagine reading a text … and every letter is a different font, or every letter is a different size, then the text is much harder to read. And this is basically what we do in codon pair deoptimization,” Wyler said.

In the hamster studies, which were published Monday in the journal Nature Microbiology, two doses of the live but weakened nasal vaccine created a much stronger immune response than either two doses of an mRNA-based vaccine or one that uses an adenovirus to ferry the vaccine instructions into cells.

The researchers think the live weakened vaccine probably worked better because it closely mimics the process of a natural infection.

The nasal vaccine also previews the entire coronavirus for the body, not just its spike proteins like current Covid-19 vaccines do, so the hamsters were able to make immune weapons against a wider range of targets.

As promising as all this sounds, vaccine experts say caution is warranted. This vaccine still has to pass more tests before it’s ready for use, but they say the results look encouraging.

Health officials consider another round of bivalent boosters for the most vulnerable Americans

“They did a very nice job. This is obviously a competent and thoughtful team that did this work, and impressive in the scope of what they did. Now it just needs to be repeated,” perhaps in primates and certainly in humans before it can be widely used, said Dr. Greg Poland, who designs vaccines at the Mayo Clinic. He was not involved in the new research.

The study began in 2021, before the Omicron variant was around, so the vaccine tested in these experiments was made with the original strain of the coronavirus. In the experiments, when they infected animals with Omicron, the live but weakened nasal vaccine still performed better than the others, but its ability to neutralize the virus was diminished. Researchers think it will need an update.

It also needs to be tested in humans, and Wyler says they’re working on that. The scientists have partnered with a Swiss company called RocketVax to start phase I clinical trials.

Other vaccines are further along, but the progress has been “slow and halting,” Poland said. Groups working on these vaccines are struggling to raise the steep costs of getting a new vaccine to market, and they’re doing it in a setting where people tend to think the vaccine race has been won and done.

In reality, Poland said, we’re far from that. All it would take is another Omicron-level shift in the evolution of the virus, and we could be back at square one, with no effective tools against the coronavirus.

“That’s foolish. We should be developing a pan-coronavirus vaccine that does induce mucosal immunity and that is long-lived,” he said.

At least four nasal vaccines for Covid-19 have reached late-stage testing in people, according to the World Health Organization’s vaccine tracker.

The nasal vaccines in use in China and India rely on harmless adenoviruses to ferry their instructions into cells, although effectiveness data for these has not been published.

Two other nasal vaccines are finishing human studies.

Covid vaccine maker Novavax says it may not survive

One, a recombinant vaccine that can be produced cheaply in chicken eggs, the same way many flu vaccines are, is being put through its paces by researchers at Mount Sinai in New York City.

Another, like the German vaccine, uses a live but weakened version of the virus. It’s being developed by a company called Codagenix. Results of those studies, which were carried out in South America and Africa, may come later this year.

The German team says it’s eagerly watching for the Codagenix data.

“They will be very important in order to know where whether this kind of attempt is basically promising or not,” Wyler said.

They have reason to worry. Respiratory infections have proved to be tough targets for inhaled vaccines.

FluMist, a live but weakened form of the flu virus, works reasonably well in children but doesn’t help adults as much. The reason is thought to be that adults already have immune memory for the flu, and when the virus is injected into the nose, the vaccine mostly boosts what’s already there.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

Still, some of the most potent vaccines such as the vaccine against measles, mumps and rubella use live attenuated viruses, so it’s a promising approach.

Another consideration is that live vaccines can’t be taken by everyone. People with very compromised immunity are often cautioned against using live vaccines because even these very weakened viruses may be risky for them.

“Although it’s strongly attenuated, it’s still a real virus,” Wyler said, so it would have to be used carefully.
