#127. Trump’s plan for A.I. supremacy fuels resistance in the Philippines.

President Trump’s 48-hour trip started with a meeting with Southeast Asian leaders in Manila. He will later meet Xi Jinping of China and Vladimir Putin of Russia in Shenzhen, a Chinese economic hub.

President Trump landed in Manila on Tuesday to begin a 48-hour Asia trip in which he will meet with Xi Jinping of China and other world leaders, with an unpredictable agenda that could include the future of American tariffs, nuclear arms control, the war in Ukraine and U.S. restrictions on advanced A.I. chips.

Mr. Trump was scheduled to meet with President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines and attend a gathering of Southeast Asian leaders. After less than 24 hours in Manila, he will head to the APEC economic summit in Shenzhen, China, where he was expected to meet with Mr. Xi and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia.

The brief itinerary appears to reflect Mr. Trump’s preferences. He dislikes big summits where he often is not the sole subject of attention. And he enjoys meeting with leaders of powerful nations — especially Mr. Xi and Mr. Putin, two authoritarian leaders he often talks about in admiring terms.

Mr. Trump will be meeting Mr. Xi for the third time this year, and a three-way meeting between China, Russia and the United States would be the first of its kind.

On Tuesday, Mr. Trump caught the end of a dayslong meeting of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations, or ASEAN, after issuing tariffs on countries across the region, even allies. Those moves added to a sentiment in Asia that the United States was not a reliable partner but rather one that treasured its commercial interests over its security interests. The only event of substance on the president’s schedule on Tuesday — signing what he had called a “trade deal” with Thailand — did not happen because Mr. Trump said it was still being finalized.

Mr. Trump met with leaders from the so-called Quad — the United States, India, Japan and Australia — soon after his arrival. All three partners are smarting from high energy prices triggered by the Iran war, a conflict Mr. Trump never discussed with them before launching it in late February.

In Shenzhen — the heart of China’s robotics and artificial intelligence industries — Mr. Trump will pursue détente with Mr. Xi. He needs to extend a soon-expiring trade truce that limited tariffs on Chinese goods entering the United States in return for a Chinese commitment not to cut off rare earths and magnets.

Then comes the main event: the meeting with Mr. Xi and Mr. Putin. Referring to the trilateral meeting, Mr. Trump told reporters before leaving Washington: “We are going to be talking about denuclearization, a tough subject because it is very hard for any of us to say we’ll get rid of our nukes.” Chinese officials have made clear they are not interested in nuclear talks until their arsenal is roughly the size of the American one.

Mr. Trump also said, “We will talk about Iran a little,” insisting the situation was in “very good shape.” Analysts say it is unlikely that the meeting will make any progress on Iran, with Russia happy to see energy prices elevated since the war began, and China eager to see them rapidly decline.

Protests: Thousands of demonstrators assembled in downtown Manila on Tuesday in opposition to Mr. Trump’s visit and to a U.S.-led initiative called Pax Silica, which aims to unite partners around A.I. supply chains independent of China. Read more ›

North Korea: Mr. Putin could meet with Kim Jong-un if the North Korean leader also travels to China for the APEC summit, Russian state news agencies reported, citing the Kremlin’s spokesman. It was not clear whether Mr. Kim would attend the summit.

Ukraine: Mr. Trump also said he would talk about Ukraine, on which Mr. Putin’s aides are engaging in talks with the U.S. and Ukrainian officials, but also renewing demands that NATO pull back its troops to where they were before the alliance expanded toward Russia.

President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia could meet with Kim Jong-un if the North Korean leader travels to China for the APEC summit this week, the Kremlin said, reflecting the deepening of ties between their countries in recent years.

Asked about the possibility of a meeting, Dmitri S. Peskov, the Kremlin spokesman, said on Monday that it would “of course” happen if Mr. Kim went to Shenzhen, the Chinese economic hub and host city, according to Russian state news agencies. North Korea has not confirmed whether Mr. Kim plans to attend the summit.

Mr. Putin and Mr. Kim have publicly met three times since 2022, and North Korea has been a key supporter of Russia’s military actions in Ukraine.

As the war in Ukraine nears its fifth anniversary, with no end in sight, the Kremlin is expected to use Mr. Putin’s many meetings at the APEC summit to showcase how Russia is regarded as one of the major international powers alongside the United States and China.

A visit by Mr. Kim would be a rare foray outside North Korea for the leader, and it would further raise the prospect that he could meet with President Trump in China. On the first day of his 48-hour trip to Asia, Mr. Trump told reporters that Mr. Kim “would like” to come to the APEC summit. The president has repeatedly said he would like to meet with Mr. Kim again.

On Wednesday, Mr. Putin is scheduled to meet with Xi Jinping, China’s leader. On Thursday, he is set to take part in a trilateral summit with Mr. Xi and Mr. Trump, the first meeting of its kind. The Kremlin said on Monday that it was working to arrange a bilateral meeting between Mr. Putin and Mr. Trump.

Before his departure for China, Mr. Putin met on Monday in the Kremlin with Jo Yong-won, a secretary of North Korea’s ruling Workers’ Party. During the meeting, Mr. Putin said that North Korea’s help in driving Ukrainian forces from the Kursk region of Russia last year “marked a new chapter in the history of military cooperation between our countries.”

Moscow planned to unveil a monument in Kursk by the end of the year to honor the North Korean troops, Mr. Putin said.

During the final meeting of the day, with ASEAN leaders, President Trump told President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines:  “I don’t want to date myself, but I knew your parents very well. They were great. I believe in genetics. They did a good job. You have very good genetics, I can tell you that.”

It was not the first time Mr. Trump has praised the family of Mr. Marcos, who is the son of the dictator Ferdinand E. Marcos, who ruled the Philippines for 20 years, imposed martial law and was ousted in 1986.

The only event of substance on President Trump’s schedule today, the signing of a trade deal between the United States and Thailand, did not happen because it was still being finalized. Trump told reporters that “Thailand will eliminate all tariffs on U.S. imports” and that the United States would provide tariff relief, but the two sides signed only a memorandum about the agreement. The president said the deal would be done in the coming weeks.

During an address to Quad leaders, President Trump called Prime Minister Narendra Modi of India, which is a member of the group, “a great leader and a friend of mine, highly respected by everybody all over the world.” The president also praised Modi as he stood with other leaders and took a few questions, calling him a “very tough man.”

India and the United States are navigating a rocky patch in their relationship, largely tied to trade and India’s purchases of Russian oil. Although Modi and Trump signed an interim framework for a trade deal in February, talks have dragged on, frustrating both parties.

The Nvidia turmoil and its implications for the U.S.-China relationship is a backdrop to President Trump’s visit. On Tuesday, shares of technology companies in Asia tumbled after the Trump administration announced rules to restrict overseas access to advanced A.I. chips like those sold by Nvidia. In Hong Kong trading, Alibaba fell 5 percent, while Tencent’s stock dropped nearly 4 percent. The Chinese A.I. firms MiniMax Group fell 7 percent, and Z.AI Co. declined 5 percent.

In the United States, tech stocks stabilized from a sell-off the day before. Nvidia shares were up about 1 percent before the start of regular trading in New York. The company is set to report its latest financial results later on Tuesday.

President Trump is expected to meet with Prime Minister Anutin Charnvirakul of Thailand on Tuesday in Manila to sign what the White House has described as a “trade deal.”

But Thailand’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs said in a statement on Tuesday that Bangkok was expecting to agree only on a “joint statement marking progress in negotiations.”

The dissonance comes amid growing signs within ASEAN, the Association of Southeast Asian Nations, that the United States is not a reliable partner. The meeting between Mr. Trump and Mr. Anutin is taking place on the sidelines of an ASEAN summit.

For more than a year, Washington and Bangkok have been negotiating a new trade agreement that covers items like tariffs, food imports and labor rights.

Last year, the United States battered Thailand with a 36 percent tariff, one of the steepest rates levied by Mr. Trump after his return to office. The United States is one of Thailand’s largest export markets, including for goods like pet food.

Mr. Trump later reduced the tariffs to 19 percent, before the U.S. Supreme Court struck them down in February. The administration eventually found a way to still impose 12.5 percent tariffs on Thai goods.

The signing of a U.S.-Thai trade deal might still be far off, requiring Thailand’s parliament to pass a law, but the meeting delivers an easy publicity win for Mr. Anutin and Mr. Trump, who is known to revel even in perceived success.

Pavida Pananond, an economist at Thammasat Business School in Bangkok, said, “The level of trust Thailand has in the U.S. is not at the same level as before all this economic weaponization of tariffs.”

After handing over the chairmanship of ASEAN to Singapore, President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines held a news conference. “President Trump has just arrived,” he said. “He is waiting for us to go to him and to invite him to this really very informal kind of summit.”

Mr. Marcos ended his comments by apologizing for not taking more questions from journalists. “I cannot keep the president of the United States waiting,” he said. “Sorry.”

At his hotel, President Trump sat down for a meeting with Quad leaders and took a few questions. He said that he was “not worried” about bank outages and that the United States would be “looking into” reports of plans by the Iranians to drop a dirty bomb in Washington. He also said that Kim Jong-un, the North Korean leader, would “like to come” to the APEC summit in Shenzhen.

Several hundred activists massed on Tuesday in central Manila to march to the Philippine International Convention Center, the venue for the Association of Southeast Asian Nations summit.

By the time President Trump landed in the city, the ranks of the protesters had swelled to thousands.

Most of the demonstrators were university students who said that they were fed up with the government, which they said was more focused on preserving its relationship with the United States than helping impoverished Filipinos.

Cinna Sison, 18, who studies European languages at the University of the Philippines, said they were inspired by the recent mass protests in France.

“I love the French,” she said. “They know how to protest.”

Ms. Sison said she had worn colorful makeup to mark her first time attending a demonstration. “I am here to show my solidarity with fellow young activists. We are here to show our disgust to the fascist Trump and to Marcos for kowtowing to his every wish,” she said, referring to President Ferdinand Marcos Jr. of the Philippines.

Mr. Trump is expected to hold a bilateral meeting with Mr. Marcos on Wednesday before he heads to China for an Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit.

The Philippine president, who earlier this year described Washington as a “steadfast partner,” has said little publicly about meeting Mr. Trump. His administration has hinted that the two sides are working on a military aid agreement.

In Manila, Ms. Sison wore a red scarf on her head and a red shirt with the words “Silicone not Silica” — a reference to Pax Silica, a U.S.-led consortium of countries aiming to create artificial intelligence supply chains independent of China.

Ms. Sison added that Pax Silica was a symbolic return of the Americans to the Philippines, the site of former U.S. military bases, as Washington leaned on a regional ally to counter China.

“Neighborliness is important, but it is not important to be subservient to U.S. interests,” she said.

Nearby, Raja Bausing, a 22-year-old visual artist, was busy putting the finishing touches on a papier-mâché model of Mr. Trump holding Mr. Marcos.

“We want to show how Trump is a warmonger, while Marcos is a lap dog,” she said. “They are very disrespectful to Filipinos. Trump was welcomed in a grand way and yet we Filipinos are maltreated.”

President Trump landed in Manila on Tuesday, beginning a trip to Asia in which he will attend an ASEAN summit, greet members of the Quad, then travel to China for an APEC meeting.

Here’s what those acronyms mean, as well as some highlights from previous summits — including gold crowns and awkward handshakes.

The Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation forum is a group of 21 member economies, most of which are from that region. Its members also include Canada, Mexico, Australia, Russia and the United States. It promotes trade in Asia, but is not a free trade bloc. Its leaders meet annually, with the location rotating each year.

Meetings between world leaders on the sidelines of the summit can often overshadow the main proceedings. This year, in the Chinese tech hub of Shenzhen, Mr. Trump is expected to meet with President Xi Jinping of China and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia, as well as with Mr. Xi separately.

Last year, when Mr. Trump visited the South Korean city of Gyeongju during the week of the APEC meeting, President Lee Jae Myung of South Korea gave him a replica of a gold crown that researchers believe was worn by an ancient Korean ruler. Mr. Trump also met with Mr. Xi in the nearby city of Busan, where the two struck a truce in their countries’ trade war.

A notable incident happened in Sydney, Australia, in 2007, when a group of local television comedians brought an Osama bin Laden look-alike through security. The team came within yards of a hotel where President George W. Bush was staying.

The Association of Southeast Asian Nations is a grouping of 11 countries — Brunei, Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos, Malaysia, Myanmar, the Philippines, Singapore, Thailand, East Timor and Vietnam — which promotes political cooperation and stability across the region. Its leaders meet twice annually.

The bloc was formed in 1967 and represents nearly 700 million people. Its economy and its role in global markets have strengthened in recent years. The United States also sees ASEAN as a geostrategic bulwark against China.

In 2009, an ASEAN meeting in Thailand fell into acrimony when Secretary of State Hillary Clinton compared North Korea’s leaders to “small children” when discussing their nuclear program. The country’s leadership, in turn, described Ms. Clinton as a “schoolgirl” and a “pensioner.”

During the 2017 ASEAN summit in Manila, Mr. Trump grimaced while jostling with Southeast Asian leaders for an awkward cross-armed handshake that was captured in a group photograph.

The Quadrilateral Security Dialogue is an informal partnership between Australia, India, Japan and the United States, which was formed in part to counter China. It was proposed by Prime Minister Shinzo Abe of Japan in 2007.

Beijing opposed the initial discussion, lodging formal diplomatic protests. Australia withdrew in 2008 and the dialogue was put on hold for some time. But the grouping was revived in 2017 on the sidelines of an ASEAN conference.

President Biden was the first U.S. president to convene a meeting among the leaders of the nations as a foursome. In 2024, he hosted the Quad leaders at his home in Wilmington, Del. Mr. Trump has appeared to be less enthusiastic about the partnership. Last year, he did not attend the Quad summit in India.

Reporting from the village of Manabayucan in the Philippines and the capital, Manila

In May, Roseller Rodriguez, a mayor in the Philippines, was startled to learn from news reports that U.S. officials had arrived in his district to start an artificial intelligence hub.

He saw a video of senior American and Filipino officials standing on a makeshift stage, unveiling a new initiative called Pax Silica.

Mr. Rodriguez had not been invited. Nor had he ever heard about the hub. Was it a data center? Where would the water and electricity come from? What would happen to the farmers living there?

Months later, he says he has few answers.

“I’m not concerned with the geopolitics of Pax Silica,” Mr. Rodriguez said in his office in the municipality of Capas. “My problem is Pax Silica will be built in our community.”

The United States started Pax Silica to unite partners around A.I. supply chains independent of China. The initiative is part of President Trump’s effort to counter Beijing’s economic and technological influence.

Trump administration officials have cast it as a 21st-century successor to Pax Americana, the era of relative peace among major powers after World War II.

The significance of Pax Silica, which 26 signatories have joined, has become heightened after revelations that Nvidia’s former chief executive, Jensen Huang, was targeted by Chinese intelligence, exposing the growing geopolitical stakes of the global A.I. race.

In the Philippines, America’s oldest ally in Asia, the backlash has been fierce.

Dozens of Filipino performers greeted President Trump as he descended the stairs of Air Force One onto the tarmac of Ninoy Aquino Airport in Manila, waving flags and playing “Y.M.C.A.” by the Village People on drums and trumpets.

President Vladimir V. Putin could meet with Kim Jong-un if the North Korean leader also travels to China for the APEC summit this week, Russian state news agencies reported, citing Dmitri S. Peskov, the Kremlin’s spokesman. Before his departure for China, Putin met on Monday with a secretary of North Korea’s ruling Workers’ Party. It was not yet clear whether the North Korean leader would attend the summit.

President Trump has landed in Manila, lingering on the tarmac for a welcome ceremony before walking alone to Marine One. He has several events tonight, but he will not leave his hotel for them. A meeting with quad leaders, a U.S.-Thailand trade deal signing and a dinner will all be held at the resort where the president is staying. In other words, he showed up late to this summit, and the summit will be coming to him.

Thousands of protesters have assembled in downtown Manila, chanting in Tagalog, “Trump leave the Philippines.” They are opposed to his Pax Silica A.I. supply chain initiative.

President Trump’s stopover at the Association of Southeast Asian Nations summit is part of a state visit to the Philippines, where he will be received with a 21-gun salute.

The last time he met President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines, he was full of praise for the Filipino leader.

“Great family, great family legacy and highly respected in his country,” Mr. Trump said while hosting Mr. Marcos at the White House last year.

But that legacy was long considered an obstacle to Mr. Marcos’s political ambitions. He is the son of the dictator Ferdinand E. Marcos, who ruled the Philippines for 20 years, imposed martial law and was ousted in 1986 by a nonviolent uprising. The elder Marcos died in exile in Hawaii, without facing trial on U.S. criminal charges that he had plundered the Philippine Treasury. Years later, a court found his estate liable for torture, summary executions and disappearances.

The younger Marcos spent years rehabilitating his family’s image and won election in 2022. He is hosting Mr. Trump at his official residence, Malacañang Palace, which was also his childhood home.

Mr. Marcos and his wife, Liza Marcos, will receive Mr. Trump on Wednesday with a 21-gun salute. Mr. Trump is expected to sign the palace guest book, hold closed-door meetings, pose for a handshake, then attend a state luncheon held in his honor.

It is not clear whether Mr. Trump, who has a well-known affinity for autocratic leaders, will meet with Mr. Marcos’s mother, Imelda, 97, with whom he has socialized in New York. Ms. Marcos wielded immense power when her husband was president and later came to be known for her flamboyant lifestyle, shoe collection and unexplained wealth.

Mr. Marcos has said that when he called to congratulate Mr. Trump on his election victory in November 2024, Mr. Trump asked about Imelda Marcos.

“My mother is really his friend, he knows my mother very well,” Mr. Marcos said, relaying the conversation. “He was asking about her, ‘How’s Imelda?’ I told him she sends her congratulations.”

After the pomp and circumstance at Malacañang, which traces its roots to the Spanish colonial era in the Philippines, Mr. Trump is headed to Shenzhen, China. The highlight of that leg of his trip will be a trilateral meeting with two strongmen: President Xi Jinping of China and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia.

For the second year in a row, President Trump is attending the annual gathering of Southeast Asian leaders, a show of alliance to a region that the United States sees as a strategic and maritime counterweight to China.

But Mr. Trump reached the gathering, held this year in Manila, only on Tuesday, just as the summit was wrapping up, and he is not expected to make any significant policies or diplomacy moves.

Mixed signals like this — to say nothing of Mr. Trump’s punishing tariffs — are adding to a widespread belief within ASEAN, the Association of Southeast Asian Nations, that the United States is not a reliable partner. That leaves the bloc balancing between Washington and Beijing, with which many of its members have deep trade partnerships.

“They’ve learned that the United States is not reliable, so a lot of the hedging has been away from the United States,” said Ted Osius, a former U.S. ambassador to Vietnam.

The Philippines, America’s oldest security ally in Asia, provides a window into this balancing act.

After taking office three years ago, President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. reversed his predecessor’s tilt toward China and pivoted back toward the United States. That stance contributed to many tense encounters between Filipino and Chinese vessels in the South China Sea, where Manila and Beijing have competing territorial claims.

As U.S. patrols in the South China Sea have dropped, Mr. Marcos has appeared to be more conciliatory toward Beijing. In August, he said that there was a “distinct possibility” that the Philippines and China could partner on oil and gas exploration in those waters despite their territorial feud.

“Marcos Jr. already has begun a shift toward China, one that a single visit by Trump is unlikely to stop,” Joshua Kurlantzick, a senior fellow for Southeast Asia and South Asia at the Council on Foreign Relations, wrote last week.

On Sunday, China and ASEAN failed to agree on a code of conduct in the South China Sea — something the Philippines, which has some of the biggest stakes in the dispute, had vowed to secure while chairing the bloc this year.

Chester Cabalza, president of International Development and Security Cooperation, a Philippine think tank, said: “Strategically, Manila opens its doors for conciliation with Beijing to stabilize normal ties. But on the ground, the real battle has yet to subside.”

Domestically, Mr. Marcos has to walk a tightrope, too.

“For Filipino fishermen and coastal communities, the South China Sea is not simply about a geopolitical dispute. It concerns livelihoods, access to maritime resources and national dignity,” said Arnaud Leveau, president of the Asia Centre, a Paris-based think tank. Any diplomatic accommodation perceived as sacrificing Filipino interests, he said, would therefore be politically risky.

The wider region has been battered by the consequences of another Trump policy: the war in Iran. Southeast Asia depends heavily on oil and gas imports from the Middle East and was roiled by soaring prices of fuel. In the Philippines, crops rotted in fields because farmers could not afford to transport them to markets. In Thailand, public servants were asked to use stairs instead of elevators to reduce energy expenses.

ASEAN comprises 11 nations — Brunei, Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos, Malaysia, Myanmar, the Philippines, Singapore, Thailand, Vietnam and, since last year, East Timor — with vastly different degrees of freedom and wealth. The bloc represents almost 700 million people, or almost 10 percent of the global population.

Making decisions on a consensus-basis and pledging not to interfere in one other’s internal affairs, the bloc has often been criticized as slow and ineffective, with the situation in Myanmar often cited as an example. A coup in that country in 2021 unleashed a civil war that ranks among the world’s most violent conflicts.

Myanmar’s leaders remain barred from ASEAN summits, under an agreement that was extended on Monday. Still, almost half of the bloc’s member states have had bilateral engagements with Myanmar since the coup leader, Min Aung Hlaing, used stage-managed elections to take the post of president this year. Washington has also recently re-engaged in direct talks with Myanmar.

Mr. Trump’s attendance at the summit, which concludes with an ASEAN-U.S. meeting, is part of a state visit to the Philippines. He is expected to hold a bilateral meeting with Mr. Marcos on Wednesday before heading to China for an Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit. Mr. Marcos has said little publicly about meeting Mr. Trump; but earlier this year, he described Washington as a “steadfast partner.” His administration has hinted that the two sides are working on a military aid agreement.

In recent days, hundreds of demonstrators have gathered in Manila to protest Mr. Trump’s visit. They oppose his “Pax Silica” initiative, a move to build a supply chain for the artificial intelligence industry that bypasses China, saying it would exploit Philippine natural resources.

Renato Reyes, who led one of the protest groups on Sunday afternoon, said, “Marcos is so desperate to welcome Donald Trump that he will roll out the red carpet, even if Trump arrives late for the summit.”

Aie Balagtas See contributed reporting from Manila, and Damien Cave from Ho Chi Minh City, Vietnam.

President Trump sets off this week for meetings in Asia that will culminate in a summit with two strongmen he has often praised: President Xi Jinping of China and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia.

It will be the first time the three men, who command the world’s largest nuclear arsenals, will have sat down together. Mr. Trump plans to discuss the reduction of nuclear weapons in the world and the war against Iran, according to a White House spokeswoman. He is not expected to bring up Russia’s war with Ukraine.

Mr. Trump will travel first to Manila, then to southern China, and will meet with regional leaders confronting an array of uncertainties. Those include the course of the war between the United States and Iran, high energy prices and mounting concern over the risks of artificial intelligence.

For Mr. Trump, the trip will be a pivot to foreign policy and relationships after American voters delivered a swift rebuke to his party in the midterm elections. His trip also comes as his administration faces scrutiny over a scandal involving the former chief executive of Nvidia, the tech giant, which has raised questions about Chinese espionage and American tech exports.

The highlight of Mr. Trump’s trip is expected to be his meeting with Mr. Xi and Mr. Putin on Thursday in China.

The prospect of their summit has drawn comparisons to the 1945 conference in the Crimean city of Yalta, where the leaders of the United States, the Soviet Union and Britain met to decide the shape of postwar Europe.

The White House has said that Mr. Trump wants to discuss the threat of nuclear proliferation. U.S. officials have expressed concerns about China’s accelerating nuclear buildup, expected to reach 1,000 nuclear warheads by 2030.

The last nuclear arms control treaty between the United States and Russia expired in February. Trump administration officials have said that any new agreement on nuclear arms control must include China, but Beijing has shown no interest in the topic. China has an estimated 620 nuclear warheads, compared with the more than 5,000 that Russia and the United States have each, according to the Stockholm International Peace Research Institute.

U.S. administration officials said they did not expect a deal out of the meeting, which they characterized as a starting point for discussions.

The gathering has prompted concern in Ukraine that Mr. Putin might push for a deal, deciding Ukraine’s fate without Kyiv’s involvement. A U.S. administration official said Ukraine was not expected to be part of the discussion between the three leaders.

Mr. Trump will start his trip in the Philippines, the oldest American treaty ally in the Asia-Pacific region, as the United States faces questions about its support for its partners in a region where China’s military and economic might continues to grow.

In Manila, President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. plans to discuss with Mr. Trump the South China Sea, where the Philippines and China have competing claims. Philippine officials have said that they hope Mr. Trump’s visit might result in more American military assistance.

Mr. Trump is also expected to meet with leaders of ASEAN, a Southeast Asian grouping of 11 nations, as well as a gathering of the so-called Quad grouping of the United States, India, Australia and Japan.

Prime Minister Anthony Albanese of Australia said on Sunday that he expected “lots of engagement” with Mr. Trump during the meetings.

“Obviously, that’s our most important ally,” he said. “But we want to see something constructive come out of that, including we want to see a de-escalation in the conflicts that are around the world.”

Chinese analysts described the meeting of the Quad group, formed in part to counter China, as an effort by Mr. Trump to project power after his party’s defeat in the midterms. “Its symbolic value outweighs its practical value,” Xu Liping, a senior scholar at Guangdong University of Foreign Studies, told Shanghai Observer, a state-run outlet.

After Manila, Mr. Trump will travel to the Chinese tech hub of Shenzhen, where he will meet Mr. Xi on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation.

Jensen Huang, a key adviser who accompanied Mr. Trump on his last trip to China, will not be by his side. Mr. Huang helped shape the president’s views about technological competition with China and persuaded him to loosen restrictions on selling advanced chips to China.

Mr. Huang stepped down from his role at Nvidia after revelations that he had an affair with a Chinese businesswoman, Alice Huang, who U.S. intelligence officials believe was collecting intelligence on Mr. Huang for Beijing. China has rejected the allegations, calling them “smears.” Ms. Huang has not responded to requests for comment.

So far, the president has not raised the issue with Mr. Xi and is not expected to during their meeting on Thursday. But the saga is likely to cause unease in China about whether Washington will impose tighter controls on the export of technology to the country.

As a result, U.S.-based analysts speculated that Mr. Xi might try to use his personal relationship with Mr. Trump to forestall such restrictions.

Yun Sun, director of the China program at the Stimson Center in Washington, said Mr. Huang’s downfall would likely mean “there is going to be more anxiety on the Chinese side about their access to American chips.” She expects Mr. Xi to emphasize the importance of easing U.S. controls on chip exports to China during their meeting.

Cooperation between China and the United States on artificial intelligence may be more difficult after the Nvidia scandal.

Still, there may be progress on the creation of a notification system that would allow the countries to alert each other about national security issues related to artificial intelligence, which the two sides agreed to in September.

“There’s some shared concern on both sides that U.S. or Chinese models might be misused by bad actors and cause damage to the U.S., China, or other countries,” said Kyle Chan, a fellow at the Brookings Institution focusing on China’s technology development.

When Mr. Xi sits down with Mr. Trump in Shenzhen, he will be dealing with a president who will soon face a new constraint at home: a Democrat-controlled Congress.

Lawmakers may pressure Mr. Trump to tighten export controls against China or accelerate weapons sales to Taiwan, according to Ali Wyne, a senior research and advocacy adviser focusing on U.S.-China relations at the International Crisis Group. That means that “Xi will have to work much harder to keep Trump on side,” Mr. Wyne said.

Mr. Trump will be the first American president to visit China twice in one calendar year.

Analysts say the leaders’ summits, which have included state visits in Beijing and then in Washington, have been the only thing holding together a fragile détente between the two countries as they clash over A.I., export controls, Taiwan and other issues.

The two sides declared a trade truce last year when they met in Busan, South Korea. That deal, which was to expire this month, has been extended until January.

China’s restrictions on the export of crucial rare-earth metals and rare-earth magnets, which are needed to manufacture most modern technology, remain one of the most difficult unresolved issues.

Mr. Trump is likely to raise this with Mr. Xi, according to a senior U.S. administration official who said that China’s continued restrictions is one reason Washington only agreed to a two-month extension of the trade truce.

Ana Swanson contributed reporting from Washington, Aie Balagtas See from Manila, and Laura Chung from Sydney, Australia.

#126. Palestinian woman shot dead by Israeli settlers near Jericho, witnesses say

Bullet hit 72-year-old Fadha Wadha in head outside her home in al-Duyuk al-Tahta, in the West Bank, say residents

A 72-year-old Palestinian woman has been shot dead in an overnight attack by Israeli settlers in a village near Jericho where settlers have recently moved in after taking over several Palestinian homes.

Fadha Wadha was standing outside her home in al-Duyuk al-Tahta in the occupied West Bank when she was struck in the head by a bullet as Israeli settlers fired towards Palestinian houses, according to testimonies from residents. Several other Palestinians, including her son, were wounded by gunfire.

Abu Younis, a cousin of Wadha’s husband, told the Guardian that the violence had begun when a group of settlers attacked a young boy they accused of entering a courtyard seized from its Palestinian residents.

Dozens of Israeli settlers, some of them armed, moved into eight buildings in al-Duyuk al-Tahta two weeks ago with the backing of the settler organisation Nachala and a regional council representing illegal settlements in the central West Bank.

“A vehicle carrying settlers chased him,” Younis said. “There were about 15 settlers in several vehicles. They began firing indiscriminately, killing the woman.”

Witnesses said Palestinian residents began to throw stones to defend themselves, as more settlers arrived at the scene, shooting at civilians and injuring six men.

Wadha’s son, Arafat Fidhat, was wounded after he rushed to the scene after learning that his mother had been shot. His wife, Naama Fidhat, told Reuters he was in shock when he accidentally collided with a parked car belonging to one of the settlers.

Witnesses said the vehicle was empty at the time, but that settlers responded by opening fire, wounding Arafat in the torso.

Naama Fidhat said her husband was being taken to a hospital in Ramallah when Israeli soldiers stopped the ambulance and arrested him. She said Israeli soldiers also arrested their son, Wadha’s grandson, early the following morning.

“What did we do? We didn’t do anything,” she said. “This is our house, they were the ones who came with weapons … we were unarmed, we were sleeping.”

The Israeli military said a group of Palestinians in ​a car “accelerated their vehicle toward an Israeli vehicle and rammed it”. It said the Palestinians exited the vehicle and attacked the Israelis, who opened fire at the Palestinians. “There are Israeli and Palestinian ​casualties in the incident,” the military said.

The arrival of the Israeli settlers in al-Duyuk al-Tahta had raised fears among Palestinian residents who have faced repeated attacks in the area.

The Binyamin regional council, which covers 47 illegal settlements, claimed the properties in the village had been bought from Palestinians, but Jericho’s governor, Hussein Hamayel, disputed the claim, saying the land belonged to the state and could not legally be sold.

Human rights activists said settlers had assaulted and robbed two Israeli activists and shot a Palestinian man in the leg during the takeover earlier this month.

“Since the settlers moved into the area on 4 November our lives have been turned upside down,” Younis told the Guardian. “We used to live peacefully and safely on our farms, but since the settlers arrived, our lives have become a living hell. I own 53 dunams [5.3 hectares] of farmland with greenhouses, but we are no longer allowed to access them. We have suffered devastating financial losses, and now people are being killed as well.”

There has been a sharp ​rise in attacks by Israeli settlers on Palestinians in the West Bank, territory Israel captured in the 1967 six-day war. UN bodies and most ​countries deem the settlements illegal, which Israel disputes.

On Tuesday, Suleiman Abu Srour, a photographer for the Palestinian news agency Wafa, was beaten by Israeli soldiers while covering events in al-Duyuk al-Tahta. Wafa said he suffered fractures and bruises, was detained for six hours and had his phone confiscated before being released.

Since the 7 October 2023 Hamas-led attacks on Israel, there has been a surge of Israeli settlement expansion, ethnic cleansing and violence against Palestinians in the occupied West Bank. More than 1,100 Palestinians have been killed there by Israeli forces or settlers.

#124. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#122. Israeli parliament elects new speaker backed by Netanyahu’s opponents

Knesset’s appointment of Matan Kahana marks step towards reversing policies of outgoing government

The Israeli parliament elected a new speaker on Tuesday, a key step toward rolling back policies passed by the outgoing government led by the prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu.

The new speaker is Matan Kahana, an ally of Gadi Eisenkot, the party leader currently working to form the next government and replace Netanyahu.

Eisenkot’s bloc has pledged to undo some of Netanyahu’s most contentious policies following Kahana’s election.

He was approved as speaker with 66 votes in the 120-member chamber, backed by the Jewish-led opposition parties and by the Arab-led parties. The candidate backed by Netanyahu’s bloc, Ofir Katz, received 52 votes following a stormy debate that stretched over seven hours.

“The appointment of Matan Kahana as Knesset speaker is a worthy democratic move that reflects the absolute majority in favour of change,” Eisenkot said ahead of the vote.

The vote took place weeks after the parliamentary elections on 27 October in which Netanyahu and his allies lost their parliamentary majority. Eisenkot was subsequently tasked by Israel’s president with trying to form a new coalition government.

Eisenkot has up to five more weeks to secure enough alliances to form a coalition.

As negotiations continue on the sidelines, his bloc, along with unaffiliated Arab-led parties, can use their majority in the newly sworn-in Knesset to advance their legislative agenda.

They introduced several bills last week, including to set term limits on the role of prime minister, to form a government-led inquiry into the Hamas-led 7 October 2023 attacks and to reverse laws that limit the powers of the judiciary.

Kahana gives the opposition bloc a major legislative advantage, according to Assaf Shapira of the Israel Democracy Institute, a Jerusalem thinktank.

“It’s important because there is not much time, because the speaker of the Knesset can make the legislative process much, much faster or much slower,” Shapira said.

Kahana replaced Amir Ohana, a Netanyahu ally, as speaker.

Eisenkot and his allies are under pressure to swiftly advance their legislative priorities in the weeks ahead. Roy Folkman, a member of Eisenkot’s Yashar party, heads the temporary committee that decides much of the Knesset’s work until a government is formed.

If Eisenkot fails to form a government, the Israeli president could give Netanyahu an opportunity to do so. That would probably give Netanyahu’s Likud party control of the committee.

Even though Netanyahu’s opponents would still hold a majority, they would “feel that their momentum has gone”, Shapira said, and there would be a “real danger that some of the legislation will be delayed”.

Chamber procedures will pose a further challenge. A bill has to be introduced on the floor, considered in committee and voted on several times on the floor before it passes the Knesset. That winding process could make it hard to move bills across the finish line before time runs out.

Shapira said Eisenkot and his allies are likely to prioritise rolling back controversial laws that gave the government a larger role in appointing judges and weakened the power of Israel’s attorney general, an independent role that is seen by many as central to Israel’s system of government checks and balances.

“It’s very easy to abolish these laws because no new institution has to be created,” he said.

Establishing a body to investigate the 7 October attacks would be easier, Shapira added, and carry greater legitimacy once a government is formed.

Netanyahu’s opponents argue he has blocked an independent probe into any military or intelligence failures around the day, the deadliest in Israel’s history, and evaded accountability.

#121. Saudi Arabia: Rights groups raise alarm over rising number of executions

Amid the war in Iran, Saudi Arabia's human rights situation remains bleak, with at least 211 executions, corporal punishment and an intensifying crackdown on critics. Is the regional conflict driving domestic repression?

Saudi Arabia continues to execute people at an alarming rate. On Saturday, Jawad Abdullah Qureiris and Fadel Hassan al-Safwani were executed in Saudi Arabia's Eastern Province. Both men were convicted of terrorist offenses, according to Saudi authorities. In 2011, al-Safwani was a prominent organizer of the peaceful protests that called for civil rights and the release of detainees. Qureiris, who was a minor at the time, participated in one of these protests in the city of Qatif.

The UN Working Group on Arbitrary Detention called for Qureiris' release in November 2024, after finding that his detention was arbitrary. The opinion also covered four other men. Qureiris was the fourth of these five to be executed. The fifth, Yousef al-Manasif, remains at risk after Saudi Arabia's Supreme Court upheld his death sentence, according to human rights organizations.

The two executions brought the number of people executed in Saudi Arabia in 2026 to 211, according to an AFP tally reported on Monday. The news agency said that the tally includes 126 people convicted of drug trafficking and 17 people executed on terrorism-related charges. Of the 211 people, 100 were Saudi nationals, while the rest were foreign nationals, AFP said.

In July, Saudi authorities executed five Ethiopian migrants for nonviolent drug-related offenses, Human Rights Watch reported. In October, Saudi authorities executed two Bangladeshi nationals, according to rights organizations. Human Rights Watch separately reported the execution of another Saudi national, Hassan Zaki al-Faraj, on October 17, for alleged offenses committed when he was a minor.

"In 2025, a record high of at least 356 people were executed and executions have continued at a high rate in 2026," Ahmed Benchemsi, Human Rights Watch's MENA spokesperson, told DW. "Saudi Arabia has executed more than 2,000 people in the past decade," he confirmed.

According to the Death Penalty Information Center, Saudi Arabia ranks third in executions after China and Iran. However, comprehensive global data for 2026 are not yet available, and many countries treat execution numbers as state secrets. However, the ranking is consistent with earlier findings from Amnesty International, which ranked Saudi Arabia as the world's third-most prolific executioner between 2022 and 2025, behind China and Iran.

According to the NGO Together against the death penalty, or ECPM, the two main methods of execution in Saudi Arabia are stoning or beheading. However, in many cases, including those of Qureiris and al-Safwani, death sentences are issued as discretionary rulings, called ta'zir. Ta'zir means that neither the crime nor the punishment is defined in Islamic law, contrary to fixed punishments under the Saudi interpretation of Islamic law, or Sharia. As a result, little to no information about the execution or the fate of the bodies is provided.

Following the US-Israeli attacks on Iran in February, Saudi Arabia has been targeted by Iran and the Iran-aligned Houthi group in Yemen. In turn, Saudi Arabia's de facto leader, Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman, is faced with a profound dilemma as the attacks have killed and injured scores of people and threaten Riyadh's efforts to diversify its economy under the Vision 2030 reform plan. Several summits and international sports events had to be canceled or postponed in light of the ongoing danger.

"The Saudi government has shown itself to be unable to defend its population from repeated Houthi attacks on its airports, exposing the vulnerability of the country despite world-record spending levels on defense equipment," Sarah Leah Whitson, human rights lawyer and former executive director of the US-based human rights watchdog DAWN, told DW. "Amid the war in Iran, the Saudi government has resumed its relentless pace of executions and corporal punishment sentences," she said.

This week, watchdogs reported on a Kenyan woman in Saudi Arabia who was sentenced to 100 lashes for giving birth outside marriage.

"The recent executions and the sentencing of a migrant domestic worker to 100 lashes for having a child out of wedlock are clear indications that the Saudi government continues to rely on brute repression to rule the country," Whitson told DW.

Maryam Aldossari, acting director of the London-based human rights organization ALQST, echoes this view. "Saudi Arabian authorities have undoubtedly used the war in Iran and wider regional tensions to intensify their crackdown, most notably through their increased use of the death penalty and as a tool of political repression against the country's Shia minority," she told DW.

Saudi Arabia is predominantly Sunni. The country's oil-rich Eastern Province — from which the recently executed men Jawad Abdullah Qureiris and Fadel Hassan al-Safwani hailed — is home to most of Saudi Arabia's Shia minority. Iran, on the other hand, is predominantly Shia. Saudi authorities have long viewed some forms of Shia political activism with suspicion, partly because of concerns over Iranian influence and alleged links between domestic dissent and Tehran, accusations that rights groups say have been used to justify repression of the minority.

However, Neil Quilliam, associate fellow in the Middle East and North Africa Programme at the London-based think tank Chatham House, remains cautious about drawing a direct causal connection between the conflict involving Iran and developments in Saudi Arabia's human rights environment. "The kingdom has introduced social reforms over the past decade, including measures that have expanded opportunities for women, while retaining controls over political dissent and activism," he told DW.

"What periods of regional tension can do is heighten official concern about national security," he said, adding that this reduces tolerance for any public criticism or perceived threats to social cohesion. "Whether that amounts to a change in the human rights situation is harder to demonstrate, but there is certainly a sense among some observers that space for dissent has become even more constrained, not only in Saudi Arabia, but in other Gulf states too," he said.

DW reached out to Saudi Arabia's Human Rights Commission on Monday, November, 16, 2026 but has not received an answer at the time of publication.

#120. Pakistani man sentenced to 27 years in US prison over Trump murder plot

Asif Merchant, allegedly linked to Iran, found by court to have sought retribution over Qassem Suleimani death

A Pakistani man was sentenced to more than 27 years in prison on Tuesday for plotting to assassinate Donald Trump in retaliation for the US killing of Revolutionary Guards commander Qassem Suleimani, the Department of Justice said.

Asif Merchant, who had alleged ties to Iran, sought to hire a hitman to assassinate a politician or a government official in the United States, a Brooklyn court found.

Suleimani, the head of Iran’s foreign military operations, was killed in a US drone strike in Baghdad in January 2020. Iranian officials have repeatedly vowed to avenge his killing.

Merchant had testified that he was forced into the plot to protect his family in the Iranian capital Tehran from the Guards, adding that he thought he would get caught before anyone was killed, multiple media outlets reported.

He said he was never ordered to kill a specific person but noted his Iranian contact had mentioned three people in connection with the plot – Trump, the former US president Joe Biden and former UN ambassador Nikki Haley.

But the United States attorney general, Todd Blanche, insisted on Tuesday that the plot was against Donald Trump.

“An Iranian agent who came to the United States to coordinate an assassination attempt against President Trump has been justly sentenced for his criminal conduct,” said Blanche after Merchant was sentenced to 330 months’ imprisonment.

“The Department of Justice will not tolerate foreign terrorist plots against our leaders and will not stop until those responsible are held accountable.”

US officials previously said Merchant had “close ties to Iran” and described his alleged plot as “straight out of the Iranian regime’s playbook”.

Merchant was arrested on 12 July 2024 as he planned to leave the country.

#118. Deutsche Bank under fire after internal documents made public

A cache of internal records outlines the bank's dealings with Jeffrey Epstein, Donald Trump and Russian oligarchs. The German lender has sought to deflect criticism by claiming its bad behavior is a thing of the past.

Deutsche Bank is in damage control mode again after an international consortium of more than 200 investigative journalists published internal documents from the global lender on Tuesday under the name "Inside Deutsche Bank."

The documents, published in Der Spiegel, Financial Times, Le Monde and The New York Times, among others, cover Deutsche Bank's business dealings with convicted sex offender Jeffrey Epstein, now US President Donald Trump, and a host of Russian oligarchs.

Gleaned from nearly 15 million documents leaked to a journalist from India and another from South Africa, the e-mails and meeting minutes trace the bank's aggressive pursuit of business deals in Russia, and with high-profile Russian oligarchs, right up until Moscow launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022 — despite risk warnings from numerous managers.

Deutsche Bank CEO Christian Sewing was among the executives who took part in the bank's Russia strategy working group. The bank also still employs more than 160 people in Russia and generated 2025 pre-tax profits of €61 million ($67 million) there through a local subsidiary.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In all, the documents cover the past 20 years of the German lender's business dealings and raise questions about the efficacy of internal controls by showing that Deutsche Bank had repeatedly put financial interests before international sanctions compliance and anti-money laundering rules.

Other red flags raised in Tuesday's reports suggest that Deutsche Bank sought to set up tax-optimization schemes for wealthy clients, that criminal networks may have been able to move illicit cash through the institution, that it helped facilitate arms deals, that it helped companies in environmentally damaging industries and that it held funds for the far-right populist Alternative for Germany (AfD) party.

Beyond more recent negative headlines involving alleged anti-money laundering and compliance failures, Deutsche Bank has been trying to restore its public image ever since its involvement in the US subprime mortgage rate scandal helped trigger the 2008 global financial crisis — a fiasco that ultimately cost the bank more than €20 billion in penalties and settlements.

When Sewing later took over in 2018, he promised to turn the institution around. Although he has made Deutsche Bank profitable once again, he did so at the cost of massive job cuts, and some compliance failings have continued.

Sewing to the German business daily Handelsblatt that some mistakes had been made under his leadership.

"When you look at the events today, you naturally ask yourself whether you could have done some things differently," he told the paper. "I am not without mistakes," he said, but added that the course he set for the bank "has always been right and remains so."

The bank's shares fell by up to 6% in Tuesday trading.

Deutsche Bank issued a statement claiming that the reports were old news and that the institution had long ago turned over a new leaf.

In an e-mailed statement to the press, Deutsche Bank Supervisory Board Chairman Alexander Wynaendts wrote, "During my tenure I haven't seen a single decision from Christian Sewing that I was not comfortable with."

A Tuesday statement from the bank described the revelations as "legacy" matters and claimed they would have no lasting negative financial impact on the institution.

Nevertheless, German Finance Minister and Vice Chancellor Lars Klingbeil on Tuesday called on Deutsche Bank to "provide full transparency regarding how it has addressed past failures and internal control deficiencies and how it will effectively prevent these in the future."

Further, Klingbeil said authorities must investigate and "take necessary action" where appropriate.

#117. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#116. 350 years ago, Ole Rømer showed that light has a finite speed

Where Readers Share Great Links. Join Free

Ole Rømer used Io’s eclipses to show that light does not travel instantaneously. In 1676, he predicted a delayed appearance of Jupiter’s moon Io and was right, providing the first solid evidence that light takes time to travel.

#115. EU, ASEAN pursue deeper trade ties, but hurdles remain as businesses struggle with informal barriers

2027 could be a landmark year for EU–Southeast Asia trade, with several deals nearing completion. But the harder task will be to ensure that businesses actually use them.

The European Union and the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN) could be heading for a breakthrough year in 2027, when the two sides mark 50 years of diplomatic ties.

A long-awaited trade agreement with Indonesia is expected to take effect, while a deal with the Philippines is nearing completion and negotiations with Thailand and Malaysia are making progress. By the end of next year, the EU could have trade deals in place with six Southeast Asian states, up from just two today.

The anniversary could also revive the long-standing ambition of a broader trade agreement between the EU and ASEAN.

On November 17, the European Commission hosted a dialogue in Brussels to discuss trade relations with Southeast Asia, including progress on existing and pending free trade agreements (FTAs).

But attention is turning to what comes next. "Finalizing FTAs is only the beginning, not the end," Chris Humphrey, executive director of the EU-ASEAN Business Council, told DW.

FTAs can reduce tariffs and remove trade restrictions, but that is of little use if businesses don't know how to use them, he added.

"Unfortunately, in many cases, this is where there is a failure. Communication and training are key," Humphrey said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

For Brussels, the priority should now be to tackle the less conspicuous obstacles to trade, he stressed.

Cutting customs delays, simplifying regulations, and helping smaller European businesses enter unfamiliar Southeast Asian markets will determine whether an FTA succeeds.

"European companies with little or no experience in Southeast Asia would benefit from greater on-the-ground support, particularly practical, firsthand guidance on informal business practices and the unwritten local rules," Alfred Gerstl, an associate professor at Palacky University Olomouc in the Czech Republic, told DW.

One option is to expand the assistance offered by European chambers of commerce, trade delegations, and EU offices in Southeast Asia. This could range from helping firms find distributors to explaining local licensing requirements and how to qualify for preferential tariffs.

In Vietnam, for instance, sustained pressure from Brussels has helped improve market access for European pharmaceutical firms. In Indonesia, the EU has worked on trade facilitation while pressing Jakarta over import restrictions and halal certification requirements, said Camille Nessel, a researcher at the Universite Libre de Bruxelles.

Regular consultations with businesses on both sides could also help Brussels spot problems before they become trade disputes.

Trade policy has also acquired a geopolitical purpose in Brussels. "If trade policy is expected to reduce strategic dependencies, we are asking it to deliver on objectives that remain insufficiently defined, let alone demonstrating how to achieve them," Nessel told DW.

The EU wants to reduce its dependence on a handful of suppliers for the minerals needed for batteries and other clean technologies, while Southeast Asian producers, particularly Indonesia, want foreign investors to help develop domestic processing industries rather than merely buy raw materials.

The EU and Indonesia have already clashed over Jakarta's restrictions on nickel exports.

European investment in processing facilities, agreements on environmental standards, and greater regulatory certainty would give both sides concrete gains, said Nessel. "The real value of an FTA is not just opening a market but giving the EU the leverage to keep it open," she added.

Pasha Hsieh, Lee Kong Chian Professor of Law and Jean Monnet Chair at Singapore Management University, said Brussels should prioritize ratifying its investment protection agreements with Singapore and Vietnam.

Signed in 2018 and 2019, respectively, the agreements still require ratification by all EU member states before entering into force. "These delays underline the need to follow through on negotiated commitments," Hsieh said.

The issue is particularly relevant as the EU looks to develop investment protocols in other Southeast Asian states where investors have sometimes faced uncertainty over legal protections. Indonesia, for instance, has terminated numerous bilateral investment treaties, including agreements with the Netherlands and Spain.

A larger question is whether the EU should continue pursuing bilateral deals with Southeast Asian countries or revive its original ambition of a region-to-region agreement with ASEAN.

The EU and ASEAN launched negotiations for a region-wide FTA in 2007 but suspended them two years later amid disagreements over wide economic disparities among ASEAN members and political concerns. Technical discussions resumed in 2017, but little has come of them.

ASEAN already has regional agreements with China and several other economies. Beijing upgraded its free trade agreement with ASEAN in October 2025.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Region-to-region frameworks extend beyond trade. Humphrey, of the EU-ASEAN Business Council, called for negotiations to begin on a region-to-region Digital Trade Agreement and Investment Protection Agreement.

The EU-Singapore Digital Trade Agreement, which entered into force in February 2026, offers a possible model for wider cooperation, he noted, adding that common approaches to customs procedures, supply-chain standards, and green technologies could create greater alignment with ASEAN members that are not yet ready for full FTAs.

"European businesses have long complained of operating at a disadvantage due to the lack of a region-to-region deal. It is high time that both sides elevated their ambitions and embraced the idea of working towards such an agreement," Humphrey said.

The 2027 anniversary would be a convenient deadline for agreeing on what comes next, but Gerstl warned against mistaking another round of diplomatic ceremony for progress.

"There is a risk that attention will focus almost exclusively on the 2027 summit in Singapore and that the group photo will become the defining image of the event, only for momentum to fade in the years that follow," he added.

#114. ‘They were keeping it for a rainy day’: Ukraine’s Kerch bridge attack is proof Kyiv still has cards to play

Partial demolition of bridge linking Russia and occupied Crimea is obvious embarrassment to Putin days before meeting with Xi and Trump

Ukraine’s attack on the Kerch road bridge overnight is the culmination of weeks, and perhaps months, of military operations plus years of industrial development, during which Kyiv has developed its own cruise missiles and long-range drones.

The demolition is not quite total, because the rail bridge is still standing, one line in operation – but the attack is an obvious embarrassment to Russia two days before Vladimir Putin is due to meet Donald Trump and Xi Jinping in Shenzhen.

Such is the collapse of a span of the roadway, and the loss of its supporting arch above, it appears to be a level of damage that “cannot be repaired any time soon”, says Ben Hodges, a former commanding general of the US army in Europe.

The 19km (12-mile), $3.6bn (£2.7bn) road bridge connecting Russia to the east of Crimea was completed in 2018, a project closely associated with Putin and his efforts to secure the 2014 annexation of Crimea by opening up a supply route to the peninsula.

Russia’s leader was filmed driving an orange truck into Crimea, greeted by cheering workers once the construction was complete. “Putin initiated this project himself,” the Kremlin spokesperson, Dmitry Peskov, said at the time.

That was four years before Putin’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine, which has, if anything, made Russia’s position in Crimea more precarious, though the change in the balance of power is a while coming.

Russia was able to bomb anywhere in Ukraine in 2022, while Kyiv was denied long-range western missiles, particularly those powerful enough to strike such a politically sensitive and strategic target.

Instead, Ukraine tried to blow the bridge up with a truck bomb in 2022 and a sea drone attack a year later. Neither did much long-term damage. It was only as Ukraine’s own long-range drones and cruise missiles came to fruition near the beginning of 2026 that a knockout strike became possible.

Volodymyr Zelenskyy said on Tuesday that the culminating attacks on the bridge, the end of two days of strikes, involved Ukrainian-made Neptune and Flamingo FP-5 cruise missiles, Palianytsia rocket drones plus Ruta cruise missiles from the Dutch manufacturer Destinus, the only foreign arms company confirmed as involved.

It was, the Ukrainian president said, the “seventh stage” in an operation “carried out over several weeks”. Radars, jamming equipment and air defence systems were progressively neutralised, opening up corridors of attack.

Meanwhile, Russia had already started what is becoming a battle of the bridges. Moscow attacked Kyiv’s busy Northern and Southern bridges in early October, part of its scaled-up campaign against the capital and the country’s other large cities.

Russia is struggling to make progress on the frontline, and a record 50,000 troops were killed or seriously wounded in October, according to the Nato secretary general, Mark Rutte. Its efforts are focused on knocking out Ukraine’s electricity, heating and water supplies, though attacking bridges show its target list is expanding in grim desperation.

Yet the Kremlin has chosen this expansion at a time when Ukraine can strike back. The pro-war Russian military blogger Military Informant notes that Ukraine has a “parity in firepower”, having downed the Kerch road bridge in two days of air raids. “It cannot be ruled out that the railway section will be destroyed next,” the blogger adds.

If anything, further attacks on the bridge are almost certain both to prevent its repair and to make resupplying Crimea more difficult. Cutting the bridge entirely, rail and road, would leave Moscow with only the R-280 land highway from Rostov to Crimea, already the subject of months of Ukrainian drone attacks.

Hodges has long argued that Crimea could be a defining theatre of the war, because “of what Crimea represents to Putin”. Ukraine should try to make “Russian occupation untenable” by destroying the bridge and asserting fire control over the R-280, he adds.

A supply squeeze, however, is a slow process, though its political benefits may be felt more quickly. Bringing down the road bridge “further kills the narrative that Russian victory is inevitable or that Crimea is theirs”, Hodges says, although Trump may not bring up the episode in his meeting with Putin and Xi on Thursday.

The danger is that both sides, in their desperation to end a deadlocked war, will escalate further. However, cut out of the talks in Shenzen, Zelenskyy had little choice but to remind Trump as well as Putin that Ukraine still has cards to play.

Emily Ferris, a Russia expert with the Rusi thinktank, says Ukraine’s military-industrial complex was ready for some months, meaning the timing of the attack on the Kerch bridge can be interpreted only as political.

“They were keeping it for a rainy day, and that day has come,” she said.

#113. ‘Spared a lot’: Kessler twins explained assisted dying decision in final letter

Pop-singing sisters, who died aged 89 last year, described their declining health and desire to depart life together

Alice and Ellen Kessler, the identical pop-singing twins who chose to jointly end their lives through assisted dying last year, said they had “reached rock bottom” due to health problems and had been relieved to be able to die together.

The German sisters, who became huge television stars in the 1960s and 70s, especially in Italy and the US, said a catalogue of health conditions had left them with no desire to carry on living.

In a letter released by a friend, the 89-year-olds wrote: “Dear friends, we’re sorry that we’ve chosen to go this way, but due to our medical conditions we had no quality of life any more.

“Apart from our heart problems, we no longer have our senses of taste and smell. On top of that Ellen has suffered a brain stem stroke, and as a result we’ve reached rock bottom.”

Because they had “entered the world together”, they wished “to depart it together the same way”, they added, concluding: “Don’t be sad; we’re happy that assisted dying exists, because certainly as a result we will have been spared a lot.”

They signed off: “Wishing you all the very best. We’ll see you on Cloud 9.”

The sisters died on 18 November last year , assisted by a doctor and a lawyer, in the villa they shared in the Grünwald district of Munich in Germany, which was divided into two mirrored halves, one for each.

They had planned the process together, working with an assisted dying organisation, but had kept this from their friends.

The letters were posted into the letter boxes of people close to them on the evening before their deaths, with a handwritten instruction on the envelope: “Don’t open until 17 November.” Inside each envelope they had placed the gift of a piece of their jewellery.

Bibi Johns, a 97-year-old actor and the singers’ closest friend, the three having met on the set of the 1959 film La Paloma, said she heard of their deaths on the radio before she had had the chance to open the letter from them.

“It was a shock,” she told the newspaper Bild. “Alice and Ellen were like sisters to me. For me it feels as if they have merely gone on a long journey. I’m still waiting for them to call me up.”

They bequeathed to her a gold pendant depicting the heads of two signs of the zodiac, a gift in 1968 from the French singer Marcel Amont to his then-partner Alice.

The year before their deaths, in an interview with Italy’s Corriere della Sera, the sisters said their wish was “to leave together, on the same day … the idea that one of the two will go first is very difficult to bear”. In their lifetimes they had also made a pact that neither would marry.

In the UK and Ireland, Samaritans can be contacted on freephone 116 123. In the US, you can call or text the National Suicide Prevention Lifeline on 988, chat on 988lifeline.org, or text HOME to 741741 to connect with a crisis counsellor. In Australia, the crisis support service Lifeline is 13 11 14. Other international helplines can be found at befrienders.org
