#112. Hashim Thaci's second conviction in The Hague reignites debate in Kosovo about justice and the Kosovo Specialist Chambers

Former President Hashim Thaci has been convicted of trying to influence witnesses in his war crimes trial. The conviction has reignited debate in Kosovo about the Kosovo Specialist Chambers, the court that convicted him.

A court in The Hague on Tuesday convicted former Kosovo President Hashim Thaci of obstructing justice and attempting to influence witnesses during his recent war crimes trial and sentenced him to 17 months in prison.

This term will be added to his 25-year sentence for war crimes, which was handed down by the same court, the Kosovo Specialist Chambers, on September 16.

According to the indictment of the Specialist Prosecutor's Office, Thaci had provided visitors at the detention facility in The Hague with confidential information about witnesses in his war crimes trial and had instructed them on how to influence their testimony.

Three other men — former head of the Kosovo Intelligence Agency Bashkim Smakaj, former Minister of Justice Hajredin Kuci, and Fadil Fazliu — were also convicted.

Thaci, who was the former political leader of the Kosovo Liberation Army (KLA) during the 1998-1999 Kosovo War, is viewed by many in his home country as a hero. His war crimes conviction in September was greeted in Kosovo with anger and dismay.

Thaci has vowed to appeal the war crimes conviction.

His two convictions touch deep sensitivities in Kosovo society. Tuesday's ruling brings renewed attention to the war that ended 27 years ago, the role played by its former leaders, and the continuing debate over justice, individual or collective responsibility for the KLA's war to liberate Kosovo from Serbia, and trust in judicial institutions.

The response from the government of Kosovo was swift.

In a statement issued on Tuesday, the government said, "Trust in justice is built not only on the decisions that are made, but also on the manner in which they are reached. Therefore, our commitment to the rule of law and our insistence on the full respect of rights and procedural guarantees are part of the same principled position: Justice must not only be done, but must also be fair, transparent, and credible."

The Kosovo Specialist Chambers was established in 2015 by the parliament of Kosovo at the instigation of the European Union and the United States.

The court was created to investigate serious crimes allegedly committed by members of the KLA during and after the Kosovo War.

For security reasons, including concerns about witness intimidation, the court was located in The Hague in the Netherlands.

After Thaci and three others were convicted of war crimes in first instance in September, Kosovo's state authorities established a working group to draft a strategy regarding the appeal proceedings before the Kosovo Specialist Chambers.

The group is chaired by Foreign Minister Glauk Konjufca and includes members of Kosovo's parliament.

Prime Minister Albin Kurti has stated that the working group continues to believe that "the first-instance judgment of the Specialist Chambers is unjust, both legally and morally," and that as a result, "the government is working to secure a correction at the appeals level before the Specialist Chambers."

In addition, the parliament of Kosovo is currently considering amendments to the law governing the Kosovo Specialist Chambers.

The draft law, which is expected to be adopted, envisages narrowing the court's mandate in several areas, including the time period covered by its jurisdiction, limiting the maximum prison sentence to 15 years, allowing sentences to be served in Kosovo, and introducing the possibility of presidential pardons for persons convicted by final court decisions.

How do political analysts and legal experts view the parliament's attempt to amend the law on the Kosovo Specialist Chambers before the judicial process involving the four men convicted of war crimes has reached a final verdict?

"From an international perspective, at first glance, this initiative could even be interpreted as an attempt to interfere in the judicial process," said political analyst and university professor Artan Muhaxheri.

"Precisely for this reason, it is essential that Kosovo's political spectrum and its legal experts demonstrate beyond any doubt that this measure does not conflict with Kosovo's international obligations toward the European Union," Muhaxheri told DW, adding that this is the only way to ensure that the desired outcome is achieved and that the initiative does not produce the opposite effect.

"Issues relating to jurisdiction, statutory limits on maximum sentences, and other similar matters must be argued professionally and rigorously, demonstrating that they fully guarantee judicial independence and enjoy solid support within Kosovo's legal system, the same legal system that, first and foremost, established the Specialist Chambers," he said.

Ehat Miftaraj, executive director of the Kosovo Law Institute (IKD), told DW that although the Kosovo Specialist Chambers were established by the Republic of Kosovo itself, "the way the judicial process has unfolded has created the impression that judges, prosecutors and certain international actors have treated not only the accused but also Kosovo itself with distrust, prejudice, and an often condescending approach."

Miftaraj went on to say that reactions and objections in Kosovo to the decisions of the Specialist Chambers are also interpreted and judged in the historical and emotional context of the war and what Kosovo's citizens experienced during and after it.

"In a democratic society, every citizen, including political and institutional representatives, has the right to criticize judicial decisions," he said. "However, there is a fundamental distinction between criticism based on legal arguments and attempts to delegitimize courts or exert influence over judicial proceedings. And here, there is room for criticism of both sides."

#110. How Ukraine struck the Kerch bridge, a symbol of Putin’s rule

With the SpaceX satellite system faltering, Ukraine’s military launched a surprise, multi-pronged attack to take down vital bridge to Crimea

At 10pm on Monday evening a Ukrainian soldier scrawled a message on a kamikaze drone. It said simply: “The bridge is fucked.” He then added: “We will stand and Moscow will fall.” It was a freezing night, with a bright starry sky and no clouds. Lines of ice had formed on the drone’s bulky wings. Soon, it would be hurtling towards its target – about 500km to the south.

Typically the soldiers who gathered in this muddy field in central Ukraine were attacking oil refineries deep inside Russia. In recent months the work had become almost mundane: they had hit so many of them. The objective that evening, however, was unique. It was a structure personally associated with Vladimir Putin and his grandiose imperial ambitions.

“We knew what the target was. Everyone was enthusiastic and excited,” one person who took part in the special operation, using the pseudonym “Leon”, told the Guardian. His 7th Deep Strike Centre is part of “Magyar’s Birds”, a brigade named after the call sign of its founder, Robert Brovdi, the head of Ukraine’s unmanned systems forces. Leon said: “Nobody gets excited any more about oil refineries. This was different.”

The soldiers got ready to launch, then watched the biplane-like drone, laden with explosives, disappear across the southern Ukrainian steppe.

Over the next four hours, hundreds of attack drones followed the same linear path, flying first over land and then over the Black Sea. In the early hours of the morning they arrived at their destination: the Kerch bridge connecting occupied Crimea with Russia. Putin opened the monumental road and rail crossing in 2018, driving across it in a Kamaz truck. This was four years after his undercover troops – known as little green men – seized the peninsula.

Russian propagandists had described the bridge as impregnable. It was, they said, an edifice as strong and unyielding as their country’s iron leader. For Ukraine, by contrast, it was a hated symbol of Russian arrogance and colonial thinking. Also, officials pointed out, it was illegal. Twice Kyiv had tried to blow it up – with a truck bomb in 2022, and a sea drone the following year.

On this occasion, the Ukrainian military’s careful, multi-stage plan worked perfectly. “It was a well-coordinated operation. Waves of drones started from different locations. The goal was to overwhelm Russia’s well-organised air defence system, rather than the bridge itself,” Leon explained.

As expected, the initial salvo tied up Russia’s local defences. According to insiders, Ukraine then launched a second drone wave. Its function was crucial and different: to act as a relay system for radio signals. Previously, Ukrainian units had used Starshield, the military equivalent of Starlink, for mid-range strikes on occupied territory. Elon Musk’s SpaceX owns the satellite internet network, which is overseen by the Pentagon and the Trump administration.

Ten days ago, however, limits were mysteriously imposed on Ukrainian drones, so they could no longer fly at above 161 km/h (100 mph). SpaceX insists there is no slowdown – a statement Ukraine’s president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, says is untrue. On Monday night, it appears Kyiv bypassed US restrictions by setting up its own extraordinary and innovative real-time video and radio connection.

Once the relay was in place, a third wave of attacks could begin. This time they featured Flamingos, a potent Ukrainian-made cruise missile capable of carrying 1,000kg of explosives – enough to seriously damage the bridge. An unknown number of missiles – some reports say at least four – were launched from southern Ukraine and sent towards Crimea.

A video from one of the Flamingos shows it drawing inexorably towards the target. The onboard camera captures a ghostly night-time scene. The crossing looms into view against a grisaille background. Suddenly a gigantic steel arch appears. The feed cuts out. Other footage shows the moment of impact: an orange fireball, pricking the surrounding blackness.

Photos taken on Tuesday morning in the aftermath of the attack show a scene of watery devastation. A massive arch had tumbled overnight into the Kerch straight, with a large concrete chunk of the road crossing. Nobody will be driving across the bridge anytime soon. The adjacent railway arch span was also damaged. “We were very happy, swapping messages all night,” Leon said.

One source in Ukraine’s armed forces said the timing of the operation – hailed by Zelenskyy as “unprecedented” – was deliberate. It was conducted ahead of a meeting on Thursday between Xi Jinping, Donald Trump and Putin in the Chinese city of Shenzhen, on the sidelines of an Apec summit. “The bridge is a symbol of Putin’s rule. Destroying it sends a message to the other two great leaders that Putin is not as strong as he pretends,” the person said.

They added: “We understand that what we did shows the world that the king is nude. Putin is weak and vulnerable and we just proved it. The operation was purposefully designed for this specific historic moment. Putin’s plan was to bluff at the summit about how powerful he is. In reality, he can’t even protect the most important and symbolic pillars of his regime.”

In addition to Putin’s political embarrassment before his peers, the disabling of the Kerch bridge has practical consequences. Since annexing Crimea, Russia has militarised the peninsula, effectively turning it into a giant and fortified army base. Since 2022 it has been used as a launch platform for drone and missile attacks on the port of Odesa, and other Ukrainian cities in the region.

Russia’s occupying southern army has relied on the bridge for regular deliveries of ammunition, equipment and fuel. With the crossing partially closed – rail travel reportedly reopened on Tuesday – Russia will have to use an under fire land corridor instead, connecting its Rostov oblast with the Moscow-controlled parts of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia and Kherson provinces.

“We have disrupted their logistics to Crimea. There will be a lot of problems for Russia’s army in the south,” Leon predicted. Debris from the bridge strike is obstructing the narrow shipping corridor through the Kerch strait, linking the Sea of Azov and the Black Sea. The Azov sea now resembles a lake. Passenger ferries are unable to move, with the occupied ports of Mariupol and Berdyansk cut off.

The soldiers returned from their mission jubilant and elated. Back at their headquarters, they gathered as usual for a minute’s silence, in memory of those killed in Russia’s bloody war. “We had our daily ritual as usual. Then there were congratulations on a very successful and important operation,” Leon said. Then he added: “We are paying them back.”

Luke Harding’s Betrayal: Trump, Putin and the New Age of Conquest is published by Guardian Faber. To support the Guardian, order your copy at guardianbookshop.com. Delivery charges may apply.

#108. Pretending to be in New Zealand, booking leave and buying consoles - the lengths fans are going to for GTA 6

Gamers can hardly contain their excitement as the release of Grand Theft Auto 6 approaches.

Grand Theft Auto fans have waited 13 long years for the latest edition of the game to be released - and going to extreme lengths to be ready for its launch.

GTA 6 will be released at midnight on Thursday - and Victoria Evans, from West Sussex, is not leaving anything to chance.

She has her alarm set for 05:00 GMT, and has a day off booked. She says she put the game's release on her leave request form which was approved, she told BBC News, despite the fact her boss "doesn't know what GTA 6 is".

She's far from the only one. For the game's launch day, HR software company BrightHR, external looked at absence data from more than 1.2 million employees from 70,000 businesses across the UK.

It found GTA was mentioned in 4.6% of time-off requests by employees. Throughout November and December, it said an estimated 83,000 days had been booked off for the game.

And booking leave is only one of the ways fans are making sure for the big day. Others are buying a new console to play it on or even pretending they're halfway around the world to get early access.

Market intelligence firm Sensor Tower estimates, external GTA 6 has already generated $1.18bn (Â£894m) from 12 million pre-orders - although Rockstar has not confirmed this.

Despite the option to preload the 212GB game, Jennifer Holmes, chief executive of the London Internet Exchange, said several UK internet providers including BT and Virgin Media had "increased capacity in anticipation of higher demand" ahead of launch.

Grand Theft Auto 6 will feature a playable female character for the first time since the 2D games

GTA 6 - all you need to know about Rockstar's blockbuster game

Gamers have long been known to book time off work for major releases.

When Red Dead Redemption 2 launched in October 2018, social media users were sharing plans to take the day off, with one analytics firm reportedly finding, external more people talking about calling in sick for it than any other game that year.

Similar conversations took place ahead of Cyberpunk 2077's release in December 2020, with fans discussing, external taking several days off to play.

GTA 6 is set in a fictional state inspired by Florida, which has featured heavily in the game's marketing, including this promotional blimp.

Some places have decided to lean into the trend for GTA 6 by offering incentives around the game - including the US Army's 3rd Infantry Division based at Fort Stewart, Georgia.

It gave some active-duty soldiers a four-day pass to play the game when it is released in November, as long as they sign a reenlistment contract for a minimum of two years between 1 August and 12 November.

"The idea was to have a unique incentives programme that connects to what soldiers are interested in," public affairs officer for the division, Lieutenant Colonel Angel Tomko confirmed to the BBC in August.

"There are 130 soldiers eligible within the battalion, and 20 have selected this incentive as part of their reenlistment," she said at the time.

Aside from taking time off, some gamers have also been discussing a technical loophole which could give them valuable extra hours of play before it officially unlocks in their country.

Because GTA 6 will release at midnight local time in all regions, New Zealand will become one of the first places where it is available, due to it reaching midnight earliest because of its position near the international date line.

This has led to some investigating ways to "trick" their consoles into thinking they're based in the island country when they are in fact thousands of miles away.

According to Google Trends, searches for the "GTA 6 New Zealand trick" have surged by more than 5,000% in the UK over the past month.

Games journalist Vic Hood said many gamers would be looking to play earlier to "get in on the action as soon as possible", as well as to prevent spoilers.

"As New Zealand is 13 hours ahead of the UK, that's 13 hours of gameplay that has time to spread across the internet and potentially spoil some key story beats and in-game surprises," she said.

"Took an extra day of paid time off when I found this out," one fan posted on Reddit on the trick.

It also means gamers would be playing the game at the much nicer time of 11:00 GMT, rather than having to stay up all night.

But another Xbox user shared caution on Reddit, noting that while the loophole had been exploited for many years for other game releases, GTA 6 "might be the game where Microsoft finally does something about this 'exploit'" - which could include banning players.

GTA 6's developers Rockstar and publishers Take Two Interactive have not commented on the loophole, and neither have Sony nor Microsoft.

Louise Wooldridge from Ampere Analysis said both Microsoft and Sony had "rules governing regional accounts and purchases" for the Xbox and PlayStation, adding that Sony's were "slightly more restrictive".

Players who choose to use the workaround could therefore risk breaching those terms and conditions and potentially face restrictions on their accounts or lose access to their digital games.

The hype for the game has taken over locations across the world, including Selfridges in London

Some gamers will have to wait even longer than their console-playing counterparts to get their hands on GTA 6, as the game will not be available on PC at launch, similar to previous GTA releases.

"Spare a thought for the PC users who will have to live vicariously through others' YouTube videos and Twitch streams," gamer Stephen Finn told the BBC.

A PS5 now costs from Â£520, while the more powerful PS5 Pro is nearly Â£790.

Despite the hefty price tag, for some the temptation to join the community in playing what many have called the biggest game launch of the year, if not decade, has been too great.

Reddit posts and videos, such as one by US YouTuber isoish, external, show fans buying consoles specifically to play the game.

"So, the only game I'm going to have on here is GTA," the content creator said in the video.

After more than a decade of waiting, it seems some fans are determined not to waste a single minute of GTA 6's release - no matter the cost or extra effort.

#107. New UK-EU youth mobility scheme may not fully launch until 2030

Exclusive: deal expected to be sealed in principle at EU-UK summit on Friday but trickier elements could be postponed for six months

The new post-Brexit UK-EU youth mobility scheme may not come fully into force until 2030.

It will be a disappointment to any under-30s in the UK and the EU who hoped to travel, work or study in each other’s territories from next year after the long-awaited new deal.

EU ambassadors will discuss the latest draft joint statement at a meeting on Wednesday in Brussels, with talk of youth mobility going right to the wire.

“We aim to apply this agreement no later than 2028 and endeavour to ensure that opportunities under the scheme will be available to our young people no later than January 2030,” a draft text agreed by negotiators from London and Brussels says.

The two sides aim to come to a “supplementary agreement” on tuition fees for EU citizens in UK universities.

The text on this most contentious subject is not yet agreed with two alternatives mooted in the draft text – either a deal that students pay a reduced international fee or, alternatively, the same fee as UK nationals. The aim is that this would be agreed separately within six months.

This is to allow linkage with the Made in Europe legislation that will not reach its final stages until possibly April next year in a vote in the European parliament. The legislation is designed to give priority to EU supply chains in the car and other industries.

By linking the two issues the UK can get assurances that the final detail of the deal on mobility cannot be signed off unless it has been included in the Made in Europe legislation.

EU countries including Germany could accept an unlimited number of young Britons under a youth mobility scheme, according to sources.

Fresh details on how a scheme might work have emerged with EU leaders and the UK expected to settle on a quota of about 100,000 a year being allowed to work, travel or study in each other’s territories.

The UK has insisted on a quota of EU citizens entering the UK under the scheme to guard against mass migration, but some countries have said they will not follow suit on the other side of the Channel and would be open to taking unlimited numbers.

The quota is expected to be worked out on a country-by-country basis with smaller countries such as Malta and Luxembourg seeking a system that will not be based on a per-capita quota per EU member state.

The new agrifood deal would not be agreed until 1 March 2028, the draft statement seen by the Guardian confirms.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

In what looks to be a breakthrough for Burnham there may also be a commitment to enter a dialogue to remove barriers to touring artists, particularly bands and theatres companies that perform in multiple EU member states.

The 72-point joint statement also paves the way for further deals on steel between the UK and the EU.

But there is no agreement yet on whether the UK would commit to dynamically aligning with the EU on trade defence measures such as tariffs on Chinese cars.

The details came as Burnham said there is “no rush” to decide on the UK’s longer-term relationship with the EU, as he came under pressure from his own party to press the brakes on the pace of the rethink.

The prime minister said he would “take stock” of the wider relationship after Friday’s summit in Brussels, at which he also hopes to secure agreements on food and farming and emissions trading.

In an interview with Sky News, Burnham admitted he would now not set out the full options for a closer EU relationship until December – but denied he was “stepping back” from his previous commitments to pursue ties.

#106. Nvidia beats Q3 expectations in first report post-Jensen Huang

The AI chipmaker said it doubled its third-quarter revenues in the three months to October and confirmed that Jensen Huang would not be reinstated as CEO after a Chinese spy scandal.

US AI chip giant Nvidia doubled its third-quarter revenues to more than $115 billion (about €104.59 billion), the company said Tuesday.

Nvidia Chairman Harvey Jones also confirmed that the board of directors has no plans to bring back former CEO Jensen Huang.

Huang resigned on Thursday after a string of extramarital affairs came to light, including with a woman who is suspected of being a Chinese intelligence asset.

In a statement on Tuesday, interim CEO and CFO Colette Kress said it is "business as usual" as the firm tries to move on from the Huang affair.

"Our momentum is real. Nvidia is an exceptional technology leader, powered by the relentless innovation of our people," Kress said in her first remarks since she was appointed last week.

She did not address Huang's resignation or the uncertainty surrounding his successor as Nvidia's CEO.

During an earnings call with Nvidia investors on Tuesday, Chairman Jones said the board had "unanimously accepted" Huang's resignation and "does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The results, which exceeded expectations, were announced hours after Huang said he would be willing to return to the company if the board agreed.

After Huang's resignation, tech industry figures such as Elon Musk, as well as US President Donald Trump, called for Huang to be reinstated as part of a campaign called "Bring Jensen Back."

In a post on X — his first public statement since the scandal plunged the company and AI sector into crisis — Huang acknowledged that he had made "serious mistakes in his personal life" and had failed to "live up to the standards expected of everyone at" the company.

Huang also said he had received an "outpouring of calls from Nvidia's employees, investors, business partners, and customers" for him to return.

Huang, 63, co-founded Nvidia, which he led for more than three decades, turning it from a maker of graphics cards used in PC gaming into the dominant supplier of chips driving advanced AI models.

Under his leadership, Nvidia grew into one of the world's biggest — and most strategically important — companies.

Nvidia has been at the center of the AI boom that has pushed markets to record levels and spurred economic growth around the world.

Nvidia's chips are critical for training and operating advanced AI systems, making them crucial to governments, the military and the tech industry.

#105. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#103. Ukraine accuses Russia of targeting rescue operation after 'double-tap' strike kills 72

Ukraine accuses Russia of targeting rescuers after âdouble-tapâ strike

Ukrainian officials have accused Russia of targeting rescue and recovery operations two weeks after a deadly "double-tap" strike at a village close to the front line killed at least 72 people.

Ukraine says Russia carried out two attacks in succession on a humanitarian aid centre in the village of Komyshuvakha on 2 November - including the use of what it said was a 3,000kg (6,600lb) aerial bomb on a shelter where residents had fled for safety.

Verified images posted in the days after the attack show damage to emergency vehicles and excavation equipment at the site.

The State Emergency Service of Ukraine (DSNS) told BBC Verify there were multiple Russian strikes during the rescue.

The DSNS said three police officers, four workers and two rescue dogs were injured while five vehicles were damaged during the operation, which has now concluded.

With the DSNS saying the death toll had risen to at least 72 people, the attack on Komyshuvakha has become the second deadliest single incident of the Ukraine war.

Emergency service vehicles have also been targeted

Oleksiy, an intelligence officer investigating the incident for Ukraine's SBU security service, who asked to be identified only by his first name, said rescue vehicles were "clearly marked" and accused Russia of "deliberately destroying humanitarian equipment".

He added the village's infrastructure and the people still living in Komyshuvakha are also being systematically targeted by drones and bombs. Oleksiy accused Russian forces of "outright terror against civilians, against peaceful residents" to make life on the front line impossible.

BBC Verify has confirmed videos being shared by Ukrainian police that show rescue workers at the scene shouting "drone, drone" and running for cover on the day after the 2 November attack. A group can also be seen treating a victim.

Oleksiy said rescue crews operated under a constant threat of attack. "The enemy knows perfectly well that what is being carried out there is essentially a humanitarian operation to search for the dead," he said.

BBC Verify has asked the Russian Ministry of Defence for comment specifically about the Komyshuvakha attacks but has not received a response.

A general statement released on 2 November by the Russian MoD said it had targeted energy and transport infrastructure used by the Ukrainian military across the country.

While there are no military or industrial facilities in Komyshuvakha it is close to a key route used for Ukrainian troop movements, supplies and evacuations. The village was previously reported to have been struck by the Russians on 28 October.

Local people headed to the shelter area on 2 November after being told by a local Telegram group that aid would be distributed between 10:00 and 12:00 local time (08:00-10:00 GMT). Ukrainian authorities said an initial strike, at 09:45 local time, hit buildings above ground killing one woman.

Video shows moments between deadly Russian attacks on village in Ukraine

Bodycam footage, released by Ukrainian police in edited segments and analysed by BBC Verify, shows people trying to free a casualty from the rubble before declaring she had died.

Throughout the footage, warnings of an impending second strike can be heard on police radios with increasing urgency until people are instructed to run to the underground shelter.

The same footage, which was taken around an hour after the first attack, goes on to show people in a shelter. As they wait in silence a large explosion can be heard and the video goes dark.

Aftermath video filmed later shows bodies being recovered from a large crater immediately above the shelter. Twisted iron and rubble from the structure below can be seen beneath the surface.

Funerals have been held almost daily for the victims of the attack, including locals working to administer aid distribution, those seeking to collect it and soldiers who officials say arrived to assist with rescue efforts.

Trevor Ball, bomb disposal expert at intelligence firm at Armament Research Services, said a crater seen in the footage was consistent with the damage and depth left by a FAB-3000, a 3,000kg Russian bomb which can destroy underground structures.

His comments were echoed by Chris Proops, an ex-UK National Crime Agency investigator, who said the scale of the damage at the scene matched the size of the FAB-3000.

The remnants of the wing from a glide bomb guidance kit

Ball and Proops both confirmed verified photos of munition fragments obtained by BBC Verify from Ukrainian authorities show part of a guidance kit that is compatible with the FAB-3000, which increases the weapon's accuracy and allows it to be fired from a greater distance from the target.

International law experts told BBC Verify that Russia had committed a war crime if it knowingly attacked rescue workers.

Ben Saul, professor of international law at the University of Sydney, said this "appears to be either a deliberate attack on civilians or civilian objects - a war crime - or an indiscriminate attack - also a war crime".

Prof Samuel Moyn of Yale University said "directly targeting rescuers or their equipment is a gross violation of international humanitarian law". Precautions have to be taken to ensure that strikes target combatants rather than others, he added.

Additional reporting by Margaryta Maliukova, BBC Monitoring

#102. Trump’s ‘trade deal’ with Thailand is not a done deal.

President Trump’s 48-hour trip started with a meeting with Southeast Asian leaders in Manila. He will later meet Xi Jinping of China and Vladimir Putin of Russia in Shenzhen, a Chinese economic hub.

President Trump landed in Manila on Tuesday to begin a 48-hour Asia trip in which he will meet with Xi Jinping of China and other world leaders, with an unpredictable agenda that could include the future of American tariffs, nuclear arms control, the war in Ukraine and U.S. restrictions on advanced A.I. chips.

Mr. Trump was scheduled to meet with President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines and attend a gathering of Southeast Asian leaders. After less than 24 hours in Manila, he will head to the APEC economic summit in Shenzhen, China, where he was expected to meet with Mr. Xi and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia.

The brief itinerary appears to reflect Mr. Trump’s preferences. He dislikes big summits where he often is not the sole subject of attention. And he enjoys meeting with leaders of powerful nations — especially Mr. Xi and Mr. Putin, two authoritarian leaders he often talks about in admiring terms.

Mr. Trump will be meeting Mr. Xi for the third time this year, and a three-way meeting between China, Russia and the United States would be the first of its kind.

On Tuesday, Mr. Trump caught the end of a dayslong meeting of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations, or ASEAN, after issuing tariffs on countries across the region, even allies. Those moves added to a sentiment in Asia that the United States was not a reliable partner but rather one that treasured its commercial interests over its security interests. The only event of substance on the president’s schedule on Tuesday — signing what he had called a “trade deal” with Thailand — did not happen because Mr. Trump said it was still being finalized.

Mr. Trump met with leaders from the so-called Quad — the United States, India, Japan and Australia — soon after his arrival. All three partners are smarting from high energy prices triggered by the Iran war, a conflict Mr. Trump never discussed with them before launching it in late February.

In Shenzhen — the heart of China’s robotics and artificial intelligence industries — Mr. Trump will pursue détente with Mr. Xi. He needs to extend a soon-expiring trade truce that limited tariffs on Chinese goods entering the United States in return for a Chinese commitment not to cut off rare earths and magnets.

Then comes the main event: the meeting with Mr. Xi and Mr. Putin. Referring to the trilateral meeting, Mr. Trump told reporters before leaving Washington: “We are going to be talking about denuclearization, a tough subject because it is very hard for any of us to say we’ll get rid of our nukes.” Chinese officials have made clear they are not interested in nuclear talks until their arsenal is roughly the size of the American one.

Mr. Trump also said, “We will talk about Iran a little,” insisting the situation was in “very good shape.” Analysts say it is unlikely that the meeting will make any progress on Iran, with Russia happy to see energy prices elevated since the war began, and China eager to see them rapidly decline.

Protests: Thousands of demonstrators assembled in downtown Manila on Tuesday in opposition to Mr. Trump’s visit and to a U.S.-led initiative called Pax Silica, which aims to unite partners around A.I. supply chains independent of China. Read more ›

North Korea: Mr. Putin could meet with Kim Jong-un if the North Korean leader also travels to China for the APEC summit, Russian state news agencies reported, citing the Kremlin’s spokesman. It was not clear whether Mr. Kim would attend the summit.

Ukraine: Mr. Trump also said he would talk about Ukraine, on which Mr. Putin’s aides are engaging in talks with the U.S. and Ukrainian officials, but also renewing demands that NATO pull back its troops to where they were before the alliance expanded toward Russia.

President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia could meet with Kim Jong-un if the North Korean leader travels to China for the APEC summit this week, the Kremlin said, reflecting the deepening of ties between their countries in recent years.

Asked about the possibility of a meeting, Dmitri S. Peskov, the Kremlin spokesman, said on Monday that it would “of course” happen if Mr. Kim went to Shenzhen, the Chinese economic hub and host city, according to Russian state news agencies. North Korea has not confirmed whether Mr. Kim plans to attend the summit.

Mr. Putin and Mr. Kim have publicly met three times since 2022, and North Korea has been a key supporter of Russia’s military actions in Ukraine.

As the war in Ukraine nears its fifth anniversary, with no end in sight, the Kremlin is expected to use Mr. Putin’s many meetings at the APEC summit to showcase how Russia is regarded as one of the major international powers alongside the United States and China.

A visit by Mr. Kim would be a rare foray outside North Korea for the leader, and it would further raise the prospect that he could meet with President Trump in China. On the first day of his 48-hour trip to Asia, Mr. Trump told reporters that Mr. Kim “would like” to come to the APEC summit. The president has repeatedly said he would like to meet with Mr. Kim again.

On Wednesday, Mr. Putin is scheduled to meet with Xi Jinping, China’s leader. On Thursday, he is set to take part in a trilateral summit with Mr. Xi and Mr. Trump, the first meeting of its kind. The Kremlin said on Monday that it was working to arrange a bilateral meeting between Mr. Putin and Mr. Trump.

Before his departure for China, Mr. Putin met on Monday in the Kremlin with Jo Yong-won, a secretary of North Korea’s ruling Workers’ Party. During the meeting, Mr. Putin said that North Korea’s help in driving Ukrainian forces from the Kursk region of Russia last year “marked a new chapter in the history of military cooperation between our countries.”

Moscow planned to unveil a monument in Kursk by the end of the year to honor the North Korean troops, Mr. Putin said.

During the final meeting of the day, with ASEAN leaders, President Trump told President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines:  “I don’t want to date myself, but I knew your parents very well. They were great. I believe in genetics. They did a good job. You have very good genetics, I can tell you that.”

It was not the first time Mr. Trump has praised the family of Mr. Marcos, who is the son of the dictator Ferdinand E. Marcos, who ruled the Philippines for 20 years, imposed martial law and was ousted in 1986.

The only event of substance on President Trump’s schedule today, the signing of a trade deal between the United States and Thailand, did not happen because it was still being finalized. Trump told reporters that “Thailand will eliminate all tariffs on U.S. imports” and that the United States would provide tariff relief, but the two sides signed only a memorandum about the agreement. The president said the deal would be done in the coming weeks.

During an address to Quad leaders, President Trump called Prime Minister Narendra Modi of India, which is a member of the group, “a great leader and a friend of mine, highly respected by everybody all over the world.” The president also praised Modi as he stood with other leaders and took a few questions, calling him a “very tough man.”

India and the United States are navigating a rocky patch in their relationship, largely tied to trade and India’s purchases of Russian oil. Although Modi and Trump signed an interim framework for a trade deal in February, talks have dragged on, frustrating both parties.

The Nvidia turmoil and its implications for the U.S.-China relationship is a backdrop to President Trump’s visit. On Tuesday, shares of technology companies in Asia tumbled after the Trump administration announced rules to restrict overseas access to advanced A.I. chips like those sold by Nvidia. In Hong Kong trading, Alibaba fell 5 percent, while Tencent’s stock dropped nearly 4 percent. The Chinese A.I. firms MiniMax Group fell 7 percent, and Z.AI Co. declined 5 percent.

In the United States, tech stocks stabilized from a sell-off the day before. Nvidia shares were up about 1 percent before the start of regular trading in New York. The company is set to report its latest financial results later on Tuesday.

President Trump is expected to meet with Prime Minister Anutin Charnvirakul of Thailand on Tuesday in Manila to sign what the White House has described as a “trade deal.”

But Thailand’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs said in a statement on Tuesday that Bangkok was expecting to agree only on a “joint statement marking progress in negotiations.”

The dissonance comes amid growing signs within ASEAN, the Association of Southeast Asian Nations, that the United States is not a reliable partner. The meeting between Mr. Trump and Mr. Anutin is taking place on the sidelines of an ASEAN summit.

For more than a year, Washington and Bangkok have been negotiating a new trade agreement that covers items like tariffs, food imports and labor rights.

Last year, the United States battered Thailand with a 36 percent tariff, one of the steepest rates levied by Mr. Trump after his return to office. The United States is one of Thailand’s largest export markets, including for goods like pet food.

Mr. Trump later reduced the tariffs to 19 percent, before the U.S. Supreme Court struck them down in February. The administration eventually found a way to still impose 12.5 percent tariffs on Thai goods.

The signing of a U.S.-Thai trade deal might still be far off, requiring Thailand’s parliament to pass a law, but the meeting delivers an easy publicity win for Mr. Anutin and Mr. Trump, who is known to revel even in perceived success.

Pavida Pananond, an economist at Thammasat Business School in Bangkok, said, “The level of trust Thailand has in the U.S. is not at the same level as before all this economic weaponization of tariffs.”

After handing over the chairmanship of ASEAN to Singapore, President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines held a news conference. “President Trump has just arrived,” he said. “He is waiting for us to go to him and to invite him to this really very informal kind of summit.”

Mr. Marcos ended his comments by apologizing for not taking more questions from journalists. “I cannot keep the president of the United States waiting,” he said. “Sorry.”

At his hotel, President Trump sat down for a meeting with Quad leaders and took a few questions. He said that he was “not worried” about bank outages and that the United States would be “looking into” reports of plans by the Iranians to drop a dirty bomb in Washington. He also said that Kim Jong-un, the North Korean leader, would “like to come” to the APEC summit in Shenzhen.

Several hundred activists massed on Tuesday in central Manila to march to the Philippine International Convention Center, the venue for the Association of Southeast Asian Nations summit.

By the time President Trump landed in the city, the ranks of the protesters had swelled to thousands.

Most of the demonstrators were university students who said that they were fed up with the government, which they said was more focused on preserving its relationship with the United States than helping impoverished Filipinos.

Cinna Sison, 18, who studies European languages at the University of the Philippines, said they were inspired by the recent mass protests in France.

“I love the French,” she said. “They know how to protest.”

Ms. Sison said she had worn colorful makeup to mark her first time attending a demonstration. “I am here to show my solidarity with fellow young activists. We are here to show our disgust to the fascist Trump and to Marcos for kowtowing to his every wish,” she said, referring to President Ferdinand Marcos Jr. of the Philippines.

Mr. Trump is expected to hold a bilateral meeting with Mr. Marcos on Wednesday before he heads to China for an Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit.

The Philippine president, who earlier this year described Washington as a “steadfast partner,” has said little publicly about meeting Mr. Trump. His administration has hinted that the two sides are working on a military aid agreement.

In Manila, Ms. Sison wore a red scarf on her head and a red shirt with the words “Silicone not Silica” — a reference to Pax Silica, a U.S.-led consortium of countries aiming to create artificial intelligence supply chains independent of China.

Ms. Sison added that Pax Silica was a symbolic return of the Americans to the Philippines, the site of former U.S. military bases, as Washington leaned on a regional ally to counter China.

“Neighborliness is important, but it is not important to be subservient to U.S. interests,” she said.

Nearby, Raja Bausing, a 22-year-old visual artist, was busy putting the finishing touches on a papier-mâché model of Mr. Trump holding Mr. Marcos.

“We want to show how Trump is a warmonger, while Marcos is a lap dog,” she said. “They are very disrespectful to Filipinos. Trump was welcomed in a grand way and yet we Filipinos are maltreated.”

President Trump landed in Manila on Tuesday, beginning a trip to Asia in which he will attend an ASEAN summit, greet members of the Quad, then travel to China for an APEC meeting.

Here’s what those acronyms mean, as well as some highlights from previous summits — including gold crowns and awkward handshakes.

The Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation forum is a group of 21 member economies, most of which are from that region. Its members also include Canada, Mexico, Australia, Russia and the United States. It promotes trade in Asia, but is not a free trade bloc. Its leaders meet annually, with the location rotating each year.

Meetings between world leaders on the sidelines of the summit can often overshadow the main proceedings. This year, in the Chinese tech hub of Shenzhen, Mr. Trump is expected to meet with President Xi Jinping of China and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia, as well as with Mr. Xi separately.

Last year, when Mr. Trump visited the South Korean city of Gyeongju during the week of the APEC meeting, President Lee Jae Myung of South Korea gave him a replica of a gold crown that researchers believe was worn by an ancient Korean ruler. Mr. Trump also met with Mr. Xi in the nearby city of Busan, where the two struck a truce in their countries’ trade war.

A notable incident happened in Sydney, Australia, in 2007, when a group of local television comedians brought an Osama bin Laden look-alike through security. The team came within yards of a hotel where President George W. Bush was staying.

The Association of Southeast Asian Nations is a grouping of 11 countries — Brunei, Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos, Malaysia, Myanmar, the Philippines, Singapore, Thailand, East Timor and Vietnam — which promotes political cooperation and stability across the region. Its leaders meet twice annually.

The bloc was formed in 1967 and represents nearly 700 million people. Its economy and its role in global markets have strengthened in recent years. The United States also sees ASEAN as a geostrategic bulwark against China.

In 2009, an ASEAN meeting in Thailand fell into acrimony when Secretary of State Hillary Clinton compared North Korea’s leaders to “small children” when discussing their nuclear program. The country’s leadership, in turn, described Ms. Clinton as a “schoolgirl” and a “pensioner.”

During the 2017 ASEAN summit in Manila, Mr. Trump grimaced while jostling with Southeast Asian leaders for an awkward cross-armed handshake that was captured in a group photograph.

The Quadrilateral Security Dialogue is an informal partnership between Australia, India, Japan and the United States, which was formed in part to counter China. It was proposed by Prime Minister Shinzo Abe of Japan in 2007.

Beijing opposed the initial discussion, lodging formal diplomatic protests. Australia withdrew in 2008 and the dialogue was put on hold for some time. But the grouping was revived in 2017 on the sidelines of an ASEAN conference.

President Biden was the first U.S. president to convene a meeting among the leaders of the nations as a foursome. In 2024, he hosted the Quad leaders at his home in Wilmington, Del. Mr. Trump has appeared to be less enthusiastic about the partnership. Last year, he did not attend the Quad summit in India.

Reporting from the village of Manabayucan in the Philippines and the capital, Manila

In May, Roseller Rodriguez, a mayor in the Philippines, was startled to learn from news reports that U.S. officials had arrived in his district to start an artificial intelligence hub.

He saw a video of senior American and Filipino officials standing on a makeshift stage, unveiling a new initiative called Pax Silica.

Mr. Rodriguez had not been invited. Nor had he ever heard about the hub. Was it a data center? Where would the water and electricity come from? What would happen to the farmers living there?

Months later, he says he has few answers.

“I’m not concerned with the geopolitics of Pax Silica,” Mr. Rodriguez said in his office in the municipality of Capas. “My problem is Pax Silica will be built in our community.”

The United States started Pax Silica to unite partners around A.I. supply chains independent of China. The initiative is part of President Trump’s effort to counter Beijing’s economic and technological influence.

Trump administration officials have cast it as a 21st-century successor to Pax Americana, the era of relative peace among major powers after World War II.

The significance of Pax Silica, which 26 signatories have joined, has become heightened after revelations that Nvidia’s former chief executive, Jensen Huang, was targeted by Chinese intelligence, exposing the growing geopolitical stakes of the global A.I. race.

In the Philippines, America’s oldest ally in Asia, the backlash has been fierce.

Dozens of Filipino performers greeted President Trump as he descended the stairs of Air Force One onto the tarmac of Ninoy Aquino Airport in Manila, waving flags and playing “Y.M.C.A.” by the Village People on drums and trumpets.

President Vladimir V. Putin could meet with Kim Jong-un if the North Korean leader also travels to China for the APEC summit this week, Russian state news agencies reported, citing Dmitri S. Peskov, the Kremlin’s spokesman. Before his departure for China, Putin met on Monday with a secretary of North Korea’s ruling Workers’ Party. It was not yet clear whether the North Korean leader would attend the summit.

President Trump has landed in Manila, lingering on the tarmac for a welcome ceremony before walking alone to Marine One. He has several events tonight, but he will not leave his hotel for them. A meeting with quad leaders, a U.S.-Thailand trade deal signing and a dinner will all be held at the resort where the president is staying. In other words, he showed up late to this summit, and the summit will be coming to him.

Thousands of protesters have assembled in downtown Manila, chanting in Tagalog, “Trump leave the Philippines.” They are opposed to his Pax Silica A.I. supply chain initiative.

President Trump’s stopover at the Association of Southeast Asian Nations summit is part of a state visit to the Philippines, where he will be received with a 21-gun salute.

The last time he met President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines, he was full of praise for the Filipino leader.

“Great family, great family legacy and highly respected in his country,” Mr. Trump said while hosting Mr. Marcos at the White House last year.

But that legacy was long considered an obstacle to Mr. Marcos’s political ambitions. He is the son of the dictator Ferdinand E. Marcos, who ruled the Philippines for 20 years, imposed martial law and was ousted in 1986 by a nonviolent uprising. The elder Marcos died in exile in Hawaii, without facing trial on U.S. criminal charges that he had plundered the Philippine Treasury. Years later, a court found his estate liable for torture, summary executions and disappearances.

The younger Marcos spent years rehabilitating his family’s image and won election in 2022. He is hosting Mr. Trump at his official residence, Malacañang Palace, which was also his childhood home.

Mr. Marcos and his wife, Liza Marcos, will receive Mr. Trump on Wednesday with a 21-gun salute. Mr. Trump is expected to sign the palace guest book, hold closed-door meetings, pose for a handshake, then attend a state luncheon held in his honor.

It is not clear whether Mr. Trump, who has a well-known affinity for autocratic leaders, will meet with Mr. Marcos’s mother, Imelda, 97, with whom he has socialized in New York. Ms. Marcos wielded immense power when her husband was president and later came to be known for her flamboyant lifestyle, shoe collection and unexplained wealth.

Mr. Marcos has said that when he called to congratulate Mr. Trump on his election victory in November 2024, Mr. Trump asked about Imelda Marcos.

“My mother is really his friend, he knows my mother very well,” Mr. Marcos said, relaying the conversation. “He was asking about her, ‘How’s Imelda?’ I told him she sends her congratulations.”

After the pomp and circumstance at Malacañang, which traces its roots to the Spanish colonial era in the Philippines, Mr. Trump is headed to Shenzhen, China. The highlight of that leg of his trip will be a trilateral meeting with two strongmen: President Xi Jinping of China and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia.

For the second year in a row, President Trump is attending the annual gathering of Southeast Asian leaders, a show of alliance to a region that the United States sees as a strategic and maritime counterweight to China.

But Mr. Trump reached the gathering, held this year in Manila, only on Tuesday, just as the summit was wrapping up, and he is not expected to make any significant policies or diplomacy moves.

Mixed signals like this — to say nothing of Mr. Trump’s punishing tariffs — are adding to a widespread belief within ASEAN, the Association of Southeast Asian Nations, that the United States is not a reliable partner. That leaves the bloc balancing between Washington and Beijing, with which many of its members have deep trade partnerships.

“They’ve learned that the United States is not reliable, so a lot of the hedging has been away from the United States,” said Ted Osius, a former U.S. ambassador to Vietnam.

The Philippines, America’s oldest security ally in Asia, provides a window into this balancing act.

After taking office three years ago, President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. reversed his predecessor’s tilt toward China and pivoted back toward the United States. That stance contributed to many tense encounters between Filipino and Chinese vessels in the South China Sea, where Manila and Beijing have competing territorial claims.

As U.S. patrols in the South China Sea have dropped, Mr. Marcos has appeared to be more conciliatory toward Beijing. In August, he said that there was a “distinct possibility” that the Philippines and China could partner on oil and gas exploration in those waters despite their territorial feud.

“Marcos Jr. already has begun a shift toward China, one that a single visit by Trump is unlikely to stop,” Joshua Kurlantzick, a senior fellow for Southeast Asia and South Asia at the Council on Foreign Relations, wrote last week.

On Sunday, China and ASEAN failed to agree on a code of conduct in the South China Sea — something the Philippines, which has some of the biggest stakes in the dispute, had vowed to secure while chairing the bloc this year.

Chester Cabalza, president of International Development and Security Cooperation, a Philippine think tank, said: “Strategically, Manila opens its doors for conciliation with Beijing to stabilize normal ties. But on the ground, the real battle has yet to subside.”

Domestically, Mr. Marcos has to walk a tightrope, too.

“For Filipino fishermen and coastal communities, the South China Sea is not simply about a geopolitical dispute. It concerns livelihoods, access to maritime resources and national dignity,” said Arnaud Leveau, president of the Asia Centre, a Paris-based think tank. Any diplomatic accommodation perceived as sacrificing Filipino interests, he said, would therefore be politically risky.

The wider region has been battered by the consequences of another Trump policy: the war in Iran. Southeast Asia depends heavily on oil and gas imports from the Middle East and was roiled by soaring prices of fuel. In the Philippines, crops rotted in fields because farmers could not afford to transport them to markets. In Thailand, public servants were asked to use stairs instead of elevators to reduce energy expenses.

ASEAN comprises 11 nations — Brunei, Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos, Malaysia, Myanmar, the Philippines, Singapore, Thailand, Vietnam and, since last year, East Timor — with vastly different degrees of freedom and wealth. The bloc represents almost 700 million people, or almost 10 percent of the global population.

Making decisions on a consensus-basis and pledging not to interfere in one other’s internal affairs, the bloc has often been criticized as slow and ineffective, with the situation in Myanmar often cited as an example. A coup in that country in 2021 unleashed a civil war that ranks among the world’s most violent conflicts.

Myanmar’s leaders remain barred from ASEAN summits, under an agreement that was extended on Monday. Still, almost half of the bloc’s member states have had bilateral engagements with Myanmar since the coup leader, Min Aung Hlaing, used stage-managed elections to take the post of president this year. Washington has also recently re-engaged in direct talks with Myanmar.

Mr. Trump’s attendance at the summit, which concludes with an ASEAN-U.S. meeting, is part of a state visit to the Philippines. He is expected to hold a bilateral meeting with Mr. Marcos on Wednesday before heading to China for an Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit. Mr. Marcos has said little publicly about meeting Mr. Trump; but earlier this year, he described Washington as a “steadfast partner.” His administration has hinted that the two sides are working on a military aid agreement.

In recent days, hundreds of demonstrators have gathered in Manila to protest Mr. Trump’s visit. They oppose his “Pax Silica” initiative, a move to build a supply chain for the artificial intelligence industry that bypasses China, saying it would exploit Philippine natural resources.

Renato Reyes, who led one of the protest groups on Sunday afternoon, said, “Marcos is so desperate to welcome Donald Trump that he will roll out the red carpet, even if Trump arrives late for the summit.”

Aie Balagtas See contributed reporting from Manila, and Damien Cave from Ho Chi Minh City, Vietnam.

President Trump sets off this week for meetings in Asia that will culminate in a summit with two strongmen he has often praised: President Xi Jinping of China and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia.

It will be the first time the three men, who command the world’s largest nuclear arsenals, will have sat down together. Mr. Trump plans to discuss the reduction of nuclear weapons in the world and the war against Iran, according to a White House spokeswoman. He is not expected to bring up Russia’s war with Ukraine.

Mr. Trump will travel first to Manila, then to southern China, and will meet with regional leaders confronting an array of uncertainties. Those include the course of the war between the United States and Iran, high energy prices and mounting concern over the risks of artificial intelligence.

For Mr. Trump, the trip will be a pivot to foreign policy and relationships after American voters delivered a swift rebuke to his party in the midterm elections. His trip also comes as his administration faces scrutiny over a scandal involving the former chief executive of Nvidia, the tech giant, which has raised questions about Chinese espionage and American tech exports.

The highlight of Mr. Trump’s trip is expected to be his meeting with Mr. Xi and Mr. Putin on Thursday in China.

The prospect of their summit has drawn comparisons to the 1945 conference in the Crimean city of Yalta, where the leaders of the United States, the Soviet Union and Britain met to decide the shape of postwar Europe.

The White House has said that Mr. Trump wants to discuss the threat of nuclear proliferation. U.S. officials have expressed concerns about China’s accelerating nuclear buildup, expected to reach 1,000 nuclear warheads by 2030.

The last nuclear arms control treaty between the United States and Russia expired in February. Trump administration officials have said that any new agreement on nuclear arms control must include China, but Beijing has shown no interest in the topic. China has an estimated 620 nuclear warheads, compared with the more than 5,000 that Russia and the United States have each, according to the Stockholm International Peace Research Institute.

U.S. administration officials said they did not expect a deal out of the meeting, which they characterized as a starting point for discussions.

The gathering has prompted concern in Ukraine that Mr. Putin might push for a deal, deciding Ukraine’s fate without Kyiv’s involvement. A U.S. administration official said Ukraine was not expected to be part of the discussion between the three leaders.

Mr. Trump will start his trip in the Philippines, the oldest American treaty ally in the Asia-Pacific region, as the United States faces questions about its support for its partners in a region where China’s military and economic might continues to grow.

In Manila, President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. plans to discuss with Mr. Trump the South China Sea, where the Philippines and China have competing claims. Philippine officials have said that they hope Mr. Trump’s visit might result in more American military assistance.

Mr. Trump is also expected to meet with leaders of ASEAN, a Southeast Asian grouping of 11 nations, as well as a gathering of the so-called Quad grouping of the United States, India, Australia and Japan.

Prime Minister Anthony Albanese of Australia said on Sunday that he expected “lots of engagement” with Mr. Trump during the meetings.

“Obviously, that’s our most important ally,” he said. “But we want to see something constructive come out of that, including we want to see a de-escalation in the conflicts that are around the world.”

Chinese analysts described the meeting of the Quad group, formed in part to counter China, as an effort by Mr. Trump to project power after his party’s defeat in the midterms. “Its symbolic value outweighs its practical value,” Xu Liping, a senior scholar at Guangdong University of Foreign Studies, told Shanghai Observer, a state-run outlet.

After Manila, Mr. Trump will travel to the Chinese tech hub of Shenzhen, where he will meet Mr. Xi on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation.

Jensen Huang, a key adviser who accompanied Mr. Trump on his last trip to China, will not be by his side. Mr. Huang helped shape the president’s views about technological competition with China and persuaded him to loosen restrictions on selling advanced chips to China.

Mr. Huang stepped down from his role at Nvidia after revelations that he had an affair with a Chinese businesswoman, Alice Huang, who U.S. intelligence officials believe was collecting intelligence on Mr. Huang for Beijing. China has rejected the allegations, calling them “smears.” Ms. Huang has not responded to requests for comment.

So far, the president has not raised the issue with Mr. Xi and is not expected to during their meeting on Thursday. But the saga is likely to cause unease in China about whether Washington will impose tighter controls on the export of technology to the country.

As a result, U.S.-based analysts speculated that Mr. Xi might try to use his personal relationship with Mr. Trump to forestall such restrictions.

Yun Sun, director of the China program at the Stimson Center in Washington, said Mr. Huang’s downfall would likely mean “there is going to be more anxiety on the Chinese side about their access to American chips.” She expects Mr. Xi to emphasize the importance of easing U.S. controls on chip exports to China during their meeting.

Cooperation between China and the United States on artificial intelligence may be more difficult after the Nvidia scandal.

Still, there may be progress on the creation of a notification system that would allow the countries to alert each other about national security issues related to artificial intelligence, which the two sides agreed to in September.

“There’s some shared concern on both sides that U.S. or Chinese models might be misused by bad actors and cause damage to the U.S., China, or other countries,” said Kyle Chan, a fellow at the Brookings Institution focusing on China’s technology development.

When Mr. Xi sits down with Mr. Trump in Shenzhen, he will be dealing with a president who will soon face a new constraint at home: a Democrat-controlled Congress.

Lawmakers may pressure Mr. Trump to tighten export controls against China or accelerate weapons sales to Taiwan, according to Ali Wyne, a senior research and advocacy adviser focusing on U.S.-China relations at the International Crisis Group. That means that “Xi will have to work much harder to keep Trump on side,” Mr. Wyne said.

Mr. Trump will be the first American president to visit China twice in one calendar year.

Analysts say the leaders’ summits, which have included state visits in Beijing and then in Washington, have been the only thing holding together a fragile détente between the two countries as they clash over A.I., export controls, Taiwan and other issues.

The two sides declared a trade truce last year when they met in Busan, South Korea. That deal, which was to expire this month, has been extended until January.

China’s restrictions on the export of crucial rare-earth metals and rare-earth magnets, which are needed to manufacture most modern technology, remain one of the most difficult unresolved issues.

Mr. Trump is likely to raise this with Mr. Xi, according to a senior U.S. administration official who said that China’s continued restrictions is one reason Washington only agreed to a two-month extension of the trade truce.

Ana Swanson contributed reporting from Washington, Aie Balagtas See from Manila, and Laura Chung from Sydney, Australia.

#101. Brexit work travel rules need urgent improvement, say UK business leaders

CBI says restrictions on cross-Channel work trips are hindering investment and trade between UK and Europe

Business chiefs have called on the UK government and the European Commission to urgently ease Brexit restrictions on business travel for cross-Channel work trips.

A group representing industry bosses said it wants commitments from Andy Burnham and European political leaders that the restrictions will be one of the priorities in 2027 for furthering ties between the two sides, after a summit in Brussels this week.

The Confederation of British Industry (CBI), which represents some of Britain’s largest companies, welcomed the prime minister’s recent calls for a reset with Europe, designed to reverse some of the damage to Britain’s economy caused by Brexit.

But it said Brexit restrictions, which include making employees obtain work permits for travel, are deterring cross-border investors and hampering trade at the very time both sides are trying to build resilience against China.

Sean McGuire, the CBI’s director of Europe and international, said discussions should have been a priority in the talks this week, after a UK-EU summit in 2025 held by Keir Starmer also referenced improving business mobility.

“Very little has been achieved. In fact, practically nothing has been achieved. The emphasis was put on youth mobility,” said McGuire.

EU diplomats have said in the past that the UK pushed hard for improving business mobility but was met with resistance from the bloc, where member states see British services – a big export particularly in the financial sector – as a threat.

McGuire said that energy, defence and car industry bosses say they are being hampered by the inability to travel freely in Europe to service or run multinational operations.

He said the issue should be of interest to all sides as they build on multiple existing laws to protect European supply chains.

“Now that we’ve got youth mobility potentially in the Friday deal, we should move seriously to address the kind of business mobility, which would apply to both the goods sector and the services sector,” he said.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

British citizens travelling to the EU post-Brexit are restricted to 90 days at a time out of a maximum of 180 days a year, limiting companies’ ability to promote employees and travel.

“If you are a CEO of a multinational you can very quickly clock up 90 days,” McGuire said.

Separately, work permits have to be obtained for each country that a worker travels to. That can affect the servicing of equipment, particularly in engineering, where products that cost millions such as lifts, escalators and factory machines come with service and maintenance contracts.

“There is a big difference between irregular migration, which needs to be tackled, and business mobility, which is bringing investment into the UK and vice versa,” he said.

While an agreement is close on youth mobility, Burnham faces the same roadblock when it comes to opportunities for under-30s, who will be offered a three-year visa scheme to work, travel or study.

“It is a trick because the UK is negotiating with Brussels but work permits are a national competency,” said one EU diplomat.

But McGuire said there is a quick fix for business mobility, involving changes to the annex of the EU-UK trade and cooperation agreement, which allows the two sides to expand visa-free business travel across more industries without reworking the main treaty.

The CBI said it is also “disappointed” that no progress has been made on mutual recognition of qualifications allowing British or EU professionals to practise in each other’s territories as they did before Brexit.

Sources said there was a view in Brussels that mutual recognition of qualifications was more beneficial to the UK than to the EU, causing energy around the talks to drain away.
