#100. How new US chip restrictions on South East Asia will hit China

Xi and Trump when they met in Beijing in May

As US President Donald Trump heads to China, his administration has renewed efforts to curb Beijing's access to advanced semiconductors.

On Monday, Washington announced new interim rules that will increase scrutiny of data centre operators in Singapore, Malaysia, Thailand, Indonesia and Brazil.

The move not only restricts the export of advanced chips, but also access to their computing power, which Chinese firms currently rent abroad to train artificial intelligence (AI) models.

South East Asian countries in particular have invested heavily in data centres to power their AI ambitions - and Chinese firms seeking computing power overseas have been a key market.

But now these firms will need a US government licence to buy advanced chips from Nvidia or other American chipmakers. This allows Washington to monitor if those chips are being used to serve Chinese companies, or being smuggled to China.

This poses a fresh challenge for Beijing, which has become deeply dependent on compute from abroad in the wake of US export restrictions. At least 30% of China's advanced AI computing power is projected to come from renting overseas or smuggling in 2027, according to research by the Institute for Progress, external, a US-based think-tank.

China's foreign ministry called the new rules "economic coercion" and said Beijing "will take necessary measures to firmly safeguard its own legitimate rights and interests".

This is also a problem for South East Asian economies where large numbers of data centres have sprung up recently. Some of the firms behind them are accused of helping China evade US export controls on advanced chips.

One of those is Singaporean data centre firm Megaspeed, whose former director Alice Huang is suspected of being a Chinese spy. Calls for tighter export controls have grown after Nvidia's Jensen Huang admited to an affair with her and resigned as CEO.

Megaspeed, the biggest buyer of Nvidia's chips in South East Asia, is accused of illegally smuggling chips to Chinese companies after purchasing them for use in data centres in Malaysia, Indonesia and Thailand.

The company has denied these allegations, defended its business as "legitimate and lawful", and said it has "retained external legal counsel to conduct a thorough internal investigation". It also said Alice Huang stepped down from her role as director in late 2023 and left the firm last year.

The US Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security, however, has said Brazil and the four South East Asian nations "present a higher-than-normal risk of diversion" of the chips that Washington wants to keep out of China's hands.

The new measures are aimed at ensuring sensitive US technology "cannot be circumvented through remote computing arrangements involving data centers in third countries," the bureau added. Licences will be issued on a "case-by-case basis", it said.

Batam in Indonesia is emerging as a major regional data centre hub

Countries like Malaysia and Thailand whose data centre roll out has been heavily driven by Chinese buyers could be hit hardest, says international trade expert Naoise McDonagh from Australia's Edith Cowan University.

But the new rules are unlikely to derail the data centre boom as a whole, says Reema Bhattacharya from advisory firm Verisk Maplecroft, adding that investment backed by major tech firms and clearly identifiable customers should "remain relatively resilient".

Singapore was an early adopter of data centres but when it began limiting new construction, investment flowed to neighbours Malaysia and Thailand. Johor in Malaysia and Batam in Indonesia have also approved billions of dollars worth of data centres to catch the spillover demand.

These countries have been offering incentives such as cheaper land to attract foreign investment, including from Chinese tech giants Alibaba and Tencent.

But now, the region could find it harder to get hold of advanced chips.

The BBC has contacted Indonesia, Malaysia and Thailand for comment.

Singapore said it is "engaging relevant US authorities" to understand the new rules better, and is "exploring how to facilitate trusted access" for local firms.

A spokesperson for Brazil's Ministry of Development, Industry, Trade and Services said it is aware of the US announcement and it "is closely monitoring any potential developments regarding this measure".

Malaysia previously said it welcomes data centre investment but "will not allow itself to be used to get around export controls".

The new rules also come just ahead of Trump's meeting with Chinese leader Xi Jinping in Shenzhen this week.

Beijing has made self-sufficiency in tech a national priority and needs the best chips to build powerful AI models.

Chinese firms DeepSeek and MoonShot have come close to US rivals like Anthropic and OpenAI. So much so that The Institute for Progress estimates top US AI models lead the best Chinese platforms by only about six and half months.

"This is despite US AI companies having significantly better compute access and financing," it said.

Still, China needs advanced US chips to ensure its best AI models are competitive, experts tell the BBC.

The new restrictions on South East Asia and Brazil have taken away "one of the more practical routes Chinese AI developers had to accessing frontier computing power," Bhattacharya said.

But tighter restrictions could also push Chinese firms towards domestic semiconductors and further drive advancement at home.

That has been a longstanding dilemma for Washington, McDonagh said: whether to keep selling chips to China to retain its reliance on the US, or restrict access and risk further spurring Chinese innovation.

#99. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#98. Who to watch in Spain's election race

Pedro SÃ¡nchez (left), Alberto NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o (centre) and Santiago Abascal (right)

Spanish voters face a snap election on 29 November, with the conservatives and the far right favourites to win after eight years of Socialist-led rule under Pedro SÃ¡nchez.

The left had hoped to focus on Spain's housing crisis, but the government has been buffeted by corruption scandals, a migrant crisis, and most recently rail disasters that left 115 people dead. Here are the key figures to look out for in the race to run Spain's next government.

FeijÃ³o's People's Party (PP) tops most of the polls and as leader of the conservative opposition since 2022 he is favourite to become Spain's next prime minister.

His pitch to voters is his experience and reputation as a capable administrator, with 13 years as president of the north-west Galicia region from 2009.

"It's called management: and it's what I know how to do, it's what I want to do, and it's the change Spain needs," he has told voters.

FeijÃ³o has held public office continuously since 1991, and although he guided the PP to win the biggest number of seats in parliament in 2023, they fell short of an outright parliamentary majority. SÃ¡nchez's Socialist PSOE stayed in power through deals with left-wing parties and the backing of Catalan and Basque nationalist parties and pro-independence groups.

FeijÃ³o says the best outcome for Spain would be a PP majority governing alone, with support in parliament from far-right party Vox. His party has worked in coalition with Vox in four Spanish regions, and he has not ruled out a national coalition under strict conditions and red lines.

Unlike SÃ¡nchez, the PP leader is not well known internationally and his lack of English may hold him back on the world stage. FeijÃ³o admits to making little effort to learn English but believes the "enormous problems" faced by Spaniards are of greater importance: "I don't know English, but I know how to govern."

SÃ¡nchez has led the Socialists for the past 12 years, and Spain since 2018.

He has built a reputation as a political survivor but this election - his sixth in 11 years - could be his toughest yet.

SÃ¡nchez called the snap election eight months early, seeking to focus on resolving Spain's housing crisis, and vowing "an epic comeback of a progressive Spain" at the ballot box.

But he and his party have been embroiled in a series of corruption scandals, a migrant crisis in Spain's Ceuta exclave on the Moroccan border and two deadly rail disasters nine months apart. The most recent tragedy claimed 69 lives at Urda on 28 October and overshadowed the start of the campaign.

The Socialist leader has been written off in politics before, only to stage comebacks, notably in 2017 and 2023.

He can point to a projected 2.6% annual growth in the Spanish economy in 2026, fuelled by tourism, but the graft cases against his inner circle and family are not going away.

JosÃ© Luis Ãbalos, who was transport minister in SÃ¡nchez's government, is now serving a 24-year prison sentence for accepting kickbacks, meanwhile leading party figure Santos CerdÃ¡n awaits trial for alleged bribery and denies wrongdoing.

SÃ¡nchez's wife, BegoÃ±a GÃ³mez, faces trial over allegations of influence peddling and embezzlement while his brother, David SÃ¡nchez, has been banned from public office for nine years for misconduct. Both deny doing anything wrong.

In a defiant message, BegoÃ±a GÃ³mez made her first party speech at the Socialist campaign launch, calling for the backing of Spanish women, "because when women step forward, all of Spain follows".

Spain PM pins hopes on housing crisis to help win snap election

Clashes erupt in Barcelona after thousands join Spain general strike

SÃ¡nchez's fate could be sealed by the rise of Abascal's anti-immigration, populist Vox - with opinion polls putting them in a comfortable third place and taking votes from the Socialists.

Abascal, 50, denies his party is far-right and has succeeded in bringing Vox into the mainstream, attracting record ratings on one of Spanish state TV's primetime entertainment shows during the campaign.

Repatriating migrants "in an illegal situation" and "remigration" of foreigners who do not contribute to Spain are big themes for Abascal, who has accused Pedro SÃ¡nchez of promoting an "act of invasion" with his policies.

He has promised to use the Spanish navy to defend the national borders and "send a message to the world that there is no room here for all of Africa and all of the Americas".

The Vox leader joined the PP aged 18, becoming a local councillor first and then an MP in the Basque regional parliament.

His political trajectory was closely shaped by his family's experience of threats and intimidation by Basque militant group Eta. Both his father and grandfather, who held public office, were targeted because of their political roles.

He left the conservatives in 2013, founding Vox the following year and becoming a Spanish MP in 2019, when Vox became the first hard-right party in decades to establish a significant presence in Congress.

Vox has adopted the Trump-inspired slogan "Make Spain Great Again" and advocates a strongly centralised vision of the country strongly opposed to the independence movement in Catalonia.

Critics have described the party's ideology as reminiscent of aspects of the era of dictator Francisco Franco era, which Vox rejects.

Former Barcelona mayor Ada Colau heads a left-wing coalition of four small left-wing parties called Frente Amplio (Broad Front) - which has lost many of its supporters in recent weeks to the Socialists and is currently polling in fourth place.

Previously called Sumar, the coalition was in government with the Socialists under leader Yolanda DÃ­az, but she decided she would not run again. Colau came into politics as a housing activist and fair rents and housing is one of her coalition's big policies.

Irene Montero of Podemos and the pro-Catalan independence ERC party of Gabriel RufiÃ¡n have combined forces in a bid to gain more seats

Other parties on the left are contesting the election separately, including the pro-Catalan independence Esquerra Republicana de Catalunya (ERC) under Gabriel RufiÃ¡n who has reached a campaign deal with Podemos under Irene Montero.

Spanish pensioner whose eviction sparked nationwide protests dies

Catalan centre-right pro-independence party Junts per Catalunya played a pivotal role in supporting Pedro SÃ¡nchez's government, even though it had just seven seats in the 350-seat Congress, and it may be central to post-election negotiations too. It was Junts' decision to reject the government's housing proposals last month that pushed the Socialist leader into snap elections.

Its lead candidate is MÃ­riam Nogueras, who has made clear that Catalan demands come first for her party. Junts president Carles Puigdemont, 63, is planning to return to Spain after the election. He has lived in self-imposed exile since 2017 after leading Catalonia's failed bid for independence.

Analysts see Madrid's regional leader Isabel DÃ­az Ayuso as a possible candidate for prime minister in the future

Isabel DÃ­az Ayuso, 48, has been president of the Madrid region since 2019 and has become one of the most influential figures within the People's Party. She leads one of the PP's strongest electoral bastions, with conservatives having ruled the region for the past 31 years.

Ayuso has built a distinct political profile that often sets her apart from the party establishment. Her direct communication style and informal use of language have helped her connect with voters and attract significant media attention.

Her tenure has not been without controversy. Her partner has been the subject of a judicial investigation relating to alleged tax offences and document forgery, allegations he denies.

Opposition parties have also questioned the Madrid regional government's purchase of a luxury penthouse for official use. Critics have challenged the rationale behind the acquisition, while regional authorities have maintained that it was intended as a government workspace.

Socialist Carlos Cuerpo, 46, was chosen by Pedro SÃ¡nchez to take charge as interim transport minister after the Urda rail disaster and has become key to his election team. Cuerpo climbed the ranks through the economy ministry and became minister of economy, trade and business in 2023.

He has been ranked as best-regarded politician among the Spanish public, according to the Centre for Sociological Research (CIS), and, when asked about his political ambitions has called himself "a team player, and I am committed to the team".

In the same light, SÃ¡nchez has said: "I believe politics is a team sport; on the opposing team we have FeijÃ³o and Abascal, and on my team we have Carlos Cuerpo."

Catalonia's Socialist president Salvador Illa is also one to watch. He served as Spain's health minister during the Covid pandemic and some have viewed him as a potential successor as Socialist leader, even though he has backed SÃ¡nchez to remain as leader.

A close ally to SÃ¡nchez, Salvador Illa (L) is often named as possible successor

Carlos HernÃ¡ndez Quero, 30, has been a Vox MP since 2023, and speaks for the party on housing and urban affairs, taking charge of one of the most important election issues.

He is not regarded as the party's second-in-command, a position more commonly associated with figures such as Montserrat LluÃ­s or Ignacio Garriga, but he is media-savvy and known for his clear, combative defence of Vox's economic policies and proposals.

Further to the right is Alvise PÃ©rez, 36, a Member of the European Parliament who leads Se AcabÃ³ la Fiesta (The party is over). His party won three seats in European elections in 2024 and attracts voters disappointed with Vox. His party adopts a hard line on immigration, corruption and the EU.

#97. Paul Kelly pleads for Albanese to ‘show some backbone’ over AI as creatives campaign for copyright protections

Music legend is joined by Jimmy Barnes and Peter Garrett in backing Not AI’s To Take campaign ahead of Aria awards

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Australia’s creative community has launched a major campaign pleading with the Albanese government not to water down copyright law amid furious lobbying from AI companies, ahead of Wednesday night’s Aria awards.

Music legends Jimmy Barnes, Paul Kelly and Peter Garrett are among those backing the Not AI’s To Take campaign, supported by dozens of creative groups representing the recording, writing, publishing, film, television and media industries.

While the government maintains it will not undermine copyright law, the industry minister, Tim Ayres, said on Tuesday “the status quo is no good” for the creative sector, ahead of a package of AI legislation expected by early 2027.

But creatives have pushed back on such claims, arguing copyright law doesn’t need updating – just better enforcement, and that changes could put the $70bn creative industry and its 600,000 workers at risk.

“[AI platforms] haven’t made one phone call yet. They haven’t called us. They haven’t called anybody,” said Dean Ormston, CEO of music rights organisation Apra Amcos.

“There’s nothing wrong with the copyright framework. The problem is getting somebody to sit at the table and have a discussion with us.”

Not AI’s To Take is backed by the likes of Aria, the Copyright Agency, the Australian Directors’ Guild, Screen Producers Australia, the Australian Society of Authors, the Australian Writers’ Guild, and media organisations including Foxtel, Free TV, Nine, Seven and Guardian Australia.

Australian musicians including Montaigne and KLP have backed the campaign online, while Guardian Australia understands Barnes, Kelly and Garrett – some of Anthony Albanese’s favourite musicians – will also feature.

“Our government has been a huge supporter of Australian music and creativity, and now we’re asking them to continue that support by standing firm on copyright and making sure big tech doesn’t get to rewrite the rules at the expense of creators,” said KLP, real name Kristy Lee Peters.

In quotes shared on the campaign’s website, Barnes said: “I think that these companies should go and f..k themselves.”

Kelly is quoted as saying: “Australia has had some of the best copyright laws, something to be proud of. Now is the time for our government to show some backbone.”

Although Albanese has declared it would be “theft” if creatives weren’t paid for their work, some AI companies have argued it is too difficult to make individual licensing deals under existing copyright law. AI companies have said they need legal certainty on the copyright issue before investing and training models in Australia, with Anthropic putting forward an “opt-out” model to scrape creative works unless a rightsholder objected.

However Anthropic told a Senate committee it would “provide ongoing access to frontier models to eligible Australian entities, regardless of whether training is permitted.”

The government is expected to unveil a long-awaited legislative package on AI in late 2026 or early 2027, with proposals around datacentres, online safety, productivity and copyright. Albanese said last week that artists “need to be compensated if their work is used”. But the creative community is concerned that lobbying from big tech may sway government decisions.

“This isn’t a smear campaign. We’re just making some noise out in the open, where tech companies are doing it in the shadows,” said Patrizia Di Biase-Dyson, CEO of the Australian Publishers Association.

“Everything is at stake. We have to do as much as possible to educate everyone.”

Ormston said creative industries welcomed the investment and opportunities of AI, and had applauded both Labor’s rebuffing of a text and data mining exemption and Albanese’s public support. But he said artists were worried about potential changes.

“Our very clear message to the government has been: hold firm. You are absolutely saying the right thing. You are absolutely right in the fact that while we want AI in this country, we want it on Australia’s terms,” Ormston said.

Asked about the campaign on Tuesday, Ayres said “nothing has changed in terms of the government’s approach” to copyright.

“I understand the anxiety, though. For those workers, the status quo is no good for them. Their work is currently being appropriated, today, offshore. We have set out a program for a scheme of legislation that would protect those rights, make sure that artificial intelligence investment in Australia is on Australia’s terms.

“We’re not about undermining copyright here. That commitment has been made so many times now.”

A spokesperson for the attorney general, Michelle Rowland, said the government was “committed to supporting” creatives, and pledged any copyright changes would “deliver meaningful control and fair compensation”.

But independent senator David Pocock urged caution around any moves, warning that copyright changes could create a legal “loophole” to make Australia a global “data laundering hotspot”.

“There are more than 100 court cases on foot involving copyright and AI training in the US and we should wait for these to play out before making any changes given the risks of getting it wrong are so high,” he said.

#95. As Trump and Xi meet, China's AI dreams face an unexpected hurdle: the Nvidia spy scandal

Jensen Huang is out but his shadow looms over the upcoming meeting between US President Donald Trump and his Chinese counterpart Xi Jinping this week.

For Beijing, the former Nvidia CEO's dramatic fall is a major challenge - and not just because of claims that a Chinese spy may have honey-trapped Huang, one of the artificial intelligence industry's most influential figures.

Rather, the allegations surrounding Huang and his affair with Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang are turning into a new flashpoint in an already fragile US-China relationship.

US intelligence reports claim she had ties to Beijing's intelligence apparatus - in particular the Ministry of State Security, China's main civilian spy agency.

The claim remains unproven, but it has already reignited debate in Washington over exports of advanced chips to China and whether the Trump administration has been too willing to ease restrictions.

For years now, advanced computer chips have been a key battleground in the US-China rivalry because they are essential for AI, supercomputing and military technology.

Chinese officials are worried this scandal could strengthen voices in Washington who argue in favour of further limiting Chinese access to cutting-edge American tech.

Just this week, the Trump administration introduced new export restrictions on Chinese firms that access advanced US chips in third countries such as Malaysia, Thailand or Brazil. They typically do this by renting computing power in data centres abroad to train their AI models. But some of the firms, like the one Alice Huang worked for, are also accused of smuggling chips to China, although they have denied this.

Until now, this controversy appeared to be a sideshow in US-China relations. But with Beijing calling the new rules "economic coercion" on the eve of Trump arriving in Shenzhen, there is a growing risk it could become something much bigger.

Watch: Jensen Huang speaks to the BBC's Faisal Islam in 2025

Democrats, who will control Congress come January, have made their position clear. Last Wednesday top Democrat Ted Lieu told Politico the Trump administration had been "1,000%" too deferential to Nvidia, putting national security at risk, while fellow Democrat Zoe Lofgren suggested Congress would now be more likely to restrict sales of AI chips to China.

The BBC has approached the White House for comment.

Beijing's foreign ministry had described the spy claims as a "smear against China".

"The US should stop fabricating so-called espionage narratives to find pretexts for imposing technological blockades," spokesperson Mao Ning said last week.

The ministry also seemed keen to keep the meeting on track, saying the two countries had kept "close communication regarding interactions between the two heads of state".

For the last year, Trump and Xi have been seeking to stabilise relations after a damaging tariff war that almost brought trade between the two countries to a standstill.

The Trump administration's decision to permit sales of advanced Nvidia H200 chips to China in late 2025 was seen as evidence that both sides were searching for areas of co-operation, even while competition intensified.

It was a rare move given Washington had spent years trying to limit China's access to the most advanced chips.

Many in the US have long viewed these chips as a national security issue, arguing they could strengthen China's military and surveillance capabilities. Jensen Huang, who rejects that claim, advocated lifting bans on the export of high-performing chips to China. Beijing argues the restrictions are an attempt to contain its rise.

History has taught Beijing and Washington that spy scandals rarely remain confined to the intelligence world.

Analysts in China already see echoes of an incident in 2023 when a suspected surveillance balloon drifted through the skies over the US before an American fighter jet shot it down. Beijing insisted it was a civilian meteorological airship that had strayed off course.

The suspected Chinese spy balloon was shot down days after it was spotted over the US

But a planned trip by then US Secretary of State Antony Blinken was cancelled and communication between the two governments deteriorated sharply.

The incident showed how quickly a security dispute could overwhelm diplomatic efforts and push relations into a deep freeze.

"The mere passage of a balloon sent American public opinion and politicians into a state of high alert, as if a "Chinese threat" were looming directly overhead," said the state media outlet, the Global Times this week as it made a direct comparison with the Nvidia scandal.

"No-one on the US side has ever apologised or taken responsibility for the genuine damage this caused to US-China relations," it added.

The state-run outlet also questioned why the Nvidia story had emerged so soon after "the hard-won detente" between Beijing and Washington, following a meeting between the two countries' leaders in September.

"Is someone behind the scenes pushing an agenda and stirring up confrontation?" it asked.

Both Trump and Xi have significant incentives to keep talking.

China's economy remains under pressure and is increasingly reliant on exports. Xi needs trade to keep flowing and this meeting is a key stepping stone to replacing the current trade truce that is due to expire in January.

Meanwhile Trump has repeatedly touted his close relationship with Xi and argued that maintaining dialogue is essential to managing competition between the two powers.

The US president is in his last two years and faces an uphill struggle to pass domestic legislation after the midterms. To counter this, he could be looking for some foreign policy wins.

He may also hope for Xi's help to end the war in the Middle East.

Neither side will want to be discussing intelligence concerns and technology controls instead of trade, investment and economic co-operation.

The summit is also being carefully choreographed by Beijing to show off its technological prowess in Shenzhen - a deeply symbolic choice of city.

It's home to some of the country's biggest tech companies and has become a showcase for Beijing's ambitions in AI and advanced manufacturing.

Also, given Washington's tighter restrictions on advanced chips, Beijing has options for retaliation.

One of its most powerful tools is its dominance of key rare earth minerals. China processes much of the world's supply for materials that are used in everything from electric vehicles and smartphones to military equipment.

Analysts have long warned that technological restrictions can trigger a cycle of retaliation in which each side reaches for its strongest economic leverage.

That would shift the current trend of US-China relations from engagement back towards mutual suspicion.

#93. ‘Godmother’ of illegal Israeli West Bank settler project Daniella Weiss among group sanctioned by Australia

Foreign affairs minister Penny Wong says rapid expansion of settlements and settler violence are ‘extinguishing the possibility of a two-state solution’

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Australia has announced another tranche of sanctions on individuals and organisations involved in “illegal settlement expansion and human rights abuses against Palestinians” in the West Bank.

In a statement, the foreign affairs minister, Penny Wong, said it was the first time Australian sanctions had targeted a construction company involved in the building of settlements.

The sanctions prohibit Australians and Australian businesses from trading with or providing financial or other support to three individuals and four organisations.

“We are taking these actions because the rapid expansion of settlements and settler violence are extinguishing the possibility of a two-state solution,” Wong’s statement said.

“This includes the E1 settlement where demolitions and the displacement of Palestinians have occurred in recent weeks. Israeli settlements in the West Bank are illegal under international law.”

Among those sanctioned is Daniella Weiss and her Israeli settler organisation, the Nachala movement, which the government said has provided support for outposts and settlements that have perpetrated serious human rights abuses.

Weiss is known as the “godmother” of the settler movement and was sanctioned by the UK in May 2025, shortly after she appeared on a Louis Theroux’s documentary The Settlers.

Prominent West Bank settler Eliav Libi has also been sanctioned, as well as the Eyal Hari Yehuda construction company which has helped develop settlements and outposts and demolished Palestinian structures in the West Bank.

Wong said the sanctioned individuals and organisations had “actively participated in the planning, coordination and establishment of new settlements, including raising funds, recruitment of volunteers and providing support to violent settlers”.

Speaking on ABC radio on Wednesday morning, Wong said Australia had been coordinating with the UK on the sanctions.

“We thought it was very important to work with the United Kingdom to have a coordinated set of sanctions ahead of those decisions.

“We urge Israel to desist from proceeding with this settlement, which renders likely unviable the prospect of a Palestinian state.”

Wong said she had spoken to her Israeli counterpart, Gideon Sa’ar, on Tuesday night when she “outlined” Australia’s position.

“We’ve made clear we will continue to take action in respect of those involved in the settlement enterprise. The settlement enterprise is violent.”

Wong had signalled further sanctions in September, after the government ruled out an import ban on trade from illegal settlements in the West Bank.

On Wednesday, the foreign affairs minister said the government was still considering further action, but provided no detail.

“We’ll continue to look at targeted measures we can take to push back against what the current Israeli government is doing,” Wong told journalists.

When asked whether Wong was concerned Israel would respond to Australia sanctions with countermeasures, the minister said it had in the past.

“We’ve seen the Israeli government take action against Australia, including asking our diplomats to leave Ramallah. We’ve also seen the caretaker prime minister, Netanyahu, be very public in his criticism of Australia.

“So obviously, there is a reaction to Australia acting, but we think this is the right thing to do.”

The statement warned Australian businesses they must not bid for construction tenders for illegal settlement developments.

Individuals breaching sanctions can attract a penalty of up to 10 years’ imprisonment and a fine of up to $910,000 or three times the value of the relevant transaction.

Businesses can be fined up to $3.64m or three times the value of the relevant transaction.

#83. ‘Heads should roll’: owner of Melbourne Cup horse found dumped in skip bin demands investigation

Stuart Ramsey says it is a ‘bloody disgrace’ the gelding was left in the skip bin while awaiting burial

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

So You Are’s owner-breeder has demanded a full investigation after the Melbourne Cup runner’s body was found in a skip at a Sydney tip, insisting “heads have got to roll”.

Stuart Ramsey says it is a “bloody disgrace” the gelding was left in the skip bin while awaiting burial at Sydney’s Cleanaway Lucas Heights Resource Recovery Centre.

The grisly sight was uncovered by a Sydney man, with online footage outraging industry participants and anti-horse racing activists alike.

New South Wales premier Chris Minns said on Wednesday the incident “obviously needs to be investigated” but “we expect to get to the bottom of it”.

“We do need better ways of disposing of horses,” Minns said.

Bjorn Baker’s promising stayer took ill at a spelling farm after finishing eighth in Australia’s most famous race on 3 November.

He was rushed to the REC Equine Specialists veterinary clinic at Horsley Park in Sydney’s west after showing signs of colic on Monday morning.

Surgery could not save the horse and he was euthanised under anaesthesia.

Racing NSW said its ongoing investigations found So You Are was being held temporarily awaiting burial when the footage was recorded.

It is common practice for vets to dispose of equine remains at landfill sites licensed to deal with horse carcasses, and is recommended by the NSW Department of Primary Industries in most cases.

For safe disposal, the carcass should be covered during transportation and buried under at least two metres of soil and a safe distance from water sources. The drugs used for euthanasia are slow to break down and poisonous to other animals, and can persist in the tissue of the dead animal for years and leach into the surrounding soil.

While the equine veterinary clinic has apologised, renowned breeder Ramsey of boutique Hunter Valley farm Turangga Stud, wants answers and accountability over the disposal of the five-year-old, who earned $1.4m from eight career wins.

“There should be a full investigation. Heads have got to roll – simple as that,” Ramsey said.

“He was more than just a racehorse. He brought our family together, and to think of the stress the family are under now because of this – it’s a bloody disgrace.

“We took 30-odd people down to Melbourne for the race. So You Are brought us all together, as only a horse can do.

REC’s managing partner, Jonathan Lumsden, said the centre had spoken with So You Are’s owners and trainers to apologise.

He pointed out the veterinary practice did not carry out the burial but took “full responsibility” for choosing the service provider and arrangements.

“He should have been promptly and respectfully buried,” Lumsden said in a statement.

Ramsey, who said he hadn’t been contacted by REC, isn’t satisfied with their explanation.

“So they should take responsibility. It wasn’t the contractor’s fault; it was their fault. They were in charge of the horse,” he said.

“They didn’t even give us the option of burying him up at Turangga … they should all be out – gone.

“I don’t mean everyone, but the bosses and the people who’ve made the decisions.”

Ramsey declared racehorses among the best-looked-after animals in the world and spoke of his affinity for So You Are.

A review into a similar incident in New Zealand, where a horse’s body was dumped at a tip, led to the establishment of a dedicated equine crematorium run by a charitable trust, which opened in 2025.

The treatment of So You Are’s remains has sparked strong criticism from animal welfare groups.

“There is always the option to bury the horse or have the horse cremated,” the Coalition for the Protection of Racehorses campaign director, Elio Celotto, said.

“However, it is more expedient to dump the horse at a local tip. This is business as usual for an industry where profit is always more important than sentiment.”

Victorian Animal Justice party MP Georgie Purcell said the tragic case should force the industry to confront serious questions about duty of care.

“The racing industry breeds these horses and profits from them but fails the most basic obligation to treat them with dignity after death,” she said.

Racing NSW said “appropriate action” would be taken if any wrongdoing was discovered in its ongoing investigation.

“Under NSW Department of Primary Industries guidelines, a licensed landfill is the preferred option for animals who have died via euthanasia due to the chemicals involved in the process,” it said.

“That burial, however, should have occurred in a timely and respectful manner.”

Baker said he was also left deeply distressed by the handling of the much-loved horse’s remains, adding they deserved to be treated with dignity and respect.

#77. David Jones: what went wrong and what happens next? Five takeaways from first creditors meeting

Upmarket retailer, which recorded a net loss of $228m last financial year, has total debt over $400m including $11m owed to staff

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Upmarket retailer David Jones owed hundreds of millions of dollars when it collapsed into administration, after accelerating financial losses and reputational damage threatened its ability to stock shelves for Christmas.

About 500 creditors attended its first meeting on Tuesday, where administrators McGrathNicol started to break down who is owed what, when those left out of pocket will know where they stand, and what ultimately went wrong.

David Jones collapsed early this month owing in excess of $400m to an assortment of creditors, including $180m of debts to trade creditors, mainly consisting of the brands that stocked its shelves.

There are also outstanding employee entitlements, such as annual and long-service leave, worth about $11m, and about $33m in unredeemed gift cards, which the retailer is still accepting.

The rest is owed to large secured creditors, led by the retailer’s main financier, Hilco, which has a controlling hand over the current process and even has its own representatives in the 19 stores marked for closure.

Taken together, the debts are well over $400m, but that total is almost certain to climb as landlords tally up their losses.

On top of unpaid rent up to the appointment of administrators, shopping centre owners will also claim for broken leases after the chain’s receivers moved to close half of its 38-store network.

What, if any, money suppliers get back hinges on whether a buyer steps in and how much they are willing to pay, or whether the chain ends up liquidated, as well as how much cash David Jones can bring in through the crucial Christmas trading period.

Because product suppliers sit near the bottom of the creditor priority list, they won’t receive any money until secured lenders and employees are paid out.

Given the size of the outstanding debts, and poor financial state of the department chain, many suppliers are bracing for a near total loss of outstanding amounts.

Shaun Fraser, a founding partner at McGrathNicol, told creditors there were “too many variables” to indicate how much, if any, suppliers would receive.

“We haven’t yet got to a point where we’re engaged enough with bidders to have a sense of what value might be there,” Fraser said.

“We haven’t reached a point in the sale process where people are talking value or price.”

Store closures mean at least 1,689 jobs will go, plus those from brands with in-store concessions, the Shop, Distributive and Allied Employees Association (SDA) said Tuesday.

Bernie Smith, the SDA national vice-president, said staff were stressed and did not know when they would be paid their entitlements. Only 170 staff were set to be redeployed, with offers to go out by Monday, he said.

Out-of-pocket suppliers are particularly frustrated, though the modern practice of holding meetings online kept any real anger contained behind muted microphones.

There were questions over whether there had been preferential treatment to some suppliers, and if the retailer had been trading insolvent before the appointment of administrators.

McGrathNicol said it would investigate when the company might have been insolvent, and if preferential payments were made.

One brand representative said they had also repeatedly asked for stock lists and weekly sales figures without response.

The administrators provided updated financials at the meeting that showed the poor state of affairs at David Jones.

The 188-year-old retailer recorded a net loss of $228m in 2025-26, before losing a further $69m in the first four months of the new financial year leading up to the administration.

Creditors were told that the funding pressures it was facing, coupled with reputational damage, was affecting its ability to get stock for the Christmas trading period.

David Jones ultimately got tipped into administration when negotiations with its main landlord, Westfield owner Scentre Group, broke down.

The retailer’s private equity owner, Anchorage Capital, wanted to radically reduce store numbers – something that is now happening during receivership – to rid the network of underperforming suburban stores.

The directors of David Jones listed several causes of the retailer’s problems, including everything from cost-of-living pressures and soft luxury demand to fierce online competition.

Fraser said there was also “clearly a business model problem” with the retailer.

Under a dual appointment, receivers FTI Consulting have taken day-to-day control of the business while administrators McGrathNicol hunt for a buyer, having called for “non-binding indicative offers” for the retailer by the end of the month.

That will trigger a process that could see formal bids lodged either late this year or early in 2027, giving some clarity to employees and suppliers ahead of the next creditors’ meeting, due by March.

The road ahead is, however, complicated by the breakdown in relations between Anchorage and the Westfield owner, which is threatening the retailer’s ability to operate at any of its complexes.

The Scentre Group chief executive, Elliott Rusanow, told Guardian Australia the landlord now had a “unique opportunity to facilitate the entry and growth of businesses that are more relevant to customers today and into the future”.

“That is the opportunity we will pursue at each of the 17 destinations with a David Jones store,” Rusanow said.

#73. Gunmen kill Costa Rican journalist who reported on drugs trade

Police cordoned off the area where the shooting happened

A Costa Rican journalist who denounced alleged links between politicians and drug traffickers in the Central American country has been shot dead.

Police said gunmen on a motorbike ambushed Camilo RodrÃ­guez, 50, as he was travelling in his car north of the capital, San JosÃ©, on Monday afternoon.

The independent reporter earlier said he had been receiving death threats from drug trafficking gangs.

Local media reported that it was the first murder of a journalist in more than two decades in Costa Rica. It comes at a time when transnational crime gangs are increasing their presence in the country, driving up its formerly low homicide rate.

The president of the Costa Rican Journalists' Association, Yanancy Noguera, said that "all evidence so far points to the silencing of a dissenting voice in Costa Rica - a person who published information about organised crime in the country".

RodrÃ­guez had been working as a journalist for several decades, both in radio and in print.

Most recently, he had been uploading videos to social media in which he named public figures such as judges, business people and politicians, which he alleged were corrupt or had ties to known drug traffickers.

In one of his videos, he alleged that the home of Costa Rica's President, Laura FernÃ¡ndez, had been constructed by a company that he said had links to a criminal gang.

FernÃ¡ndez, who categorically denied any wrongdoing, took the journalist to court, accusing him of defamation.

Reacting to his murder in a video message, the president strongly condemned the attack, calling it "a stain on freedom of expression".

She added that she had instructed police to do their utmost to "punish Camilo's murderers".

RodrÃ­guez had also tried to stand for political office, seeking to run as the presidential candidate for the Costa Rican Renovation Party in 2021.

However, his candidacy was rejected by the electoral authorities after the party failed to meet the necessary criteria required to file candidates.

Costa Rica's Attorney General, Carlo DÃ­az, said that "silencing someone who reports on drug trafficking or corruption, in this case a communicator, is a signal we cannot tolerate in a democratic society".

#69. Carney looks to China as Trump tariffs squeeze Canada's car industry

Carney eyes 'other options' for embattled Canadian auto sector on China trip

Canada's car industry has become one of the clearest stress tests for Ottawa as it grapples with US tariffs on the sector.

Now Prime Minister Mark Carney has signalled that he is looking to China, where he is on a visit for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit (Apec), as part of the solution.

But that avenue is not without controversy, and is once again drawing the ire of those close to Donald Trump.

While in Cambodia earlier this week, Carney told reporters that Canada could turn to European and Asian carmakers to save its auto manufacturing sector if a trade deal with the US could not be reached.

If Canadian auto workers are "not fully utilised in a North American context, that provides other opportunities.

"Canada's going to move forward either way," he said.

He went on to meet Chinese President Xi Jinping in China on Tuesday for their second head-to-head in less than a year. The two talked of the importance of developing their relations, and Carney said they had agreed to further tariff cuts.

Neither side mentioned vehicles specifically, though analysts suggest the issue will have been prominent in their minds.

Carney and Xi met on Tuesday to discuss cooperation and trade ahead of this week's Apec summit

In January, Carney said that opening the door to China's electric vehicles, external (EVs) could result in investment into Canadian-owned companies and the creation of manufacturing jobs in the sector.

American tariffs on imports of Canadian-made vehicles have led to thousands of job losses in Canada and anxiety over the future, as 90% of cars made in Canada are shipped to and sold in the US.

At the same time, China has been eyeing Canada as a potential entry-point to the North American EV market. Earlier this year, Canada announced that it would let in 49,000 EVs from China at a low tariff rate, with plans to raise that quota gradually to 70,000 by 2030.

The move was a significant break from the US, which tariffs Chinese EVs heavily to protect domestic manufacturing.

Peter Navarro, Trump's senior counsel for trade and manufacturing, warned the prime minister against cosying up to China in a post on X, external.

"Are u really threatening to play China off the US? This is offensive in the extreme," he wrote on Monday. "You should be far more worried China is going to eat Canada alive."

His comments were reposted by Pete Hoekstra, the US ambassador to Canada.

Canada's international trade minister Maninder Sidhu toured a Chinese EV plant earlier this month

Chinese EV manufacturers like BYD and Chery have already been lobbying the Canadian government for market access.

Last week, Canada's Minister of International Trade Maninder Sidhu toured a Chery plant in China, external. He told reporters afterwards that Canadians could expect to see affordable Chinese EVs in Canada by early 2027.

These moves come as the toll of US tariffs on Canada's auto sector has become harder to ignore.

Stellantis, a multinational car manufacturer that made models like the Dodge Challenger in Canada, recently announced it would sell its assembly plant in Brampton, Ontario.

The facility had been idle since 2023 for a planned retooling, but what was supposed to be a temporary shutdown became permanent after Trump's tariffs.

The sale has led to speculation that a Chinese EV manufacturer could move into the facility, after Canadian media outlet CTV News reported, external, citing anonymous sources, that Stellantis was planning to lease back 25% of its plant to assemble "affordable" EVs.

The idea is unpopular with Ontario Premier Doug Ford. In a letter to Carney, he warned against opening the door for Chinese EV kits to be assembled at the Brampton plant.

China overwhelmingly builds its cars at home and ships them whole - or to be assembled in kits - overseas.

Canada's deal with China signals it is serious about shift from US

How China made electric vehicles mainstream

Lynette Ong, director of the China Governance Lab at the University of Toronto, said introducing Chinese auto manufacturing in Canada would be "controversial" due to fears that Canadian jobs could be lost and that the North American industry could be eroded.

"We want to make sure they're hiring Canadian workers, that they are helping train Canadian workers and that they are transferring the technology over," Ong said.

Carney noted there are "clear guardrails" in how Canada deals with China, including barring work on sensitive matters like artificial intelligence and critical minerals.

Canada has also eyed other partnerships. It is notably pursuing associate member status with the EU, and had signed a free trade deal with the Philippines earlier this week.

Just how much Canada is willing to embrace Chinese manufacturing, however, still depends on the outcome of ongoing US-Canada trade talks.

Progress has been made in negotiations, Carney recently said, but differences remain. It is unclear if Trump and Carney will speak on the sidelines of the Apec summit later this week.

#66. Hugh Jackman, Rebel Wilson and 150 Australian artists join call to block Woodside gas development near Scott Reef

Actors, authors and musicians sign open letter urging Murray Watt to reject the fossil fuel company’s plan to drill for gas in Browse basin off Kimberley coast

Sign up for climate and environment editor Adam Morton’s free Clear Air newsletter here

More than 150 Australian artists, including Hugh Jackman, Rebel Wilson and Jessica Mauboy, have backed a Greenpeace-led campaign against Woodside Energy’s proposal to develop the Browse basin, about 300km off the Kimberley coast.

Woodside, the country’s largest fossil fuel company, describes Browse as “Australia’s biggest undeveloped offshore gas resource”. The oil and gas giant is hoping to win federal government approval to drill more than 50 gas wells about 3km from the Scott Reef area, which is home to more than 1,500 species, including endangered pygmy blue whales.

Actors Jackman, Wilson, Richard Roxburgh and Mia Wasikowska are among prominent Australians who oppose the Browse development on the basis that the proposed gasfield could damage the unique reef ecosystem.

They join musicians Jimmy Barnes, Paul Kelly, Tame Impala’s Kevin Parker, Tina Arena, Jessica Mauboy, G Flip, Vance Joy, Missy Higgins and Baker Boy in signing an open letter calling on the federal environment minister, Murray Watt, not to approve the development.

The letter – which says the Browse project “would turn Scott Reef into an industrial gas zone with decades of deafening seismic blasting [and] carbon dumping beneath the ocean floor” – was also signed by authors JM Coetzee, Helen Garner, Anna Funder and Diana Reid.

Sign up to get climate and environment editor Adam Morton’s Clear Air column as a free newsletter

“Drilling at Scott Reef could cause an oil spill up to 30 times bigger than the 2009 Montara disaster, polluting Scott Reef with oil condensate, impacting the Kimberley coastline and even reaching as far as Indonesia,” it continued.

The open letter comes after a supergroup of more than 20 Australian artists – including King Gizzard & the Lizard Wizard, Barnes, John Butler and Montaigne – last Monday released If Not Now, a musical call to arms about the reef.

The song, a reimagining of King Gizzard’s 2020 track, If Not Now, Then When?, is a collaboration between Greenpeace Australia Pacific, Green Music Australia and p(doom) Records.

Browse, first proposed by Woodside in 2018, would see gas piped about 900km to its ageing North West Shelf gas export plant, which is adjacent to the world heritage-listed Murujuga rock art.

Last month, Guardian Australia revealed that WA’s independent environmental watchdog had sharply criticised the proposal, saying that plans to protect turtles and blue whales near Scott Reef would be ineffective.

A draft letter from the Environmental Protection Authority, written in 2023, advised that it may be impossible to reduce the risk of an oil spill to an acceptable level.

Greenpeace last month nominated Scott Reef for national heritage listing, based on its ecological and scientific values.

Modelling by the Institute for Energy Economics and Financial Analysis suggests that Browse would generate up to 6.8m tonnes of carbon dioxide emissions yearly at peak production, accounting for between 2.9% and 3.7% of Australia’s emissions in 2035.

“Browse gas is likely four times more expensive than existing domestic gas,” the institute found last year, adding that the project “is unlikely to be competitive either domestically or internationally, based on IEEFA cost estimates”.

Matt Roberts, executive director of the Conservation Council of Western Australia, earlier this month described Woodside’s proposal as “extremely concerning”.

“At the end of the day, we don’t need Browse gas. Western Australia has enough gas available to support us through the transition to a renewable future and regional demand for LNG is declining.

“The federal and state governments have enough evidence to reject this disastrous proposal and save Scott Reef, and we expect them to do just that.”

A spokesperson for the national EPA has told Guardian Australia the development was being assessed under the national Environment Protection and Biodiversity Conservation Act, and the “decision clock” had been paused while the agency waited on “further technical advice”.

A Woodside spokesperson said the proposed development “remains subject to a detailed, public and transparent assessment by state and Commonwealth regulators”.

Feedback had helped reduce “the infrastructure footprint around environmentally sensitive areas like Scott Reef – which will not be built on, drilled or mined”, they said.

“Browse is a natural gas and condensate development, not an oil development. References to a potential ‘oil spill’ from Browse are inaccurate.” The spokesperson added that the “longest credible duration of a potential well blowout, while still highly unlikely”, was around 12 hours.

#64. UK sanctions firms over Israeli West Bank settlements

The E1 area east of Jerusalem is a strategically important part of the occupied West Bank

The UK has sanctioned companies it said are involved in building settlements in the occupied West Bank, in the first package of measures since the government announced a tougher approach to Israeli actions in the territory.

The government said the sanctions against eight construction and property development firms would disrupt their flows of finance and ability to support building in the West Bank.

The construction of settlements, which are illegal under international law, has risen sharply in recent years. Settler attacks on Palestinians and their property have also surged.

Israeli Foreign Minister Gideon Saar called the sanctions a "hostile act targeting private, law-abiding companies".

Criticising the move on X, he wrote that Israel - as it has done in the past - will respond with "countermeasures", and that "decisions will be announced in due course".

"Israel will not bow to external pressure or change its policies as a result," Saar said, adding that they will "consider new ways to strengthen Jewish communities" in the West Bank, and "protect those involved in developing and building".

Israel previously condemned UK sanctions related to West Bank settlements, accusing the Labour government of "systematically acting against the State of Israel".

The Palestinian foreign ministry welcomed the sanctions and called on other countries to follow suit.

It comes two days before the deadline for bids for construction tenders in the highly sensitive E1 area east of Jerusalem.

The plans, which have been condemned internationally, would in effect divide the West Bank in two and cut it off from East Jerusalem.

UK Foreign Secretary Ed Miliband has said the E1 development would render a two-state solution "unviable" by "cutting through the heart of Palestine".

The sanctions also target Tzav 9, an Israeli activist movement which the UK government says has organised and carried out blockades to prevent aid entering Gaza.

Responding to the announcement, the group said: "Tzav 9 is a movement of abiding citizens from all the political spectrum, and we've always protested peacefully."

It comes after the UK expanded its human rights sanctions regime last month, enabling the government to sanction individuals and companies involved in violations of international humanitarian law anywhere in the world.

The government is also developing separate legislation to ban the import of goods from Israeli settlements and the provision of advertising services for settlement properties in the UK.

Miliband said: "Protecting the two-state solution is the North Star of this government's approach.

"That is why my message on E1 is clear: do not bid, do not build, and do not profit from the destruction of the two-state solution.

"The status quo in the West Bank and Gaza is unacceptable.

"We are committed to working productively with a new Israeli government and the Palestinian Authority to advance lasting peace and security for Israelis and Palestinians alike."

In September, the UK government announced that it would ban the import of goods from Israeli settlements, as well as acting against companies and individuals providing services for settlement expansion.

At the time, Miliband said the move marked the beginning of a "new approach" to the Israeli-Palestinian conflict, which "not only calls out injustice and suffering, but acts".

That announcement prompted a furious reaction from Israel, which said it would shut the British consulate in East Jerusalem in retaliation.

Last month, most diplomats left the consulate - which handled UK relations with Palestinians in Jerusalem as well as in the West Bank and Gaza - but the UK insisted that the building would not be closing and would operate as a "UK mission".

Israel has built about 160 settlements housing 700,000 Jews since it occupied the West Bank and East Jerusalem during the 1967 Middle East war. An estimated 3.3 million Palestinians live alongside them.

There has been a surge in attacks by settlers on Palestinians and their property in the West Bank since the start of the Gaza war, which was triggered by the Hamas-led attack on Israel on 7 October 2023.
