#61. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

At least 501 children have been killed in Ukraine since February 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale invasion, said Catherine Russell, executive director of the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF).

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.

Russell warned the real figure is “likely far higher” than the numbers verified by the UN agency.

Additionally, almost 1,000 Ukrainian children have been injured, “leaving them with wounds and scars - both visible and invisible - that could last for life,” she added.

The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Russia’s independent news outlet Fontanka is reporting that the cafe belongs to Prigozhin, on the basis of the Concord company’s ownership mentioned on the cafe’s receipt.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

CNN has not been able to independently verify Prigozhin’s claims and whether he is the legal owner of the cafe.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#60. Trump says US taking 'very seriously' any Iranian plot against Washington

Trump says US âlooking intoâ reported Iranian dirty bomb plot

President Donald Trump has said the US is taking "very seriously" the potential for any Iranian plot to attack Washington DC, after he was asked about a CNN report citing US intelligence assessments.

In the Philippines, where he attended the Asean Summit, Trump nodded when asked by a journalist about the report that Tehran was planning to detonate a so-called dirty bomb in the US capital and said: "We're looking into it."

CNN cited unnamed US intelligence officials suggesting Iran could target senior figures in Trump's administration with such an attack.

Iran called the claim "baseless." Concerns about dirty bombs grew after the 9/11 attacks in 2001, although no such attack has ever occurred.

Such a device would contain radioactive material - such as uranium - which would be scattered through the air when its conventional explosive detonated.

The BBC has not independently verified CNN's reporting.

During his comments to reporters in Manila, Trump was asked whether there was reason to believe an attack was imminent.

"I hope it's not," he said, "but we take it very seriously because they [the Iranians] happen to be crazy."

The US president was answering questions from reporters in the Philippines

In a statement before Trump's comments, White House spokesperson Lauren Bis wrote: "We remain vigilant monitoring any and all potential threats, but at this time there are no credible threats to the homeland."

Iranian foreign ministry spokesman Esmail Baghaei rebutted the CNN article and other US reports.

He posted on X: "Those who initiated the aggression against Iran are now seeking to lay the media groundwork for their sinister designs by manufacturing and disseminating baseless allegations."

Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf, Iran's parliamentary speaker and chief negotiator in indirect talks with the US, was similarly dismissive of the CNN report.

"Same false flags, same war mongering, new logos," he posted on X.

Mick Mulroy, a former CIA paramilitary officer and deputy assistant defence secretary, told the BBC: "A dirty bomb is not difficult to make or deploy."

"Although it is not a weapon of mass destruction, it can be a weapon of mass disruption," he added.

Imran Bayoumi, a geostrategy expert with the Atlantic Council in Washington DC and former policy adviser to the defence department, said that it remains "unclear how serious" would be the threat of a supposed Iranian dirty bomb.

"But Iran has planned and threatened to attack the US in the past," he said. "It is also unclear why this report is becoming public now."

It comes as intelligence officials and members of the Trump administration seek congressional renewal of a key national security law.

Section 702 of the Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Act (Fisa), which allows the US to âspy on foreign nationals, expires next March.

#58. Eurovision organisers criticise Malaysian removal of LGBT+ footage in first Asian contest

Broadcaster TV3 appears to cut footage of Thai drag performers and gay Philippine jury spokesperson

Eurovision’s organisers have said they are “surprised and disappointed” that the first Eurovision Asia was altered when it was broadcast in Malaysia, apparently removing LGBTQ+ imagery and people from the show.

The European Broadcasting Union (EBU), the alliance of public broadcasters that owns Eurovision, said it would write to Voxovation, the company producing the Asian contest under licence, and ask it to establish with the Malaysian broadcaster TV3 why it happened.

Footage circulating online of the contest, which was won by the Philippines’ Carmelle, showed the first of two cuts during the voting in Saturday’s final in Bangkok, when the Philippines’ spokesperson, Vice Ganda, awarded the Philippine jury’s points.

Vice Ganda is gay and identifies as non-binary. The comedian is among the biggest stars on Philippine television, has fronted the country’s most watched daytime show, and has campaigned for LGBTQ+ acceptance at home.

Announcing a national jury’s points is one of the most visible roles in a Eurovision final. Each country’s spokesperson appears in turn to reveal how their jury has ranked the other entries.

While the international feed showed Vice Ganda announcing that the Philippine jury had given its 12 points, the maximum, to the Malaysian entrant, Aina Abdul, TV3 viewers saw only the scoreboard.

Separate footage shows part of the show’s opening number showcasing Thai culture, in which drag performers appeared on street food carts, replaced with a wide shot taken from rehearsals.

Eurovision fans accused the channel of “censorship”. The Guardian has not been able to review TV3’s broadcast independently as its livestream is no longer available online.

Martin Green, the director of the Eurovision song contest, said: “We are surprised and disappointed to learn that the broadcast of Eurovision song contest Asia was altered in Malaysia.

“We take this matter seriously and will be writing to Voxovation, the producers of the show, to seek clarification and ask them to establish with Malaysian broadcaster TV3 why this happened.

“This appears to be isolated to the Malaysian broadcast of the show, and all other transmissions were not altered.”

Voxovation said in a statement that it was aware of the concerns raised and was “looking into the matter with the broadcaster to establish the circumstances … We are working with the Malaysian broadcaster to make sure this doesn’t happen again.”

Neither the EBU nor Voxovation said whether the contest’s participation agreement required broadcasters to carry the show unedited or what the consequences would be if one did not.

The Guardian has asked Media Prima, Malaysia’s largest media group and TV3’s owner, for comment.

This is not the first time the wider Eurovision song contest has faced allegations of censorship. In May 2018 the EBU terminated its partnership with the Chinese streaming service Mango TV after it dropped the Irish and Albanian entries and blurred a rainbow flag in the audience, saying such censorship was not in line with its “values of universality and inclusivity”.

Asked before Saturday’s final how the EBU’s commitment to its values squared with the laws in Malaysia and Bangladesh, the other competing country where same-sex relations are criminalised, the EBU’s head of communications, Dave Goodman, said the contest’s values were “non-negotiable”.

Malaysia has some of the harshest anti-LGBTQ+ laws in Asia, with oral and anal sex being punishable by up to 20 years in prison.

Rights groups have warned of a hardening climate. In November last year, authorities raided a men-only spa in Kuala Lumpur and detained more than 200 men, though no charges followed. And in 2022, police raided an LGBTQ+ Halloween party in the capital and arrested 20 people.

In 2023, a festival appearance by the British band the 1975, whose singer criticised the country’s homophobic laws and kissed a male bandmate on stage, led to the event being cancelled and prompted tighter entry rules for foreign performers.

TV3 has faced pressure over LGBTQ+ visibility before. In February 2025, it apologised after male guests arrived at a red carpet event in clothing resembling women’s dress.

#55. Rio Tinto agrees to pay US$250m to begin repairing ‘devastation’ left by Panguna mine

Community leader says fund gives chance to find solutions to ‘huge problems we have been living with since the days of the mine’s operation’

Anglo-Australian miner Rio Tinto has agreed to pay a quarter-of-a-billion dollars to begin the clean-up of the toxic Panguna mine site it abandoned more than three decades ago.

Panguna, once one of the world’s most profitable gold and copper mines, was shuttered in 1989 after violent protests over environmental damage and the inequitable division of the mine’s profits sparked a decade-long civil war on the Papua New Guinean island of Bougainville.

But the sprawling mine pit, once a mountain, remains a scar in the middle of the island, with vast pillars of rubble at risk of collapse, and polluted rivers running bright blue with toxins.

Rio Tinto was forced from the island and has never returned, but in recent years has been part of a detailed consultation process with landowners and government. It has now agreed to voluntarily contribute US$250m (A$370m) towards the remediation of the mine site.

Theonila Roka Matbob, a former cabinet minister in Bougainville’s autonomous government and Panguna community leader, said the fund was a vital contribution towards repairing the “devastation left by the Panguna mine”.

“We have a lot of work still ahead of us, but the establishment of this fund gives us the chance to find solutions to the huge problems we have been living with since the days of the mine’s operation.

“No amount of money will restore the damage caused to the environment in Panguna, but this sets a foundation for us to invest in addressing the environmental issues and invest in our children’s futures as well.”

Roka Matbob said the most fundamental remediation was to improve “accessibility” for the people living around Panguna – “accessibility to clean drinking water that is safe and secure … and infrastructure, in bridges that makes people’s access to trade, to school, to health[care], much easier. This is one critical area that immediately needs attention.”

The president of Bougainville, Ishmael Toroama, seeking to become the world’s newest head of state at the forefront of a long-running independence movement on the island, said the money would make a “meaningful difference” to villages and communities that had suffered from the impacts of the mine’s pollution.

“This work will deliver benefits not only for people today, but for future generations. Continued engagement with our communities will be essential to ensuring the work responds to local priorities and delivers lasting outcomes.”

The Rio commitment has emerged from the Panguna Mine Legacy Impact Assessment consultation process, which was initiated in response to a human rights complaint brought by the Australian-based Human Rights Law Centre, with Roka Matbob as the lead complainant.

Keren Adams, the HRLC’s legal director, said establishing the fund had taken years of work.

“It is a testament to the courage and persistence of community leaders, and the willingness of all parties to engage in difficult but constructive dialogue,” she said.

“The significant funding contributed by Rio Tinto creates a real opportunity to now address an environmental disaster that has remained unresolved for decades.”

Mark Davies, Rio Tinto’s chief safety and technical officer, said the establishment of the fund was an “important milestone … intended to support the transition from the assessment to practical remedy implementation”.

“Community engagement is an important next step to identify priorities and we remain committed to working with communities to shape the activities supported by the fund.”

The quarter-of-a-billion dollars will not be sufficient to entirely remediate the mine site, but it is being seen as a major step towards rehabilitation.

The funding commitment for Panguna comes at an acutely sensitive time for the restive Bougainville island.

The mine has become inextricably linked with the Bougainville’s political future: restarting operations at Panguna – believed to still hold at least $60bn in unrealised reserves – is seen by many, including the president, as the fundamental financial basis for the island’s independent future. Indian miner Lloyds has been granted a 25-year large-scale mining lease, but any future mining is years from starting.

Bougainville, a group of islands off Papua New Guinea to the north of Australia, has been variously controlled by Britain, Germany, Japan, Australia and PNG.

It declared independence in 1975 as the Republic of North Solomons, but was absorbed into the newly independent PNG weeks later.

Its close ethnic ties and proximity to the Solomons archipelago means it has always sat uneasily with Port Moresby’s rule.

That discontent came to a violent head in 1988 after a long-running dispute over the lucrative Panguna copper and gold mine.

The mine, carved from a mountain in central Bougainville, was critically important to PNG, at one point generating more than 45% of the country's national export revenue.

Multinational Rio Tinto and other international investors earned nearly US$2bn from the mine over 17 years of operation. But less than 1% of the mine’s profits went to Bougainvilleans, whose homes and lands had been destroyed by it.

Under the leadership of a former mine worker, Francis Ona, disaffected Bougainvilleans ran a sabotage campaign which forced the mine’s closure in 1989.

The PNG Government responded by sending in police, then soldiers, to restore law and order. Bougainville was blockaded and the island descended into a brutal civil war that ran for a decade, and saw as many as 20,000 people die.

PNG later hired international mercenaries to end the conflict (the notorious Sandline affair), before a peace agreement was signed in 2001.

In a 2019 referendum, 97.7% of Bougainvilleans voted in favour of independence. Many argue that reopening Panguna mine will be key to the island’s economic future as an independent nation.

Rio Tinto and other international investors earned nearly $2bn from Panguna over 17 years of operation from 1972. But less than 1% of the mine’s profits went to Bougainvilleans, whose homes and lands were destroyed by it.

Disaffected landowners ran a sabotage campaign that forced Panguna’s closure in 1989. The PNG government responded by sending in police, then soldiers, to restore law and order. Bougainville was blockaded and the island descended into a brutal civil war – known as “the crisis” – that ran for a decade. Between 15,000 and 20,000 people were killed, roughly 10% of the island’s population.

A recent environmental impact assessment found the abandoned mine remains an acute threat to people’s lives, including from landslides, levee collapses and floods. Untouched, the river delta will stay polluted for a century.

The future of Bougainville’s independence campaign is now before the PNG parliament, which will decide whether or not to recognise a UN-backed independence referendum held on the island in 2019, in which 97.7% of voters were in favour of Bougainville seceding and becoming an independent country.

The PNG parliament, which heard lengthy and impassioned debate over Bougainville last month, appears almost certain to vote against recognising the referendum result.

But Toroama, who has staked his presidency on achieving political independence, has said, regardless of the parliament’s decision, Bougainville will enter a period of “self-government” from September next year, before seceding, breaking away from PNG as a fully fledged independent nation, by 2030.

#54. Nvidia partner Megaspeed being investigated by US government over chip exports

The United States has confirmed it is investigating computing company Megaspeed, suspecting it of violating US export controls by helping to ship tech giant Nvidia's chips to China.

In a joint statement, the US Department of Justice and Department of Commerce said they had been investigating Singapore-based firm Megaspeed since 2024.

It comes after questions were raised about the possibility high-end AI chips may have ended up in China despite US export restrictions.

Concerns emerged in recent weeks after Nvidia founder Jensen Huang revealed he had an extramarital affair, allegedly with former Megaspeed executive Alice Huang. US authorities suspect her of spying for Beijing, which China denies.

Jensen Huang resigned from his role as chief executive last week following revelations he had two affairs.

Megaspeed is the biggest buyer of Nvidia chips in South East Asia and is suspected of helping divert advanced chips to China. The Singapore-based company said on Sunday it had launched an internal investigation into its business.

The justice and commerce departments said on Monday that "it is in the public interest to provide the facts and address inaccurate information" regarding the investigation.

Laying out a timeline, it said it began investigating Megaspeed in 2024 "for potential export control violations".

In May 2025, Department of Commerce investigators requested records from Nvidia "concerning direct or indirect sales to Megaspeed". In June 2025, Nvidia placed Megaspeed on its 'no sell' list.

Agents from the Bureau of Industry and Security, in conjunction with Singaporean law enforcement officials, then conducted an on-site check at Megaspeed that month and interviewed Alice Huang, who was chief executive at the time.

Alice Huang and Jensen Huang pictured together in Taiwan in June 2024

Following this enforcement action, the Department of Justice took the lead role in the criminal investigation of Megaspeed.

The Nvidia scandal has created tensions between the US Treasury and Commerce departments over shipments of high-end chips to Megaspeed.

On Friday, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent blamed the US Commerce Department, led by Howard Lutnick, for failing to properly oversee the export of the chips.

Lutnick has been called to testify before the Senate Banking Committee next month over his department's role in allowing Nvidia to export its advanced chips.

While Singapore does not prohibit the transfer of advanced AI chips to companies abroad, such moves can breach US export controls.

Huang has agreed to testify in Congress, including before the House Select Committee on China to discuss whether US national security was compromised by his affair.

The White House said on Monday it would further restrict China's access to AI chips from firms like Nvidia.

The reported affairs by Jensen Huang created a crisis for one of the world's most valuable firms - a major company in the AI industry and central to the US as it competes with China for dominance.

Trump downplays warnings of AI risks, citing rivalry with China

In the statement, the US government said it would "bring the full extent of the law against Megaspeed for any criminal conduct that may have occurred".

They added: "Sensitive technologies like semiconductors are among the most valuable and strategic assets of the US economy, and the Department of Justice and the Department of Commerce will remain vigilant in pursuing justice for those who want to compromise America's technological edge."

#48. Indian woman gang-raped while pregnant in 2002 'heartbroken' after two perpetrators bailed

The attack on Bilkis Bano and her family was one of the most horrific crimes during the Gujarat riots

The article has details that some readers may find disturbing

A pregnant Muslim woman who was gang-raped and saw 14 family members murdered during the 2002 Gujarat riots has spoken of her "pain, shock and disbelief" after two of her rapists were recently granted bail.

Bilkis Bano said in a statement that she heard about the top court order through press reports and vowed to fight back.

The freed men are from a group of 11 convicts - part of a Hindu mob - serving life terms for the attack on Bano.

All the men were also freed in 2022 after a Gujarat government order. That decision, and the celebrations as they left prison, caused global outrage. In 2024, the Supreme Court overturned the order.

In a short three-page order dated 28 October, the Supreme Court granted bail to Bipinchand Kanaiyalal Joshi and Pradip Ramanlal Modhiya and suspended their sentences while their petition challenging a 2017 Bombay High Court order is being heard.

The judges explained that the early release was because the two convicts "have been under incarceration for a period of more than 17 years" and "that one of the appellants has a severe handicap".

On Tuesday, Bano's lawyer Shobha Gupta told the BBC that the news of Joshi and Modhiya's release had left "Bilkis heartbroken".

"She has to restart her battle for justice. She had no information that this case was coming up in the Supreme Court. What is troubling for us is that every time something like this happens, we worry that it could affect her faith in judiciary."

The riots began after a fire on a passenger train in Godhra town killed 60 Hindu pilgrims

Gupta said it was difficult to understand why the convicts were being released because "it's not a routine criminal case, it's a case that shocks the conscience of the world, of entire humanity".

The attack on Bano and her family was one of the most horrific crimes during the Gujarat riots, which began after 60 Hindu pilgrims were killed in a fire on a passenger train in the town of Godhra.

Hindu mobs attacked Muslim neighbourhoods, blaming them for starting the fire. The riots continued for three days and more than 1,000 people died, most of them Muslims.

When rioters attacked Bano's village and started setting Muslim homes on fire, she and 16 relatives went on the run. She was 19, had a three-year-old daughter and was pregnant with her second child.

They were attacked on the morning of 3 March 2002 by a group of men "with swords and sticks", she told me in 2017. Her three-year-old daughter, she said, was snatched from her lap and killed.

How a Gujarat riot victim stood up to her rapists

India Supreme Court cancels release of 2002 riots rapists

India PM Modi's government okayed rapists' release

Her attackers were her neighbours, men she had seen almost daily while growing up. They tore off her clothes and several of them raped her, ignoring her pleas for mercy.

Her cousin, who had given birth two days earlier, was raped and murdered and her newborn baby was killed too.

Bano survived only because she lost consciousness and her attackers left, believing she was dead. Two boys - seven and four - were the only other survivors of the massacre.

The riots continued for three days and more than 1,000 people died, most of them Muslims

Her fight for justice has been long and nightmarish. The first arrests were made only in 2004 after the Supreme Court called in federal investigators and transferred her case to Mumbai, saying that courts in Gujarat could not deliver justice to her.

In her statement released on Monday night, Bano spoke of "fresh tears of pain, shock and disbelief".

"I still cannot believe that once again, for the second time, an order has been passed by the Supreme Court without calling me up and without hearing me," she said.

"For 24 long years, I have stood up to fight for justice... again and again. Yet today, I find myself losing the very justice I had fought so hard to win.... And I am scared of losing faith in justice, yet again."

Bano vowed to "fight this order... for my children, who do not deserve to live haunted by fears and scars for the rest of their lives".

"I do this for my three-year-old Saleha who deserved to live and whose memory demands justice. I do this for women everywhere. Because no woman, no survivor, no citizen should have to endure this eternal test of belonging and equality, and fight for justice for the rest of her life."

Prime Minister Narendra Modi was Gujarat chief minister at the time of the riots, and was criticised for not doing enough to prevent the carnage. He has always denied wrongdoing and has not apologised for the riots.

In 2013, a Supreme Court panel also said that there was insufficient evidence to prosecute him. But critics have continued to blame him for the riots happening on his watch.

#47. Hundreds arrested in anti-Trump protests in Philippines amid anger over Iran war and AI pact

Organisers say up to 10,000 people could join next round of demonstrations as US president arrives for Asean summit

Hundreds of people have been arrested in heated demonstrations against Donald Trump in Manila, as the US president arrived in the city for a leaders’ summit.

Organisers claimed as many as 10,000 people would be joining protests against Trump in the capital of the Philippines on Tuesday, where he is attending the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (Asean) summit. The crowds were expected to try to march to the summit site.

Authorities have arrested nearly 300 people in widespread protests held in the days leading up to the summit, with large parts of the capital closed off and tight security in place.

Some burned effigies of Trump and faced off with police officers armed with batons and shields. Slogans on placards called the American leader the world’s “No 1 terrorist” in protests held on Monday.

Demonstrators angry at the cost of living and energy shock caused by the Iran war, and the new US-led Pax Silica initiative on AI and critical minerals, tried to reach the summit at the Philippine International Convention Center on Monday, but were blocked by police.

Pax Silica has sparked tensions in the Philippines over plans to build a tech hub outside Manila that critics say will affect water and energy supplies, and displace members of the Ayta Indigenous community.

Large sections of central Manila were closed to the public as part of the security cordon and public holidays put in place for the Asean summit.

After departing from Fairbanks in Alaska, Trump emerged from Air Force One at Ninoy Aquino International airport at 5.54pm local time (0954 GMT), live video showed.

Trump will meet with the leaders of Australia, Japan and India on Tuesday night before heading to Shenzhen in China on Wednesday. There he will attend the Asia Pacific Economic Cooperation (Apec) summit and hold trilateral talks with China’s president, Xi Jinping, and Vladimir Putin of Russia.

The backlash against Trump follows the ousting of the boss of Nvidia, Jensen Huang, and allegations members of the US administration, including the commerce secretary, Howard Lutnick, knew about his affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Trump’s delegation was expected to include Elon Musk.

Protest leader Renato Reyes blamed the arrests in Manila on the heavy handed response of the government on the president, Ferdinand Marcos Jr, calling Monday the largest single day of police arrests since Marcos came to office in 2022.

“It is really crazy that the Philippine government is rolling out the red carpet for someone who has been so flatly rejected by the American people,” Reyes said. “Trump started a war that devastated the global economy and pushed oil prices in the Philippines to historic highs. It has caused untold suffering on the Filipino people.

“So, yes, Trump will have to pay for his crimes and he will have to be answerable, not just to the American people, but to the people of the world.”

The Philippine national police said some officers were injured during confrontations with demonstrators opposed to the summit, including when objects were allegedly thrown at officers.

Pax Silica will include a technology hub planned at New Clark City in Tarlac, about 100km from Manila.

Intended to bolster western stocks of AI technology and reduce “excessive dependencies” on single source supply chains, including from China, Pax Silica will provide chips and critical minerals to 26 participating signatory governments.

Amnesty International said demonstrations should not be forcibly or violently dispersed.

“Reports of arrests during the protests demand immediate scrutiny,” Amnesty’s director for the Philippines, Ritz Lee Santos III, said. “Authorities must explain the specific grounds for each arrest and any continued detention. Anyone detained solely for peacefully exercising their rights must be released immediately.”

President Donald Trump said Tuesday that US authorities take "very seriously" any possible threat to Washington, following reports Iran was planning to target officials, possibly with a radioactive bomb. Speaking with FRANCE 24's Monte Francis, Barbara Slavin, Distinguished Fellow at the Stimson Center in Washington, says that Iran may be "playing mind games with the United States as a way perhaps of deterring President Trump from unleashing a massive new attack on Iran".

#44. Trump in Asia ahead of talks with Russian and Chinese presidents

US President Donald Trump has arrived in the Philippines' capital on the first leg of a trip to Asia, which will end in a meeting in China with its President Xi Jinping and his Russian counterpart Vladimir Putin.

A red carpet was rolled out for Trump as he walked off Air Force One in Manila. A band played one of his favourite songs, Y.M.C.A., before he was whisked away by helicopter to a hotel.

Trump's first meeting at the Asean Summit was with US partners in the Quad - India's Narendra Modi, whom he described as a "very tough man", Japan's Sanae Takaichi, who "may be even tougher", and Australia's Anthony Albanese.

"Our countries are the four corners of the free, open, stable, prosperous Indo-Pacific," the US president said.

"We have outstanding relationships and co-ordinate closely... my phone is always open," Trump added.

Many countries in South East Asia have been hit hard by Trump's tariffs and his war on Iran - but their exports to the US rose 30% last year thanks to the AI boom for which the region manufactures many of the components.

The US is hoping to draw more countries here into its "Pax Silica" tech and AI ecosystem, and away from China.

But China's huge influence in this region cannot easily be challenged, and certainly not in the few hours Trump is in the Philippines.

Later on Tuesday, Trump signed a trade deal with Thailand alongside the country's Prime Minister Anutin Charnvirakul.

The US is Thailand's largest export market, accounting for 24% of its total shipments this year.

Trump then joined South East Asia leaders at a dinner, telling those gathered: "This is one of the world's most vibrant and productive regions filled with the tremendous potential."

After an overnight stay in Manila, the US president will fly to Shenzhen in China to attend a summit of Asia Pacific leaders and have nuclear talks with Xi Jinping and Vladimir Putin.

Asked if he would also be open to a one-on-one meeting with Putin, Trump said: "If it's available, I would".

#43. Hi-tech Shenzhen takes centre stage as Xi sends ‘Chinamaxxing’ message at Apec summit

Host city offers showcase of China’s rise into a confident and innovative power central to global economy

In a Shenzhen square on a balmy evening, locals and tourists watched the dozens of glinting towers that make up the city’s skyline ripple through the colour spectrum – cyan and violet, cyber green, China’s national scarlet and gold – repeatedly flashing the words “Apec 2026”.

The Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit is a dream showcase for one of China’s most advanced cities, and the country’s leader, Xi Jinping, is basking in its neon glow.

On the sidelines, a series of high-profile bilateral meetings and the announcement of a historic three-way summit with Donald Trump and Vladimir Putin have cemented Xi’s status as an agenda setter on the global stage.

The broader Apec summit, meanwhile, provides an opportunity for China to present itself as a steward of multilateralism against what it terms “unilateralism and protectionism” – that is to say, trade measures taken against Beijing by countries such as the US.

All this is set against the futuristic backdrop that is Shenzhen, a city that perhaps better than no other embodies China’s rise into a confident, technologically innovative power central to the global economy.

A photo display in the Apec media centre charts Shenzhen’s transformation from sparsely populated marshland, calling it “a miracle in the history of world development created by the Chinese people”.

“[Xi] wants people to go home Chinamaxxing,” said Prof Astrid Nordin, of King’s College London. “Shenzhen is itself part of the message. It’s a showcase of Chinese technological and economic achievements, and of China’s claim that it offers opportunities for prosperity in a world increasingly characterised by uncertainty and conflict.”

Like the light show, the summit is being carefully coordinated. The Financial Times cited people familiar with the matter as saying it would be one of the most tightly controlled in the organisation’s history, with leaders having to travel to a state guesthouse beside Shenzhen’s tiger safari park to meet Xi. The paper said diplomats had grumbled that the format “seems designed to reduce the free-flowing discussion characteristic of prior editions”.

Setting out its expectations for the summit, China’s foreign ministry has said it looks forward to “practical results in key areas such as trade, connectivity, digital economy, AI and so on”.

Already, Xi has announced trade wins with Canada’s Mark Carney, extending remission measures for certain Chinese steel and aluminium products by two years in exchange for tariff relief on Canadian pork, fish and seafood exports.

A meeting with Papua New ⁠Guinea’s leader, meanwhile, showed how Beijing’s geopolitical priorities have been absorbed and reflected by its allies, with James Marape recounting to Xi how he had closed Taiwan’s office in his country despite domestic challenges in doing so.

The most important meeting will be between Xi and Trump. The leaders of the world’s two superpowers are expected to tackle a broad range of issues including AI and trade. In a move that could strain the fragile tariff truce that the two leaders first agreed last year, the Trump administration said on Monday it would further restrict China’s access to advanced AI chips.

Christopher Adams, a senior adviser at the law firm Covington who was at the centre of trade negotiations with China for the Obama and first Trump administrations, said: “The trade truce may increasingly look like a ceasefire in a war where major combat operations remain on hold but skirmishes are escalating to the point where the line between peace and war is blurred.

“I don’t see pressure to announce a further extension of the trade truce in Shenzhen. If a significant extension is announced, that would be an upside surprise.”

China has not hesitated to leverage Apec’s multinational platform in service of its arguments. A commentary by the state news agency Xinhua on Tuesday said: “Amid deepening geopolitical rivalries and growing global divisions, [China’s hosting] sends a clear signal that great-power competition need not be a zero-sum game, and that one country’s development need not come at the expense of another.”

The chair of the Apec senior officials’ meeting, Chen Xu, said Beijing had made trade “openness” a key focus of its Apec year and looked forward to “achieving more practical outcomes on the trade front”.

The much anticipated trilateral summit with Trump and Putin, on the other hand, could prove largely symbolic. For Xi, “it provides the appearance of China shaping the world order as an equal to the United States,” said Dr Kevork Oskanian, an expert on Russia at the University of Exeter. “Agreement on substantial issues, which would, in any case, require extensive preparatory negotiations, is unlikely.”

For China’s ruling Communist party, the political pomp has another key purpose. Nordin said summits were “occasions for leaders to tell stories about the world, about their country’s place in it … and one of the most important audiences for those stories is at home.”

For the locals watching the skyscraper light show on Monday night, the symbolism was not lost. China’s export might had helped it become increasingly powerful on the world stage, said one local man, who did not give his name. “It’s so great that the summit will take place in Shenzhen,” he said. “I feel very proud.”
